Plan: AWSF — Agentic Workflow Software Factory

Implementation plan for the accepted fused architecture of awsf-architecture-proposal.md: MAW's deterministic task lifecycle fused with SSSF's observable phase pipeline, in TypeScript on Node ≥ 22.12, across two workspaces and three operating systems. Ten milestones · thirty-four bounded tasks (M9 is post-v1 work intake) · one worker per task per fresh session. This document is a planning artifact only — authoring it wrote no application code, initialized no repository, called no provider, created no worktree, and mutated none of the three source repositories.

In Plain Language

This section explains the whole plan without technical vocabulary. Everything after it says the same things precisely, for the people — and machines — building it.

What this is about

You use AI assistants to write software for you, and you have built two different systems to manage them. The first one is a strict supervisor: it has a rulebook that says exactly which step may follow which, it refuses every shortcut, it cannot start an assistant without writing that down first, and nothing joins your real project until you personally sign for it. The second one — inspired by another engineer's factory you studied on video — is a glass-walled workshop: you can watch every worker on one webpage while it happens, see what each step did, what it checked, how long it took, and what it cost, down to the cent.

Each one is missing exactly what the other has. The supervisor is blind: between "work started" and "work finished" you cannot see anything happening, and its safety equipment silently stops working the moment you move it off the one kind of computer it was built on — it would tell you "everything was cleaned up" without ever having looked. The glass workshop has no rulebook: nothing stops work from skipping steps, nobody has to sign before work is accepted, and — the deal-breaker — it can only talk to one of your three AI subscriptions. The one you value most, it cannot reach at all.

Why merge them, and why now

You are about to change machines: a new Mac laptop is coming and a Linux desktop is planned, alongside the Windows machine you use today. The supervisor's silent-failure problem stops being theoretical the day you copy it onto the Mac. And you are about to do bigger work, where "I can see what every assistant is doing and what it costs" stops being a luxury. So instead of patching either system, this plan builds one new factory — AWSF — that keeps the supervisor's rulebook and the workshop's glass walls, and fixes the known defects of both while the walls are open.

The factory, as a factory

Picture one building.

  • The rulebook is bolted to the wall. A fixed list says which step may follow which — ten stations, and every allowed move between them written down. Every forbidden move is also written down, with the exact reason the factory gives when it refuses. The rulebook is a machine, not a person: it cannot be argued out of anything.
  • Every worker clocks in before touching a tool. The factory physically cannot start an assistant without first writing its name in the logbook. If the note cannot be written, the worker never starts. If the building loses power halfway through hiring, no half-hired worker is left wandering — that is tested by deliberately cutting the power.
  • The glass wall shows everything. One webpage: a card per job, a colored dot-trail per worker, the money and time counters, and a step-by-step log. The page can look but never touch — there is deliberately no button on it that approves, cancels, retries, or changes anything. Looking is safe; touching happens only at your keyboard.
  • The logbook is the truth; the display is a copy. If the display's records are ever damaged or deleted, they are rebuilt from the logbook, identically. Losing the display loses nothing.
  • The quality bench measures the actual part. Your real tests run against the exact finished work, by machine, outside any assistant's control. Failures go back to the same worker at the same bench — cheap, because that worker still remembers the job — before anything more expensive happens.
  • You sign at a real desk. Accepting finished work into your real project happens only when a human types the command in a terminal. There is a waiting room with no clock: work waits for you forever, and no timer may ever throw it out.

What using it will actually feel like

You type one short command describing the job and which assistant should do it. The factory sets up the sealed copy, writes the worker into the logbook, and starts it. You open the webpage: a new card appears with the job's name, a dot-trail per worker growing in real time, a step log scrolling on the right, and three small counters — money, time, and volume of words processed. When you have three jobs running, you have three cards, and a glance tells you which is building, which is testing, and which is waiting for you.

When the work passes its tests — and, for risky jobs, after the other company's assistant has written its opinion — the card turns into a summary: what changed, what was checked, what the second opinion said, and the exact command to type if you accept it. You type that command in a terminal; the factory double-checks everything one last time and folds the work into your real project. If you do nothing, it waits. If you decline, the work is kept exactly where it is. Nothing in this factory ever deletes anything automatically.

What each milestone is for, and what you can do when it finishes

MilestoneIn plain termsWhat you can do once it is finished
M0 — this planWrite the whole factory down before building any of it, and put every open decision in front of you with a recommendation.Read it, disagree in the margins, and approve the architecture before a line of code exists.
M1 — the rulebookWrite the ten stations and every allowed and forbidden move, and prove the refusals in code before anything can start a worker.Watch the factory correctly refuse every one of the seventy-five forbidden moves, with the right reason for each.
M2 — the logbookBuild the permanent record and the display's copy of it, and prove that pulling the plug mid-sentence never corrupts either.Delete the display's database on purpose, rebuild it with one command, and see identical screens.
M3 — the clock-in machine ⚠Build the part that starts and stops workers. This is the most dangerous milestone, because its failures are silent — a ghost worker no record knows about. A pretend assistant stands in for real ones, so it costs nothing.Kill the factory at the worst possible moment, on purpose, and verify no ghost worker survives — on every kind of computer you own.
M4 — the real assistantsConnect Claude and GPT for real, each through its own door. Google's door gets a doorbell and a "not verified yet" sign until someone confirms its radio speaks a readable language.Run one real, bounded hello-world through each company and see its true name, cost label, and word-count on the record.
M5 — the assembly lineBuild the six job types, the sealed copies, the quality bench, the what-may-this-worker-touch rules, and the cheap send-it-back loop.Run a whole pretend job start-to-finish: watch a deliberate mistake get caught by the bench and fixed by the same worker, for free.
M6 — your deskBuild the commands you type: start, watch, accept, cancel, retry, diagnose, rebuild. Make accepting work impossible anywhere except a terminal with you at it.Drive an entire pretend job from your keyboard alone, sign it, and see it land — with zero spend.
M7 — the glass wallBuild the webpage: the job cards, the dot-trails, the step log, the money labels that never lie, and the one and only button (file a card away).Watch a live run on the webpage while it is still running — and confirm the page has no lever that could change anything.
M8 — opening dayProve it on your real machines with two real jobs — one easy, one genuinely risky with a second opinion — and fill in the it-works-here checklist on each computer, from that computer.Trust it, and know from counted evidence what a real job costs you.
M9 — the work queue (after opening day)Give the factory an in-tray: turn each piece of work into its own small card with a title, what "done" means, and what it waits on — and let an assistant tidy a vague card into a clear one. Add a board that shows the cards.Look at one page and see every job you have not started yet, which ones are ready to run, and what you expect them to cost — then start one without retyping it.

Using both AI companies on purpose — and who picks

You pay for two working subscriptions — Claude, and GPT through the Pi tool — and each has its own monthly allowance. Either company's assistant can do the building work, and you choose which one, per job, when you start it. The program never chooses for you, and one rule sounds pedantic but is load-bearing: the factory is forbidden from picking an assistant because the other one is running low. "I picked GPT for this job" is a judgement about who fits the work; "the program picked GPT because Claude was at 80%" is a budget decision wearing a judgement's clothes, and a month later you cannot tell which jobs got the right worker. If the assistant you chose is unavailable, the job stops and tells you — it never quietly swaps.

The second opinion on risky work always comes from the other company — two assistants from the same company tend to make the same mistakes, so an opinion from the same house is worth much less. A pleasant side effect: sending building work to one company automatically sends reviewing work to the other, so your two allowances balance themselves without any scheduler.

Your third subscription — Google's — gets a reserved parking space in the design. But its door stays closed and marked "not verified" until someone actually starts it once and reads what comes out. This factory does not build around equipment nobody has switched on.

How you will know it worked

  • The pull-the-plug test. Kill the factory at the exact worst moment, between "worker hired" and "worker started" — no ghost process survives. On every machine.
  • The delete-the-display test. Delete the webpage's database mid-week, rebuild it with one command, and every screen comes back identical. The old file is kept, not thrown away.
  • The waiting room has no clock. Work waiting for your signature waits forever. No timeout may ever move it.
  • The money labels never lie. A Claude job says "— subscription", never "$0.00". An estimate says it is an estimate. A total mixing known and unknown costs says "partial".
  • The webpage cannot act. A test literally reads the list of things the webpage's server can do and fails if an accept, cancel, retry, or settings-change action ever appears on it.
  • Nothing is ever deleted automatically. Not work, not records, not sealed copies, not even the replaced database file.

What is deliberately not being built

No AI manager, AI inspector, or AI repairman — the rulebook, the bench, and a diagnosis command are machines, not conversations. No automatic restarts. No background watchers. No paperwork pipelines that generate reports about reports. No way for the webpage to change anything. No sending your work anywhere — the factory cannot even push to the internet; that ability simply is not built. These are not postponed features: removing that machinery is the point, because most of it existed to catch problems the machinery itself created.

The one-paragraph version
Merge your two systems into one factory: the strict supervisor's rulebook — ten stations, every move written down, workers who cannot start without clocking in, and your signature required at a real keyboard before anything joins your project — behind the glass wall of the workshop you admired, where one webpage shows every worker, every check, and every honestly-labelled cent, live. Let either AI company build, chosen by you and never by whoever has allowance left; let the other company give the second opinion on risky work. Build it in nine milestones — rulebook first, permanent records second, the dangerous start/stop machinery third and most carefully, real assistants only after pretend ones prove the plumbing, and the webpage near the end because it only watches. Prove every safety property by deliberately breaking things: pull the plug, delete the display, kill workers mid-job. Then run two real jobs, count what they cost, and only claim "it works on the Mac" after it has run on the Mac.

Purpose

Build AWSF — a single-operator agentic software factory that fuses the two working systems: my-agentic-workflow's deterministic control plane (guarded state machine, PID-before-spawn barrier, human-TTY-only landing) and super-simple-software-factory's observable execution pipeline (typed phases, runtime-validated envelopes, postcondition gates with same-session corrections, live SQLite-backed dashboard). One sentence governs the fusion: the state machine governs the task; the phase engine governs the sojourn inside the executing states (awsf-architecture-proposal.md §7.1).

The target is TypeScript on Node ≥ 22.12, two npm workspaces (core/ with no build step under native type-stripping, dashboard/ as Vue 3 + Vite), a pinned dependency allowlist enforced by a meta-test, and roughly 11,000–15,000 production lines plus 10,000–15,000 test lines. This plan decomposes the accepted architecture into ten milestones and thirty-four bounded tasks (M0–M8 are v1; M9 is post-v1 work intake), each sized for one worker in one fresh session, each with explicit entry conditions, outputs, proofs, and stopping criteria.

What this plan is, and is not
This is a planning artifact only. Its authoring performed no live provider call, wrote no AWSF production code, initialized no repository, created no worktree, pushed nothing, deleted nothing, and read the three source repositories strictly read-only. Every invariant stated below that came from a source repository carries a file:line citation so a downstream builder verifies rather than trusts.

Problem

Both parent systems work, and neither is sufficient. The defects below were verified read-only in the actual repositories at the cited lines on 2026-08-04; they are the concrete reasons a fusion is being built rather than either system extended in place.

MAW — a control plane that is blind and non-portable

  1. No observability layer. Between RUNNING and GATING the operator sees a one-line heartbeat at most. There is no per-phase trace, no event log, no cost/usage surface, no live view of tool activity. MAW has nothing to project into — no equivalent of SSSF's seven-table trace exists anywhere in src/.
  2. No typed inter-phase contracts. Worker output is a RESULT line parsed by prefix from stdout (run.mjs:122); there is no schema, no validation, no retained invalid payload, no typed handoff between phases.
  3. Process supervision is Linux-only and fails silently elsewhere. processGroupMembers() returns [] when process.platform !== 'linux' (run.mjs:19-24), so on macOS every cancellation reports "no survivors" without having looked. MAW's own README calls this out as the single most important thing to test on the destination machine (README.md:72-75) — the defect class this whole redesign exists to eliminate.
  4. Prompts ride process arguments. claudeCommand() appends the prompt as the final argv element (providers/claude-code.mjs:6) — visible to every process lister, subject to OS argv length limits, and a leak channel. Prompts must travel on stdin.
  5. No durable landing state. Landing goes AWAITING_OWNER → LANDED in one step (state.mjs:127); a crash mid-fast-forward leaves a world that is neither. Recovery is a guess, and this system forbids guessing.
  6. Acceptance records can be written into the canonical checkout after landing — a post-landing mutation of the thing gates already signed off on.

SSSF — an observable pipeline with no spine, locked to one provider

  1. Cannot reach Claude Pro at all. Config validation rejects any agent whose coding_agent != "pi" (agents.py:61-63), and Claude Pro cannot be used through Pi. Santiago's most valued subscription is structurally unreachable; the default model is google/gemini-3.6-flash (sssf.config.yaml:5).
  2. No state machine and no spawn barrier. Phases run in whatever order a script calls them; nothing refuses an illegal sequence, nothing registers a PID before a process exists, and there is no human gate anywhere — work lands on the active branch with no worktree isolation.
  3. diff_matches_claims checks one direction only. It verifies every claimed file exists (gates.py:61-68) and never notices a file the agent changed and did not report. The gate's own name promises set equality; it delivers a subset check.
  4. Permissions are enforced only after mutation. The change-set fingerprint comparison (permissions.py:50-75) is excellent — it caught a real agent running git checkout to revert the quality check judging it (permissions.py:5-8) — but it is the only layer; there is no worktree, no OS sandbox, and the write has already happened by the time it is seen.
  5. The trace schema has no version marker and no indexes. Additive ALTER migrations with no user_version (tracer.py:92-99) force the read side to probe PRAGMA table_info per table (db.ts:97-120); with no indexes, every 500 ms dashboard poll is a full scan.
  6. tracer.event() has no error boundary. A locked or corrupt SQLite file aborts the running phase — observability can kill the work it observes, the exact inversion of "the journal is truth, the DB is a copy".

Solution

One system, six pillars, four load-bearing fusion decisions — all settled in awsf-architecture-proposal.md and treated as immutable by this plan.

The six pillars

#PillarWhat it makes impossible
1Host owns the loop. No model selects phases, changes risk tier, advances lifecycle state, lands code, or repairs orchestration state.Model verdicts moving work forward; the pilot's rubber-stamped closures.
2Journal is truth; SQLite is a rebuildable projection. Append-only fsynced journal.jsonl + atomically replaced status.json; awsf db rebuild reconstructs the DB byte-identically.The dashboard ever becoming a liability; observability killing the work it observes (SSSF's tracer abort).
3No process before registration. The launcher blocks until journal, status, and projection acknowledge its identity; registration failure kills the tree and returns the reservation.Hidden subprocesses; a pane that looks idle while a provider is alive.
4Typed envelopes and earned success. Every phase starts FAILED; every agent output is schema-validated at runtime; gates record what they verified whether or not they passed."Said done, wrote nothing"; contracts drifting across three handwritten copies.
5Host owns Git; humans own landing. Agents edit, never commit/merge/push/rebase/reset/clean. Landing is local fast-forward only, from an interactive TTY, through a persisted LANDING state. No push path exists.Any automated route into the canonical repository; ambiguous crash-mid-landing recovery.
6Portability is a contract, not a hope. One ProcessSupervisor port, three platform implementations, one contract suite; a port that cannot enumerate survivors errors, never returns [].MAW's silent [] on Darwin; platform claims made from the wrong machine.

The four fusion decisions

  1. Two-tier correction economics with reservations. Intra-phase corrections re-prompt the same provider session — context intact, cheap, and they do not consume tier calls (SSSF's insight, agents.py:107-110). Only an exhausted phase escalates to a state transition, which is counted, rationed, and evidence-gated (MAW's insight, state.mjs:118). Calls are reserved before launch, converted to spent on GO, released if registration fails; spend carries across attempts.
  2. Spawning centralized in a host TransportBroker. Adapters build pure ProcessSpec descriptors and parse streams; they may not import node:child_process or call fetch — enforced by an architecture test. An adapter that can spawn can bypass the barrier, so no adapter can spawn.
  3. LANDING is a persisted state. A crash mid-fast-forward recovers unambiguously or blocks; it never guesses.
  4. One language, one runtime, two workspaces. The highest-severity defect in the current stack is portability, and portability is a process-supervision problem solved once, in one place, contract-tested per machine.

Ownership and Trust Boundaries

Four ownership classes, kept strictly apart — MAW's boundary rule, preserved verbatim because it is the one part of the old stack that never failed.

ClassLives atOwnerLifecycle
Durable intentawsf.config.yaml, prompts/, specs/, AGENTS.md — committed in the AWSF repoOwner, via reviewed commitsVersioned forever; no machine paths, no credentials, no PIDs, no quota fields
Durable sourceThe canonical target repository being worked onOwner; host performs only FF landingMutated exclusively by human-approved fast-forward; no push path exists
Disposable executionManaged worktrees under the per-machine worktree rootHost creates; nothing deletes automaticallyOne worktree per attempt; listed by awsf gc, removed only by the human
Machine-local runtime<state-root>/projects/<project>/tasks/<task>/<attempt>/ via platform-paths.tsHostJournals append-only; attempts sealed on terminal states; private/continuity.json mode 0600

Trust boundaries

  • Host code is trusted. It owns transitions, spawning, Git, gates, and landing.
  • Model output is untrusted data. Envelopes are parsed, size-capped (256 KiB), schema-validated, path-normalized and containment-checked; invalid ones are retained with violations, never repaired silently.
  • Provider streams are untrusted input. Line-framed with per-instance decoders, sequence-numbered by the host; provider timestamps advisory; provider tool IDs never reach a normalized event (the host mints t1, t2, …); unrepresentable model identities fail closed (E_MODEL_UNRESOLVED).
  • The dashboard is a read-only consumer on a readonly SQLite connection, loopback-only, with exactly one write (archive flag). Its absence — even its corruption — may never affect a task beyond the degraded-observability hold at GATING.
  • The human is the only landing authority. AWAITING_OWNER → LANDING demands an interactive TTY; every other route to LANDED throws HumanGateBypass.
  • Credentials cross no boundary. Env is an allowlist (PATH, HOME, TMPDIR + injected locale pins); an allowlisted key holding credential-shaped bytes is a hard E_REDACTION error (launch.ts:44-62); journals, SQLite, API responses, and fixtures are credential-free by meta-test.

The Lifecycle Contract

Ten states, twenty-five legal edges, seventy-five rejected ordered pairs, one ordered rejection contract. The tables in this section are normative — the test suites of M1 encode them literally, and the implementation is written afterwards to satisfy the tests, never the reverse. This merges MAW's GATING/REVIEWING (host-owned gating and inverted-provider review encoded structurally) with the proposal's persisted LANDING (crash-recoverable fast-forward). PLANNING/BUILDING/DOCUMENTING are deliberately not states — they are phases, and promoting them would make the transition matrix workflow-dependent and destroy its testability.

States

StateMeaningProvider may run
DRAFTIntent, tier, and route recorded. No worktree.no
PREPAREDWorktree materialized, configured ignored dependency seeds copied into it, base SHA pinned, roster resolved, adapter/sandbox/observability preflight passed.no
RUNNINGPhase engine active; each ordinary agent phase runs as its own registered configured provider process under task-edge or compiled-phase authorization.yes
GATINGHost runs postcondition gates on the committed candidate SHA, outside model control.no
REVIEWINGT2 only. Cross-provider reviewer, diff-scoped, advisory. Diff-scoped is a host-enforced property, not an aspiration: the reviewer receives a host-composed awsf.review-context/v1 envelope carrying the owner's request, the acceptance criteria, the exact base and candidate SHAs, the Git-observed changed-file list, a bounded whole-hunk diff with its full-diff digest, and the complete TestOutput; review_evidence_present refuses the phase whose prompt did not carry it.yes
AWAITING_OWNEREverything machine-checkable has passed. Waiting on a human. No timeout.no
LANDINGHuman approved the exact candidate; local fast-forward in progress. Persisted, so a crash here recovers unambiguously.no
LANDEDCanonical HEAD equals the candidate. Terminal.no
BLOCKEDHalted with a reason code. Terminal for this attempt; awsf retry mints attempt n+1 at DRAFT.no
CANCELLEDHuman stopped it. Tree terminated, survivors reported. Terminal.no

The twenty-five legal transitions

#From → ToActorTriggerGuard (evidence that must be present)Spawn
L1DRAFT → PREPAREDhostawsf startworktree created, every configured repository-relative ignored seed safely copied, base SHA pinned, config validates, adapter/sandbox/observability preflight passes—
L2DRAFT → BLOCKEDhostpreflight failurereason.code ∈ BLOCKER_CODES—
L3DRAFT → CANCELLEDhumanawsf cancelexplicit, interactive—
L4PREPARED → RUNNINGhostworkflow startworkflow compiled; tier ceiling has headroom; call reserved✦
L5PREPARED → BLOCKEDhostfaultblocker code—
L6PREPARED → CANCELLEDhumanawsf cancelexplicit—
L7RUNNING → GATINGhostphases completeall required phases terminal-success; host commit created; 40-hex candidate SHA ≠ base—
L8RUNNING → BLOCKEDhostfaultcrash / silence / quota / phase abort / permission breach / budget exhaustion—
L9RUNNING → CANCELLEDhumanawsf canceltree terminated, survivors reported—
L10GATING → RUNNINGhost or ownergate failedcorrection budget remains; reason.command and reason.output both present; actor's tranche unspent✦
L11GATING → REVIEWINGhostgates pass, T2gates pass and tier ≥ 2✦
L12GATING → AWAITING_OWNERhostgates pass, T0/T1gates pass and tier < 2—
L13GATING → BLOCKEDhostbudget spentgates failed, correction budget exhausted—
L14GATING → CANCELLEDhumanawsf cancelexplicit—
L15REVIEWING → AWAITING_OWNERhostreview recordedverdict recorded and internally consistent—
L16REVIEWING → RUNNINGownerowner accepts a finding≥ 1 finding of severity ≥ medium with both file and detail; owner tranche unspent; stale gates invalidated✦
L17REVIEWING → BLOCKEDhostreview unreachable, malformed, or unevidencedone transport retry or a host-determined review-malformed / review-evidence-invalid failure; code ∈ {review-unavailable, review-malformed, review-evidence-invalid}. A verdict_consistent failure is not among them — an inconsistent verdict is content—
L18REVIEWING → CANCELLEDhumanawsf cancelexplicit—
L19AWAITING_OWNER → RUNNINGhumanrework requestconcrete named defect or rework request; budget remains; gates and review invalidated✦
L20AWAITING_OWNER → LANDINGhumanawsf landinteractive TTY; exact SHA and summary displayed and confirmed; gates pass; required review present; journey and protected approvals valid; FF preflight passes—
L21AWAITING_OWNER → BLOCKEDhostrecord fault onlyreason.source === 'record', code ∈ {record-corrupt, unknown-state, ambiguous-pid, unreadable-worktree}—
L22AWAITING_OWNER → CANCELLEDhumanexplicit rejectionexplicit—
L23LANDING → LANDEDhostFF confirmedcanonical HEAD equals candidate; checkout clean—
L24LANDING → BLOCKEDhostFF impossiblenon-FF, dirty canonical tree, Git failure, ambiguous crash recovery—
L25AWAITING_OWNER → REVIEWINGowner or humanawsf review — the recorded review was not evidenceinteractive TTY; reason.source === 'human'; non-blank invalidation reason; tier ≥ 2; 40-hex candidate; gates pass and every configured gate has a passing row bound to that candidate; the superseded review is recorded, names this tree, and is invalidated; reviewEvidenceDefect ∈ {evidence-gate-absent, evidence-gate-failed}; host-observed candidateUnchanged; gatesInvalidated absent; owner re-entry allowance unspent✦
Task-edge spawn sites — six edges, and only six
L4, L10, L11, L16, L19, L25 are the complete set of lifecycle edges that may authorize a launch. Any other task-state pair attempting one raises IllegalSpawnSite. The first workflow provider remains L4 after PREPARED → RUNNING is durably persisted; mandatory review remains L11, the first review/owner rework launch remains L16/L19, and a replacement review is L25. Each consumes a held reservation, converted to spent on GO, released if registration fails.

A multi-agent compiled workflow does not invent lifecycle edges for its later phases. A later ordinary agent phase uses a distinct discriminated phase registration while the durable task remains RUNNING. A trusted host verifier must prove the task session, workflow id, phase id and one-based ordinal, compiled agent kind, explicit configured adapter and role, and one held call reservation before the broker creates a child. code/engineer, REVIEWING, mandatory review, and first owner-rework launches cannot use it. RUNNING → RUNNING remains AlreadyInState; the matrix, rejection order, correction edges, and task-edge sites were unchanged by that split. (They moved once afterwards, and only once: L25.)

L25 preserves a green rather than invalidating one, and that is why it needs its own edge. The contract already had "the owner rejects the review and re-enters" (L16); it lacked "the owner rejects the review and re-buys the review". The asymmetry existed because the contract assumed a review's content could be wrong but its evidence could not be missing. L16 and L19 must invalidate the stale gates because they change the tree; L25 must not speak to invalidation at all, because the tree does not change and the old green vouches for exactly the tree it saw. Its eligibility is host-determined, not discretionary: reviewEvidenceDefect is read off the recorded review phase's gate rows and has exactly two members, so a review carrying a passing review_evidence_present row is not replaceable at all — its verdict stands, whatever it says, and the owner's remedies are L16, L19, L20 or L22. The owner's --reason string is a record, never a key. If disliking a verdict could buy a cold second opinion, the mandatory opposite-provider review would be a one-shot lottery, and "you may only do it once" is not an answer to that, because once is enough.

L21 is deliberately narrow. AWAITING_OWNER has no timeout, so no clock may produce this edge — only a corrupt record or an unreadable worktree, exactly as MAW enforces today (state.mjs:55-60). A task waiting on the owner waits forever; that is the point.

L16 requires a concrete defect. A style note is not a defect: if it authorized re-work, "the reviewer had opinions" would be indistinguishable from "the code is wrong" (state.mjs:89-95). This is why ReviewFinding carries severity — SSSF's review envelope has no such field, which is why SSSF cannot express the rule.

The seventy-five illegal pairs — by class, with the error each throws

10 × 10 = 100 ordered pairs; 25 legal; 75 illegal, each an explicit M1 test asserting both that it throws and which error it throws. The classes partition cleanly because the rejection order (below) fires the first applicable error:

ClassPairsError thrownMembers
Out of a terminal state27TerminalAttemptevery pair from BLOCKED, LANDED, CANCELLED to any of the other nine states (3 × 9). awsf retry is not a transition — it mints attempt n+1 at DRAFT.
Self-transition10AlreadyInStateall ten X → X pairs, including RUNNING → RUNNING.
Human gate bypass6HumanGateBypass{DRAFT, PREPARED, RUNNING, GATING, REVIEWING, AWAITING_OWNER} → LANDED. The error names the actual violation — an attempt to land without the human gate — rather than the generic pair complaint. (Terminal-state sources already fall under TerminalAttempt, which fires earlier.)
Everything else32IllegalTransitionevery skip-ahead (DRAFT → RUNNING, PREPARED → GATING, GATING → LANDING…), every backward jump not on the correction list (GATING → PREPARED, REVIEWING → GATING…), RUNNING → REVIEWING (review reads a committed diff, so gating is mandatory first), RUNNING → AWAITING_OWNER, DRAFT → LANDING, LANDING → RUNNING, and the rest of the enumeration the M1 suite generates programmatically and asserts equals exactly 32. This cell was 33 until L25. AWAITING_OWNER → REVIEWING left this class when the owner gained the right to re-buy a review that carried no evidence; the first three classes' arithmetic ("3 × 9", "all ten X → X pairs") is untouched, so the amendment is a single cell.

27 + 10 + 6 + 32 = 75. The M1 suite generates the full 100-pair matrix, asserts exactly 25 accept, and asserts the class → error mapping above pair by pair — enumerating the legal set literally, never deriving it from the implementation.

The ordered rejection contract — and why the order is the contract

A rejection must name the real defect, or fixing what it named would let an illegitimate transition through (MAW proved this discipline at state.mjs:8-24; AWSF extends its 8 steps to 11). Evaluated strictly in order:

#ErrorFires whenWhy it outranks everything below it
1NonDeterministicEvidencereason.source not on the closed allowlist (process state, exit codes, git state, gate results, record faults, explicit human input)If conversational evidence could reach any later check, a well-formed lie would pass the checks below. Evidence legitimacy precedes everything.
2TerminalAttemptfrom-state is BLOCKED / LANDED / CANCELLEDA sealed attempt must say "this attempt is over", not "that pair is unknown" — the fix is awsf retry, not a different pair.
3AlreadyInStateself-transitionBefore pair legality: "you are already there" is more precise than "illegal pair", and a retry loop that self-transitions must hear the former.
4HumanGateBypasstarget is LANDED from anything but LANDINGNaming the violated invariant (the human gate) beats naming the violated pair; an automation bug that tries to land must be identified as exactly that.
5IllegalTransitionthe pair is not one of the 25Only now is "unknown pair" the true, most-specific complaint.
6CorrectionAllowanceExhausted (global)the whole correction budget is goneOnce the global budget is spent no actor can restore it, so actor identity (below) is irrelevant — reporting ActorNotPermitted first would invite switching actors, which cannot help.
7ActorNotPermittedpair is legal, this actor may not make itActor legitimacy precedes actor-specific budgets: "you may never do this" beats "you ran out".
8InteractiveOwnerRequireda human edge attempted without a TTYThe right actor in the wrong medium — piped stdin, an API — must hear "come to a terminal", not a budget or evidence complaint.
9CorrectionAllowanceExhausted (tranche)this actor's own tranche is spentActor-specific budget after actor legitimacy; the other actor's tranche may remain.
10InsufficientEvidencepair, actor, budget fine; evidence is notOnly a fully legitimate request earns a critique of its evidence — otherwise fabricating evidence would look like the fix.
11CallCeilingExceededa spawn-site edge at the per-tier or lifetime ceilingLast, because it is the only rejection whose remedy is economic (a new attempt or an owner decision), and it must not mask a defect the checks above would have named.

Call ceilings, reservations, and correction allowances

TierMeaningCall ceilingRequired controls
T0Read-only analysis or planning1No mutation
T1Localized, reversible mutation3Worktree, host commit, gates, human landing
T2Security, process control, persistent data, cross-component, ambiguous or weakly-tested work5T1 plus opposite-provider review and end-user journey
  • protected is orthogonal to tier: credential access, external mutation, deletion, migration, release/cutover, credit-billed execution.
  • Reservations: reserve before launch so concurrent paths cannot exceed the ceiling; convert to spent when the launcher receives GO; release if registration fails and the provider never ran. Spend carries across attempts of the same task. Retry clears attempt-local reservations/corrections but records the currently loaded redacted effective-config snapshot and correction allowance, never the prior attempt's stale gate configuration. A composite (fusion) adapter declares its full cost in advance — two workers plus a fuser reserve three calls. A workflow whose minimum call count cannot fit the selected tier is rejected before execution.
  • Correction allowances — two allowances, not one. correction_allowance: {auto: 1, owner: 1} bounds the intra-phase correction (ladder rungs 2–3): one automatic and at most one owner-authorized, per phase, refreshed when a new phase begins. Separately, one owner re-entry (rungs 4–5: an owner-actor L10, L16, L19, L25) is available per attempt, drawn from its own counter (owner_reentries) and reset only by awsf retry. The two are coupled in value, not in scope: the configured owner number bounds both, so an attempt buys one owner-authorized re-entry of either kind — after a replacement review, an owner rework is refused as exhausted, and vice versa. Keeping them in one counter meant a phase beginning after an owner re-entry silently refunded it. Intra-phase corrections cost tokens, not calls; GATING → RUNNING costs a call. The dashboard must show both meters.
  • Routing may never read quota. The owner picks the provider; the runner never picks it because a subscription was running low. Quota exhaustion blocks with the reset time — it is never a retry and never a substitution.

The phase submachine

The Phase Contract

One primitive, ported from SSSF and typed for AWSF:

type PhaseKind = "agent" | "code" | "engineer";

interface PhaseDefinition<TOutput> {
  id: string;
  kind: PhaseKind;
  owner: string;                     // agent name, "git", or the engineer
  description: string;               // EARNED, not restated — see below
  outputSchema: TSchema;             // TypeBox: runtime schema + static type + JSON Schema
  maxCorrections: number;
  gates: readonly GateDefinition[];
  execute(ctx: PhaseContext): Promise<TOutput>;
}
  • Three kinds. agent phases each spawn a separate configured provider process through the broker and must emit an envelope. The first is task-edge-authorized (normally L4); later ordinary phases in the same durable RUNNING sojourn use host-verified compiled-phase authorization. code phases are host-run subprocesses (tests, typecheck) whose envelope the host generates. engineer phases record human input (the request, an approval) — neither may use agent-phase authorization.
  • The earned-description rule ports verbatim from data_types.py:31-53 and fires at workflow compilation, not runtime: a description that is blank or a normalized restatement of the phase name is rejected, because that sentence is the only intent the trace, the console, and the phase block ever show. commit_plan: "Commit the plan" is a construction-time error.
  • Success must be earned. Every phase is constructed FAILED-equivalent (starts QUEUED, and any abnormal exit records fail); only a clean exit through validation flips it — SSSF's discipline at data_types.py:63 and runner.py:88-105, kept structurally.

The six initial workflows — six, not twelve

WorkflowPhasesTier
scoutrequest → scoutT0
planrequest → plannerT0
buildrequest → builder → testsT1
plan-build-testrequest → planner → builder → testsT1
build-reviewrequest → builder → tests → review context → reviewerT2
simple-sdlcplanner → builder → tests → documenter → final tests → review context → reviewerT2
intake — M9, post-v1request → intakeT0

A seventh workflow is added only after a repeated real use case appears — never speculatively. intake is that seventh, and it was earned, not assumed: authoring this plan's own thirty task tickets by hand is the repeated real use case, and the demand signal is countable rather than imagined. It ships in M9, after the pilots — never in v1, where the six stay six.

The full agent-call pipeline

authorize launch                              // L4 first; later ordinary agent = compiled-phase proof
  → verify durable RUNNING + exact compiled identity + explicit adapter/role + held reservation
  → broker registration barrier → spend on GO
send(prompt)                                   // prompt on STDIN, never argv
  → parse (≤ 2 JSON fix attempts, SAME session)
  → for round in 0..maxCorrections:
        run gates; record GateReport rows and gate events
        if clean: break
        if round === maxCorrections: throw GateFailure → phase FAILED → L8/L13
        send CorrectionRequest into the SAME session:
            phase + round · previous envelope ref · exact schema violations
            · gate checks with bounded output tails · remaining call budget
  → permissions.enforce(treeBefore)            // breach ABORTS, never corrects
  → host captures diff, creates commit, persists envelope + agent_session row

Usage accounting across corrections (SSSF's rule, agents.py:106-110): every send costs, so spend accumulates across all rounds; but context occupancy is the last send's, because retries re-enter the same window. The phase_end event carries phase totals, never the last send's. The context meter and the cost chip legitimately read different numbers from the same phase.

The escalation ladder

RungMechanismCostWho authorizes
1JSON parse retry — re-prompt the same session with the exact violationstokenshost, ≤ 2 per send
2Intra-phase gate correction — same session, violations + bounded tails attachedtokenshost, auto: 1
3Owner-authorized intra-phase correctiontokensowner, owner: 1 per phase, if ceiling has room
4Inter-state correction — GATING → RUNNING (L10) with the failed command and output as evidence1 tier call (reserved, then spent)host draws the per-phase automatic tranche; an owner draws the per-attempt re-entry
5Owner re-entry, one per attempt: rework — REVIEWING → RUNNING (L16) / AWAITING_OWNER → RUNNING (L19), stale gates and review invalidated; or a replacement review — AWAITING_OWNER → REVIEWING (L25), stale review invalidated and gates preserved and revalidated, available only where the recorded review carried no evidence1 tier callowner / human only
6Attempt exhausted — BLOCKED; awsf retry mints attempt n+1, spend carried forwarda fresh attempthuman

A review concern reaches the human; it never automatically invokes the builder. Transport failure does not cold-restart a correction session.

Envelope & Gate Contract

Every schema below is defined once, in TypeBox, in core/src/contracts/ — yielding the runtime validator, the static TypeScript type, and the JSON Schema emission from a single source. The JSON Schema is injected into each agent prompt automatically. AWSF never maintains a handwritten envelope example beside the schema and the gate; that three-copy drift is SSSF's worst maintenance defect and it dies here.

Wire envelope vs stored envelope

SSSF conflates what the model says with what the system records. AWSF separates them: the wire envelope is only what the model emits — a model is never asked to echo a phase_id it could get wrong — and the host wraps it into the stored envelope with identity, validity, and provenance.

interface ArtifactClaim {
  path: string;                 // worktree-relative, normalized, no traversal
  kind: "source" | "test" | "plan" | "documentation" | "report";
  description: string;
}

interface EnvelopeBase {                     // WIRE — what the model emits
  schema: string;                            // e.g. "awsf.build-output/v1"
  producerStatus: "success" | "failure";
  summary: string;
  artifacts: ArtifactClaim[];
  notesForNextPhase: string;
}

interface StoredEnvelope<T extends EnvelopeBase> {   // HOST wrapper
  envelopeId: string; sessionId: string; phaseId: string;
  correctionRound: number; agent: string; schemaId: string;
  valid: boolean; createdAt: string;
  payload: T | null;
  violations: ValidationViolation[];
  rawOutputPath: string;
}

Phase envelopes

interface PlanOutput extends EnvelopeBase {
  schema: "awsf.plan-output/v1";
  goals: string[]; nonGoals: string[];
  implementationSteps: Array<{ id: string; title: string; files: string[];
                               acceptanceCriteria: string[] }>;
  testStrategy: string[];
  risks: Array<{ risk: string; mitigation: string }>;
  openQuestions: string[];        // a successful plan may leave none that are blocking
}

interface BuildOutput extends EnvelopeBase {
  schema: "awsf.build-output/v1";
  changedFiles: string[];         // candidate SHA deliberately ABSENT — the host computes it
  implementationNotes: string[];
  commandsRun: Array<{ argv: string[]; exitCode: number | null }>;
  proposedCommitMessage: string;
}

interface TestOutput extends EnvelopeBase {  // HOST-generated, never model-generated
  schema: "awsf.test-output/v1";
  passed: boolean; candidateSha: string;
  commands: Array<{ gateId: string; argv: string[]; exitCode: number;
                    durationMs: number; outputRef: string }>;
  failures: string[];
  outputTail: string;             // verbatim, unparsed, ≤ 4000 chars
}

interface ReviewFinding {
  id: string;
  severity: "low" | "medium" | "high" | "critical";
  file: string; line: number | null;
  title: string; detail: string; evidence: string;
}

interface ReviewOutput extends EnvelopeBase {
  schema: "awsf.review-output/v1";
  verdict: "accept" | "concern";
  reviewedSha: string;
  findings: ReviewFinding[];
  limitations: string[];
}

interface DocumentOutput extends EnvelopeBase {
  schema: "awsf.document-output/v1";
  changedFiles: string[];
  documentedAreas: Array<{ subject: string; documentPath: string }>;
  proposedCommitMessage: string;
}

interface ScoutOutput extends EnvelopeBase {
  schema: "awsf.scout-output/v1";
  findings: Array<{ file: string; note: string }>;
}

TestOutput.outputTail carries the failure verbatim and unparsed (SSSF's reasoning at data_types.py:158-163): the builder cannot open a log file it was never handed, and every test runner formats failures differently, so a generic parser would be confidently wrong.

Validation and parse-retry

  • Final assistant content must be exactly one JSON object; prose and code fences are rejected — the parser strips fences and takes the outermost {…} before rejecting.
  • Maximum envelope size 256 KiB. Unknown fields rejected. Paths normalized and containment-checked against the worktree.
  • ≤ 2 JSON fix attempts, in the same session, sharing the correction allowance.
  • Invalid envelopes are retained with their violations so the trace shows what the model actually said. Valid envelopes are written immutably to disk, appended to the journal, then projected.
  • Downstream phases receive validated payloads by host rendering into {previous_envelope} in the compiled prompt — never via conversational memory.

The thirteen gates

gate(envelope, ctx) ⇒ GateReport, where report.check(item, ok, note) appends and returns this (ported from data_types.py:264-283). Every check is recorded whether or not it passed — a green gate says what it verified, not merely that it passed.

GateApplies toWhat it checks
envelope_validevery agent phaseschema parses; producerStatus === "success"
artifacts_existevery agent phaseeach declared artifact exists; note carries size
files_non_emptyplan, documentdeclared artifacts are non-zero
json_parsesany phase declaring .json artifactsit parses; note carries the top-level type
diff_matches_claims ★build, fix, documentexact set equality between the host-captured diff (including deletions) and changedFiles — closing SSSF's one-directional hole at gates.py:61-68, which never notices files the agent changed and did not report
head_advancedbuilda host commit exists and differs from base — the "said done, wrote nothing" gate
candidate_hygiene ★every host-created candidatenon-configurable git diff --check base..candidate against the exact clean candidate; bounded findings block before configured commands
no_protected_paths ★every agent phaseno policy.protected_paths entry in the change-set
writes_within_globsevery write-capable phaseevery changed path matches the agent's writes
verdict_consistentreviewaccept ⇒ no high/critical findings; concern ⇒ at least one concrete finding; reviewed SHA equals candidate
review_evidence_present ★reviewthe review actually saw something. Two halves, both required: a pre-GO fitness precondition — the composed awsf.review-context/v1 validates, its changed-file set equals Git's, a changed candidate carries at least one hunk, a candidate that removes lines still shows removals after bounding, the digest matches the full diff on disk, and the request and acceptance criteria are present — so a review that could not be evidence is refused before a call is spent; and a post-phase check that the exact serialization reached the compiled prompt, by digest. Every check of verdict_consistent passes vacuously for an empty finding list, and findings are empty when nothing is inspectable; this is the gate that makes "nothing was inspectable" a failure
commands_pass(gateId)code phasesconfigured argv, clean before/after, exit 0, bounded tail on failure
journey_passesT2 / protectedthe recorded end-user journey ran against the exact candidate SHA

★ = strengthened or new in AWSF. Gates by phase: Plan — envelope, non-empty goals/steps/acceptance, no blocking questions. Build — envelope, exact diff match, writes allowed, protected machinery unchanged, head advanced. Candidate — immutable whitespace hygiene over the exact base-to-candidate commit range. Test — configured executable-plus-argv entries, exact candidate SHA, clean before/after, all required exit codes zero. Review — opposite provider, exact reviewed SHA, verdict consistent, finding paths inside candidate context. Document — docs allowlist, non-empty files, exact diff match, final tests re-run afterward. Final task — required phases complete, budget valid, exact SHA gated, required review present, journey passing for T2/protected, FF possible.

Why a permission breach is not a gate violation
A gate violation is work an agent can be asked to redo; a breach cannot be corrected by re-prompting because the write already happened (permissions.py:23-25). A breach aborts the phase and names every offending path. The mechanism — fingerprint the working tree's change-set before the agent runs, compare after (permissions.py:50-75) — catches what a write-watcher misses: an agent that runs git checkout to revert a file it was being judged by. That is not hypothetical; it happened to SSSF's author (permissions.py:5-8). AWSF keeps this as the last of seven layers (managed worktree · clean start · adapter tool allowlist · OS sandbox where available · post-run change-set comparison · exact write-glob enforcement · canonical repo outside the sandbox root), not the only one. And two configuration rules learned the hard way carry over: tools is a capability list, not a sandbox — writes is the enforced statement; and --tools filters extension tools too, so an agent whose harness extension registers a tool must name it or the tool is silently filtered out. writes: [] restricts the repository, never the session runtime — every agent can always write its own report under the attempt directory.

Adapter & Launcher Contract

Adapters describe and parse; the broker spawns. An adapter that can spawn can bypass PID registration, so no adapter can spawn — architecture tests assert that nothing under adapters/ imports node:child_process or calls global fetch, and that only execution/transport-broker.ts spawns. Launch descriptors are pure data (the fusion harness's discipline, launch.ts:4-15): built and unit-tested down to exact argv without any child ever starting.

interface ModelInfo {
  adapter: string; provider: string; requestedModel: string;
  contextWindow: number | null;                 // null = catalog declares no ceiling
  supportsThinking: boolean; supportsTools: boolean; supportsImages: boolean;
  continuity: "same-session-correction" | "none";
  usageAuthority: "provider" | "partial" | "none";
  costAuthority: "provider" | "catalog-estimate" | "unavailable";
}

interface ProcessSpec {                          // PURE DATA. Spawns nothing.
  executable: string;                            // NAME; the broker resolves it
  argv: readonly string[];                       // never a command line
  cwd: string;
  env: Readonly<Record<string, string>>;         // allowlist + injection
  stdin: string;                                 // the prompt NEVER rides argv
  shell: false;                                  // structural, not a default
}

interface HarnessAdapter {
  readonly id: string;
  isAvailable(signal?: AbortSignal): Promise<Availability>;
  getModelInfo(model: string): Promise<ModelInfo>;
  buildSpec(request: ModelRequest): ProcessSpec;            // pure — descriptor tests assert exact argv
  parse(transport: ProcessTransport): AsyncIterable<NormalizedEvent>;
  execute(request: ModelRequest, broker: TransportBroker,
          signal: AbortSignal): AsyncIterable<NormalizedEvent>;
}

interface TransportBroker {
  startProcess(registration: ProcessRegistration, spec: ProcessSpec,
               signal: AbortSignal): Promise<ProcessTransport>;
  startHttp?(registration: DispatchRegistration, spec: HttpRequestSpec,
             signal: AbortSignal): Promise<HttpTransport>;   // future API adapters drop in here
}

Environment is an allowlist, not a filter (launch.ts:33-62): PATH, HOME, TMPDIR pass; LANG/LC_ALL/TZ/NO_COLOR/TERM are injected after filtering so ambient values cannot override them; an allowlisted key holding credential-shaped bytes is a hard E_REDACTION error, not a pass-through. System prompts go to a mode-0600 temp file, never argv.

The five adapters and their real flags

AdapterCommandNotes
claude-codeclaude --print --output-format stream-json --include-partial-messages --verbose --model <m> --no-session-persistence --effort <lvl> --append-system-prompt-file <f>, prompt on stdinRead-only profiles pass an explicit deny list (--disallowed-tools Bash,Write,Edit,NotebookEdit) rather than trusting the default — a default that widens in a future release silently widens the ceiling (launch.ts:66-76).
pi-codexpi --mode json -p --provider openai-codex --model <m> --no-session --thinking <lvl> --no-extensions --no-skills --no-prompt-templates --no-themes --no-context-files --tools <list>, prompt on stdin--no-extensions is load-bearing: it stops a child recursively loading the harness (launch.ts:139-141).
antigravityagyShips disabled behind a documented probe. Until a machine-parseable stream mode is confirmed by someone reading real output, isAvailable() returns blocked with E_ADAPTER_UNVERIFIED. The interface has a slot; the slot is empty and honest. See Questionable Q1.
stubnone — fixture transcriptsEvery phase, gate, workflow, and journey test runs on this at zero quota cost. M1–M3 and all M5 journeys never touch a subscription.
fusioncompositeFans one prompt to two backends and merges; every constituent call is separately reserved and registered — two workers plus a fuser declare three calls up front. Scope: Questionable Q5.

The twelve normalized event kinds and their invariants

run.started · model.resolved · text.delta · thinking.delta · tool.requested
tool.completed · usage · quota · notice · run.completed · run.failed · run.cancelled
  • Sequence is authoritative; provider timestamps are advisory (events.ts:45-49).
  • Exactly one terminal event per run.
  • tool.completed is an explicit settlement — a terminal never closes tool calls implicitly, and cancellation settles every open tool first (events.ts:51-54, adapters.ts:504-513, 789-798).
  • Provider tool IDs never reach a normalized event; the host mints t1, t2, ….
  • TokenUsage: null ≠ zero — null means the provider did not report it; 0 is authoritative data (events.ts:24-33).
  • A model the harness cannot represent does not silently resolve — it fails closed via E_MODEL_UNRESOLVED (adapters.ts:396-413).
  • model.resolved carries provenance: "stream-authoritative" | "route-attributed" so the UI never presents an inferred identity as a confirmed one.
  • thinking.delta streams for live display and is never persisted — reasoning token counts only; chain-of-thought content is neither handoff nor evidence.
  • Notice codes: unknown-provider-event · non-json-output · clock-skew · malformed-usage · output-truncated · sqlite-projection-failed.

The launcher barrier

Liveness while running (from run.mjs:139-146): a monitor watches process-group CPU, a record-update-failed flag, a hard timeout, and the silence window (default 2700 s). Any trip starts tree termination — TERM to the group, grace (2 s), KILL — then reports reality: survivors are enumerated and reported, never assumed dead (run.mjs:28-37). A platform port that cannot enumerate must return an error — never [].

Per-platform cancellation ladder

PlatformSupervisionSandboxv1 write-capable?
Linuxdetached process group; kill(-pgid); survivors from /procBubblewrap / Landlock brokeryes
macOSsame signals; survivors via ps -o pid=,pgid=,stat=reviewed Seatbelt / sandbox-exec brokeryes
WindowsJob Object launcher + TerminateJobObject; survivors via Get-CimInstance Win32_Processnone nativeno — route through WSL2

Native Windows hosts the dashboard and read-only workflows in v1. Native mutation stays blocked until a CREATE_SUSPENDED launcher and a filesystem sandbox broker exist — no suspended-process parity is claimed until a native helper is built and verified. The UI must distinguish os-enforced from tool-policy from unavailable; it must not call all three "sandboxed."

Cost / quota / usage authority

RouteusageAuthoritycostAuthority (default)Renders as
claude-code (Claude Pro)providerunavailable (adapters.ts:57-61 — load-bearing)— subscription, never $0.00
pi-codex (ChatGPT Plus)providerproviderthe provider-reported figure
antigravitynone until verifiedunavailable— (adapter blocked)
stub / fusionnone / per constituentunavailable / per constituent— / labelled partial when mixed
  • costAuthority is three-valued — provider | catalog-estimate | unavailable — with pricing.models empty by default. If the owner populates the pricing table, the same phases render ≈ $0.21 (est.) with the authority visible. Never a bare number without its authority; a mixed session total is labelled partial.
  • Token metrics: input, output, cache-read, cache-write, reasoning; null ≠ 0; reasoningRelation: included-in-output | additive | unknown — SSSF's "reasoning is a share of output" convention (data_types.py:399-403) becomes the included-in-output case, not a global truth.
  • Quota is never a retry — it blocks with the reset time. Routing may never read quota.
  • Errors: E_INVALID_REQUEST · E_BACKEND_FAILURE · E_TERMINAL_MISSING · E_MODEL_UNRESOLVED · E_MODEL_MISMATCH · E_TIMEOUT · E_CANCELLED · E_QUOTA_EXHAUSTED · E_POLICY_CEILING_UNENFORCEABLE · E_ADAPTER_UNVERIFIED · E_REDACTION. One retry on 5xx-class transport failure, then ProviderUnavailable. A routing failure on a mandatory review means the review did not happen — never a substitute provider, never a quieter tier (run.mjs:224-226).

Observability Contract

The pipeline, end to end — every stage host-owned, every stage bounded:

Projection-failure policy — degraded mode

A projection failure never kills a running model
It emits a sqlite-projection-failed notice, marks the session observability_degraded, and the run continues — the journal already has the event. But the attempt may not advance past GATING while degraded; the CLI reports it loudly and awsf db rebuild must succeed first. Truth is preserved, invisible completion is impossible, and no provider is ever killed for a dashboard's sake. (Contrast SSSF, where tracer.event() has no error boundary and a locked DB aborts the phase.) If projection fails before provider release, the provider simply never executes — that is the barrier working.

Tool-call folding and token accounting

  • tool.requested creates one projected tool_call row in running; updates do not create rows; tool.completed updates it with result snippet, duration, and outcome. The canonical journal retains request and completion separately.
  • Context occupancy is not a sum: it is occupancy after the last turn, while spend accumulates across all turns. The two dashboard numbers are different on purpose.
  • persist_thinking_text: false is not a setting to flip lightly — it is invariant 9 (thinking streamed, never persisted).

Schema — full DDL, adopted as written

PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL;
PRAGMA synchronous = NORMAL;
PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;
PRAGMA busy_timeout = 5000;
PRAGMA wal_autocheckpoint = 1000;

CREATE TABLE sessions (
  session_id             TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  project_slug           TEXT NOT NULL,
  task_id                TEXT NOT NULL,
  attempt                INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (attempt >= 1),
  workflow_id            TEXT NOT NULL,
  risk_tier              INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (risk_tier IN (0,1,2)),
  is_protected           INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (is_protected IN (0,1)),
  lifecycle_state        TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (lifecycle_state IN (
                           'DRAFT','PREPARED','RUNNING','GATING','REVIEWING',
                           'AWAITING_OWNER','LANDING','LANDED','BLOCKED','CANCELLED')),
  request_text           TEXT NOT NULL,
  base_sha               TEXT, head_sha TEXT, candidate_sha TEXT,
  worker_provider        TEXT, worker_model_requested TEXT, worker_model_resolved TEXT,
  review_provider        TEXT, review_verdict TEXT,
  call_ceiling           INTEGER NOT NULL,
  calls_reserved         INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (calls_reserved >= 0),
  calls_spent            INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (calls_spent >= 0),
  corrections_auto       INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  corrections_owner      INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  input_tokens INTEGER, output_tokens INTEGER,
  cache_read_tokens INTEGER, cache_write_tokens INTEGER,
  reasoning_tokens INTEGER, total_tokens INTEGER,
  reasoning_relation     TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'unknown'
                         CHECK (reasoning_relation IN ('included-in-output','additive','unknown')),
  usage_authority        TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'none'
                         CHECK (usage_authority IN ('provider','partial','none')),
  estimated_cost_usd     REAL,
  cost_authority         TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'unavailable'
                         CHECK (cost_authority IN ('provider','catalog-estimate','unavailable')),
  cost_partial           INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (cost_partial IN (0,1)),
  observability_degraded INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (observability_degraded IN (0,1)),
  archived               INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (archived IN (0,1)),
  started_at TEXT NOT NULL, updated_at TEXT NOT NULL, ended_at TEXT,
  state_revision         INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (state_revision >= 0),
  last_projected_seq     INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (last_projected_seq >= 0),
  config_snapshot_json   TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (json_valid(config_snapshot_json)),
  journal_path           TEXT NOT NULL,
  UNIQUE (project_slug, task_id, attempt)
) STRICT;

-- The state machine's audit trail. SSSF has no equivalent because it has no state machine.
CREATE TABLE transitions (
  transition_id  TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  session_id     TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sessions(session_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  seq            INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (seq >= 1),
  from_state     TEXT NOT NULL, to_state TEXT NOT NULL,
  actor          TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (actor IN ('host','owner','human')),
  edge_id        TEXT NOT NULL,                       -- 'L20'
  reason_source  TEXT NOT NULL, reason_code TEXT, reason_detail TEXT,
  spawn_site     INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (spawn_site IN (0,1)),
  at             TEXT NOT NULL,
  UNIQUE (session_id, seq)
) STRICT;

CREATE TABLE phases (
  phase_id       TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  session_id     TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sessions(session_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  ordinal        INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (ordinal >= 1),
  phase_key TEXT NOT NULL, name TEXT NOT NULL,
  kind           TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (kind IN ('agent','code','engineer')),
  owner TEXT NOT NULL, description TEXT NOT NULL,
  status         TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN (
                   'QUEUED','RUNNING','VALIDATING','CORRECTING',
                   'SUCCEEDED','FAILED','SKIPPED','CANCELLED')),
  correction_count INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (correction_count >= 0),
  max_corrections  INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (max_corrections >= 0),
  error_code TEXT, error_message TEXT,
  started_at TEXT, ended_at TEXT, created_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  UNIQUE (session_id, ordinal)
) STRICT;

CREATE TABLE events (
  event_row         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
  event_id          TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  session_id        TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sessions(session_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  phase_id          TEXT REFERENCES phases(phase_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  run_id TEXT, parent_event_id TEXT,
  first_source_seq  INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (first_source_seq >= 1),
  last_source_seq   INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (last_source_seq >= first_source_seq),
  type TEXT NOT NULL, name TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT '', status TEXT,
  payload_json      TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}' CHECK (json_valid(payload_json)),
  started_at TEXT NOT NULL, ended_at TEXT,
  input_tokens INTEGER, output_tokens INTEGER,
  cache_read_tokens INTEGER, cache_write_tokens INTEGER,
  reasoning_tokens INTEGER, total_tokens INTEGER,
  estimated_cost_usd REAL,
  cost_authority    TEXT CHECK (cost_authority IS NULL OR cost_authority IN
                      ('provider','catalog-estimate','unavailable')),
  redaction_level   TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'public'
                    CHECK (redaction_level IN ('public','private-ref'))
) STRICT;

CREATE TABLE envelopes (
  envelope_id      TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  session_id       TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sessions(session_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  phase_id         TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES phases(phase_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  agent TEXT NOT NULL, schema_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  correction_round INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (correction_round >= 0),
  valid            INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (valid IN (0,1)),
  producer_status  TEXT CHECK (producer_status IS NULL OR producer_status IN ('success','failure')),
  payload_json     TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (json_valid(payload_json)),
  violations_json  TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (json_valid(violations_json)),
  file_path TEXT NOT NULL, created_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  UNIQUE (phase_id, correction_round)
) STRICT;

CREATE TABLE gate_results (
  gate_result_id   TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  session_id       TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sessions(session_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  phase_id         TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES phases(phase_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  correction_round INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (correction_round >= 0),
  gate_id          TEXT NOT NULL,
  gate_kind        TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (gate_kind IN ('pure','filesystem','git','subprocess','journey')),
  candidate_sha    TEXT,
  passed           INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (passed IN (0,1)),
  command_json     TEXT CHECK (command_json IS NULL OR json_valid(command_json)),
  exit_code        INTEGER,
  checks_json      TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (json_valid(checks_json)),   -- WHAT it verified, pass or fail
  violations_json  TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (json_valid(violations_json)),
  output_path TEXT, started_at TEXT NOT NULL, ended_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  UNIQUE (phase_id, correction_round, gate_id)
) STRICT;

CREATE TABLE processes (
  process_id       TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  session_id       TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sessions(session_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  phase_id         TEXT REFERENCES phases(phase_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  run_id TEXT NOT NULL, adapter_id TEXT NOT NULL, role TEXT NOT NULL,
  transport        TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (transport IN ('process','http','fixture','composite')),
  pid INTEGER, pgid INTEGER,
  process_start_identity TEXT,                       -- so a recycled PID is never killed by mistake
  status           TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN
                     ('RESERVED','REGISTERED','RUNNING','EXITED','FAILED','CANCELLED')),
  command_json     TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (json_valid(command_json)),
  cwd_display TEXT NOT NULL,
  registered_at TEXT NOT NULL, released_at TEXT, ended_at TEXT,
  exit_code INTEGER, exit_signal TEXT,
  cancellation_json TEXT CHECK (cancellation_json IS NULL OR json_valid(cancellation_json)),
  UNIQUE (session_id, run_id)
) STRICT;

CREATE TABLE agent_sessions (
  session_id       TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sessions(session_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  agent TEXT NOT NULL, adapter_id TEXT NOT NULL, provider TEXT NOT NULL, color TEXT,
  requested_model TEXT NOT NULL, resolved_model TEXT,
  model_provenance TEXT CHECK (model_provenance IN ('stream-authoritative','route-attributed')),
  host_continuity_ref TEXT,
  context_tokens   INTEGER,                          -- occupancy after the LAST turn, not a sum
  context_window   INTEGER,                          -- NULL = catalog declares no ceiling
  call_count       INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (call_count >= 0),
  input_tokens INTEGER, output_tokens INTEGER,
  cache_read_tokens INTEGER, cache_write_tokens INTEGER,
  reasoning_tokens INTEGER, total_tokens INTEGER,
  estimated_cost_usd REAL,
  cost_authority   TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'unavailable'
                   CHECK (cost_authority IN ('provider','catalog-estimate','unavailable')),
  created_at TEXT NOT NULL, last_used_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (session_id, agent)
) STRICT;

CREATE INDEX idx_sessions_recent      ON sessions(archived, started_at DESC, session_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_sessions_state       ON sessions(lifecycle_state, started_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_sessions_task        ON sessions(project_slug, task_id, attempt DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_transitions_session  ON transitions(session_id, seq);
CREATE INDEX idx_phases_session       ON phases(session_id, ordinal);
CREATE INDEX idx_events_cursor        ON events(session_id, event_row);
CREATE INDEX idx_events_phase_cursor  ON events(phase_id, event_row);
CREATE INDEX idx_events_type          ON events(session_id, type, event_row);
CREATE INDEX idx_envelopes_phase      ON envelopes(phase_id, created_at);
CREATE INDEX idx_gates_phase          ON gate_results(phase_id, correction_round, gate_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_processes_live       ON processes(session_id, status)
                                      WHERE status IN ('RESERVED','REGISTERED','RUNNING');
CREATE INDEX idx_agent_sessions       ON agent_sessions(session_id, last_used_at);

PRAGMA user_version = 1;

idx_events_cursor is the load-bearing index: the poll query is WHERE session_id = ? AND event_row > ? ORDER BY event_row LIMIT ? — the same rowid-cursor mechanism SSSF's reader uses (db.ts:362-384), but indexed. SSSF has no indexes at all, so every dashboard query there is a full scan every 500 ms.

Migrations, concurrency, and the rebuild guarantee

  • Migrations: numbered SQL files plus PRAGMA user_version, each applied in BEGIN IMMEDIATE. Startup refuses a database newer than the binary — the explicit fix for SSSF's marker-less additive ALTER list (tracer.py:92-99) that forces its reader to probe PRAGMA table_info on every request (db.ts:97-120). Migrations never rewrite canonical journals and never invent missing usage, cost, or model identity.
  • Concurrency: one writer per workflow process; the API reads on a readonly: true connection with every query a SELECT, except the single archive write on its own lazily-opened connection.
  • Rebuild guarantee: awsf db rebuild writes a fresh DB from journals and atomically swaps it after validation; the previous file is retained, not deleted; every dashboard view over the rebuilt DB is byte-identical to the pre-deletion state — an M2 acceptance test, not an aspiration.
  • Driver boundary: node:sqlite sits behind observability/sqlite.ts with a startup feature probe, so its relative youth is contained and a corrupt DB is disposable by design.

Visual Design Contract

The 37 screenshots in super_simple_software_factory_indydevdan_youtube_video_screenshots/ are the design spec. This section binds each component to the screenshot that defines its look, fixes the tokens, and inventories every animation. The dashboard is Vue 3 + Vite, loopback-only, cursor-polling; it contains two AWSF-only components — StateRibbon and OwnerGateCard — because SSSF has no state machine and no landing gate to render.

Design tokens — verbatim, the single source

Every animation above sits inside @media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) — under it, transitions collapse to instant state changes and the halo becomes a static ring.

Accessibility rules — correctness, not taste

  • Context meters render nothing when the denominator is unknown (context_window IS NULL) — never a fake percentage.
  • Estimated costs carry ≈ and their authority label; mixed totals say partial; subscription routes say — subscription.
  • route-attributed model identity renders visually distinct from stream-authoritative.
  • SandboxBadge states are exactly os-enforced | tool-policy | unavailable — never a generic "sandboxed".
  • Keyboard navigation and visible focus required on every interactive element; the drawer traps focus and restores it on close.
  • No critical state is communicated by color alone — every state chip carries its text, every gate badge its name.
  • Numeric columns use font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums so meters do not jitter.

Portability Matrix

One row per platform-dependent behaviour, one column per platform. Every cell starts PENDING. A cell may be filled only with evidence produced on the machine the column names — a passing Linux suite is not evidence about Darwin, and no macOS, Windows, or WSL2 claim may ever be made from another platform. This rule is itself an acceptance row: MAW's two /proc dependencies failed quietly off Linux (README.md:72-75), and this matrix is the standing instrument that prevents the recurrence.

BehaviourLinuxmacOSWindows-nativeWSL2

Tree cancellation (TERM → grace → KILL → report)PENDING — Linux desktopPENDING — M5 MacBook ProPENDING — Windows-nativePASS — 2026-08-09 · test:contract + test:sim
Launcher barrier (register → GO → exec/resume)PENDING — Linux desktopPENDING — M5 MacBook ProPENDING — Windows-nativePASS — 2026-08-09 · test:sim 54/54
Sandbox broker (bwrap / Seatbelt / none)PENDING — Linux desktopPENDING — M5 MacBook ProN/A — Windows-native shows unavailableDEFERRED — WSL2, 2026-08-09 · no live broker case in required suites
