Installed-vs-portable consistency (package.json bin)PENDING — Linux desktopPENDING — M5 MacBook ProPENDING — Windows-nativeDEFERRED — WSL2, 2026-08-09 · no packed-install case in required suites
node:sqlite feature probe (WAL, STRICT, json_valid)PENDING — Linux desktopPENDING — M5 MacBook ProPENDING — Windows-nativePASS — 2026-08-09 · test:sim opened and rebuilt live databases
Write-capable workflows end-to-endPENDING — Linux desktopPENDING — M5 MacBook ProBLOCKED by design (v1 routes through WSL2)DEFERRED — WSL2, 2026-08-09 · not exercised by contract/simulation suites

M8 (T27) fills this matrix machine by machine. The filled matrix — with per-cell dates and the command output that proved each cell — is the only artifact that may back a portability claim in the README.

Explicitly Not Built

These are removals, not postponements, and meta-tests fail if any grows back. The reasoning is counter-intuitive and worth stating once: most of the removed machinery existed to detect problems that the machinery itself created. The pilot's watchdogs patched the timing races of its own coordinators; its receipts triggered verifier relaunches against unchanged product; its doctor diagnosed states its orchestrator had corrupted. Remove the machinery and the problems it was catching stop happening. What genuinely protected the work — isolation, real tests on the real result, explicit human approval, never deleting anything — is all kept, and it was never the expensive part.

  • LLM orchestrator, supervisor, or doctor agent — diagnosis is a deterministic read-only command
  • Automatic restart of any model process — BLOCKED is a resting state
  • Background monitors or watchdog daemons
  • Receipt/manifest/digest pipelines — Git and the journal are the evidence store
  • Any dashboard write path to the lifecycle — no landing, approval, retry, cancel, or config-mutation route, asserted by meta-test
  • WebSockets or any push transport — cursor polling only
  • Any push implementation — landing is local fast-forward only
  • Automatic deletion of anything — worktrees, attempts, logs, DB files; awsf gc lists only
  • Provider fallback or substitution — a failed declared route blocks
  • Quota-aware routing — the runner never picks a provider by remaining allowance
  • Shell parsing — executable + argv array, shell: false, structurally
  • Conversational evidence — no transition may cite terminal text, checklists, or markers
  • Non-loopback dashboard binding (out of MVP; would require an explicit one-use bearer token)
  • Persisted chain-of-thought — reasoning counts only, never content

File Manifest

Two npm workspaces so the dependency allowlist is mechanically checkable per workspace. There is deliberately no orchestrator/ directory — there is a CLI that calls a workflow that opens phases; naming a directory "orchestrator" is how one grows back. Everything below is new; the repository does not exist yet.

  • new awsf.config.yaml — the only committed tuning surface: adapters, routing, agents (Model·Prompt·Harness·Tools dial), workflows, gates, risk, policy, observability, pricing (empty by default)
  • new package.json — workspaces ["core","dashboard"]; engines node ≥ 22.12; canonical scripts (see Validation)
  • new tsconfig.json — strict; erasableSyntaxOnly-compatible settings for strip-only mode (no enums, no parameter properties, no decorators)
  • new justfile — just plan | build | sdlc | dash | doctor | rebuild; thin wrappers over the npm scripts, never a second truth
  • new AGENTS.md — the invariants an agent working on AWSF must not break; no live task state, ever
  • new README.md — usage + the portability table with per-machine status
  • core/ core/src/cli/{main.ts, tty.ts, commands/} — command surface: new start run status watch rework land cancel retry doctor db rebuild dash gc; tty.ts owns the isTTY checks
  • core/ core/src/config/{schema.ts, load.ts, effective-config.ts} — TypeBox config schema; loader; redacted effective-config for API/UI
  • core/ core/src/state/task-machine.ts — PURE. 10 states, 24 edges, 76 rejections, ordered errors; imports nothing impure (fenced by test)
  • core/ core/src/state/phase-machine.ts — the phase submachine; same purity fence
  • core/ core/src/state/{guards.ts, tiers.ts, errors.ts} — evidence guards per edge; tier/ceiling math; the 11 ordered rejection error classes
  • core/ core/src/contracts/{envelope-base, plan-output, build-output, test-output, review-output, document-output, scout-output, normalized-events}.ts — TypeBox schemas: runtime validator + static type + JSON Schema emission from one source
  • core/ core/src/workflow/{compiler.ts, engine.ts, phase.ts, corrections.ts} — compile recipes (earned-description fires here), drive phases, correction loop
  • core/ core/src/workflow/recipes/{scout, plan, build, plan-build-test, build-review, simple-sdlc}.ts — the six workflows, one file each
  • core/ core/src/execution/transport-broker.ts — THE ONLY module importing node:child_process (architecture-tested)
  • core/ core/src/execution/launcher-barrier.ts — PID-before-exec handshake, platform-neutral contract
  • core/ core/src/execution/launcher.ts — the gated child: report identity, block for GO, exec/resume
  • core/ core/src/execution/process-controller.ts — TERM → grace → KILL → enumerate survivors → report reality
  • core/ core/src/execution/platform/{posix.ts, darwin.ts, win32.ts} — the three ProcessSupervisor ports under one contract suite; enumeration failure errors, never []
  • core/ core/src/execution/call-budget.ts — reservations: reserve → spend-on-GO → release-on-registration-failure; spend carried across attempts
  • core/ core/src/execution/continuity-store.ts — provider session refs, mode 0600, never in API responses
  • core/ core/src/adapters/{interface.ts, registry.ts, catalog.ts} — HarnessAdapter/ProcessSpec/ModelInfo; registration; local model catalog
  • core/ core/src/adapters/{claude-code.ts, pi-codex.ts, antigravity.ts, stub.ts, fusion.ts} — the five adapters; antigravity ships as probe + E_ADAPTER_UNVERIFIED; fusion per Q5
  • core/ core/src/adapters/stream/{line-framer.ts, event-sequencer.ts, output-budget.ts} — ported from fusion-harness patterns with attribution; per-instance decoders; bounded capture
  • core/ core/src/persistence/{journal.ts, status-store.ts, attempt-lock.ts, replay.ts, platform-paths.ts} — append-only fsynced journal; atomic status; locks; replay; state-root resolution
  • core/ core/src/observability/{sqlite.ts, projector.ts, rebuild.ts, queries.ts} — driver boundary + feature probe; the only SQLite writers; rebuild with retained prior file; read queries for API
  • core/ core/src/observability/migrations/0001-initial.sql — the DDL above; user_version stamped
  • core/ core/src/gates/{interface.ts, envelope.ts, artifacts.ts, git-diff.ts, candidate-hygiene.ts, commands.ts, command-evidence.ts, review.ts, review-diff.ts, journey.ts} — the thirteen gates; GateReport.check() records every check; review-diff.ts is the diff-bounding discipline (whole hunks, deletions before additions, every omitted file named), deliberately separate from command-evidence.ts's head/first-failure/tail
  • core/ core/src/git/{worktrees.ts, changes.ts, commit.ts, land.ts} — host-owned Git: worktree create/list (no delete), safe filesystem-only provisioning of configured ignored dependency seeds, change-set capture, deterministic commits, FF-only landing
  • core/ core/src/policy/{permission-profiles.ts, path-policy.ts, sandbox-broker.ts, risk.ts, redaction.ts} — tool profiles; write-glob + protected paths; OS sandbox grants; tier classification; credential scrubbing
  • core/ core/src/api/{server.ts, routes.ts, responses.ts, security.ts} — node:http, loopback-only, Host/Origin validation, strict CSP, the seven read routes + one archive write
  • core/ core/test/{unit, contract, simulation, journeys, platform, fixtures/{providers, permissions}}/ — the pyramid; fixtures include captured real provider streams (M4) and permission cases
  • new prompts/{scout, planner, builder, reviewer, documenter}/{system.md, user.md} — versioned prompt pairs; schema JSON injected at compile time, never handwritten
  • dashboard/ dashboard/shared/types.ts — imported by BOTH the API layer and the client — SSSF's pattern (apps/visualizer/shared/types.ts), kept
  • dashboard/ dashboard/src/{components, composables, routes, stores, styles}/ — Vue 3 components per the Visual Design Contract; usePolling composable owns cadence/backoff/focus rules
  • dashboard/ dashboard/vite.config.ts — dev proxy to the local API; build to static assets served by the API process
  • new specs/{awsf-plan.html, awsf-plan-build-prompts.md, awsf-plan-acceptance.md} — this plan and its two companions (the three files that exist today)
  • new records/pilots/ — concise accepted pilot summaries from M8, not routine receipts
  • new specs/tickets/{T01…T30}.md + README.md — this plan's thirty tasks, one file per task, frontmatter (id, title, milestone, tier, state, depends_on, workflow) over the build prompt carried byte-identical; the README records which fields are derived and by what rule. Split from the companion 2026-08-06; the M9 fixture corpus
  • core/ core/src/contracts/ticket.ts — M9 — TypeBox Ticket: one source, validator + static type + JSON Schema, exactly as every envelope
  • core/ core/src/persistence/ticket-store.ts — M9 — frontmatter read/write over specs/tickets/*.md; no database of its own, no SQLite write (the M1 fence holds)
  • core/ core/src/workflow/recipes/intake.ts + prompts/intake/{system.md, user.md} — M9 — the seventh recipe; its agent's writes names specs/tickets/** and nothing else
  • dashboard/ dashboard/src/routes/backlog.vue — M9 — the board; read-only, one more read route, zero new write routes

Milestones & Tasks

IMPORTANT: Execute every milestone and task step by step, in order, top to bottom.

Status markers: [] idle · [wip] in progress · [x] complete · [f] failed. All start as []; the Build Plan workflow updates them as it works. Each task is sized for one worker in one fresh session and carries explicit entry conditions, outputs, proofs, and stopping criteria. No task produces a receipt; Git and the journal are the evidence store. Every milestone before M4 runs entirely on the stub adapter — zero quota spend.

The seven ordering gates — none may be resequenced
G1 State-transition tests green before any provider-spawning code exists (a fresh session handed the whole design will build the barrier first, because it is the interesting part, and then have no way to prove it gates anything). · G2 Journal/status crash recovery green before the barrier may release a provider. · G3 Stub adapter and process simulations green before any live provider probe. · G4 Sandbox/worktree/write-boundary controls exist before a real write-capable model runs. · G5 Envelope and failed-gate correction journeys green before implementation pilots. · G6 Human landing and end-user journey tests pass before production adoption. · G7 Platform claims made only after execution on the destination machine.

Exists so the owner approves the architecture before any code. Deliverables: this plan, the companion build prompts, and the acceptance checklist. Authoring is complete; the milestone closes only on owner approval.

[x] Milestone M1: Contracts & State

Exists because every other component depends on transition correctness, and because G1 demands the refusals be provable before anything can spawn. Five tasks. Pure code plus meta-tests; no I/O beyond the config loader reading a YAML file; nothing touches a provider or a process.

Intra-milestone ordering gate (G1)
T4's transition suite — all 24 legal edges, all 76 rejections with their exact error classes, the full 11-step rejection order, the actor matrix, and the ceilings — must be green (via T5) before a single line of broker, barrier, or launcher code is written in M3. T4 writes the suites RED against a module that does not exist; T5 implements until green. If M3 begins early, the system gains the ability to spend provider quota before it has the ability to refuse an illegal state change — the predecessor's exact failure ordering.

[x] Milestone M3: Execution Kernel ⚠

The riskiest milestone in the plan — it is where a wrong answer fails quietly, exactly the defect class this redesign exists to eliminate. Give it the most capable model and the most attention (see the companion build prompts' budgeting). Four tasks, all on the stub adapter; no live provider exists anywhere in M3.

[x] Milestone M4: Real Adapters

Exists to connect the two working subscriptions natively — the CLI-subprocess pattern is the design, not a workaround — and to park the third honestly. Three tasks. Fixture integrity rule: no task may satisfy itself with fixtures invented from the same unverified protocol assumption it is testing; every real adapter requires a captured real stream fixture from one bounded live probe. Cross-building rule: where practical, the opposite provider reviews or implements the adapter whose assumptions concern that provider.

Testing Pyramid

Seven layers. Each row states what the layer proves and what it cannot — the second column is the one that keeps verification honest.

LayerProvesCannot proveRuns
1 · Unit (pure state, contracts, gates, budget math)The 24/76 matrix, the 11-step rejection order, schema validity, glob semantics, reservation arithmeticAnything about real processes, files, or providersevery session · npm run test:unit
2 · Contract (ports: ProcessSupervisor, adapter descriptors)Every platform port satisfies one behavioral contract; every adapter's argv is byte-exact and pureThat the contract holds on a machine it has not run on (G7)every session · npm run test:contract
3 · Simulation (crash injection, process trees, projector, degraded mode)Kill-host-mid-registration leaves no orphan; grandchildren are reaped; rebuild is identical; recovery never guessesReal provider protocol behaviorevery session · npm run test:sim
4 · Journeys (stub end-to-end)The whole economic story — correction, breach abort, review inversion, TTY landing — at zero quotaThat real streams parse; that real models behave like fixturesevery session · npm run test:journeys
5 · Bounded live probes (one per provider, M4)Real route, real stream shape, real model identity, real usage fields — captured into fixturesOngoing provider stability; anything about the unprobed provider (agy)once per adapter, ≤ 1 call each
6 · Platform verification (T27, per machine)The matrix rows, on the machine named, from that machineAnything about a machine it has not run on — a green Linux suite is not evidence about Darwinper machine · test:contract + test:sim locally
7 · Pilots (two real tasks, M8)The end-user journey; real costs, counted and categorized; cross-provider review liveLong-run economics — that takes accumulated instrumented dataonce each · live

Destination-hardware testing is explicitly pending until run on the actual machines — the macOS column of the Portability Matrix stays PENDING until the M5 MacBook Pro executes it locally.

Validation Commands

Canonical package scripts are the validation interface. T1 creates exactly these scripts; T28's reconciliation test asserts every command documented anywhere exists for real. Nothing here has been executed yet — this is a planning artifact and every box below starts unchecked; the Build Plan workflow checks them as milestones complete.

  • [] npm run test:unit — 25 legal transitions accepted; 75 rejected with the exact error class each; the 11-step rejection order pairwise; full actor matrix; per-tier and lifetime ceilings; evidence guards; phase submachine; contracts single-sourced; config loader rejections; gate positives and negatives; display-correctness rules
  • [] npm run test:contract — the supervision contract on this machine's port (enumeration failure ⇒ error, never []); byte-exact adapter descriptors; no prompt in argv
  • [] npm run test:sim — kill-host between registration and release ⇒ no provider process exists; registration failure ⇒ reservation returned, provider never ran; a valid second compiled agent phase launches under durable RUNNING and spends on GO; grandchild reaped with truthful survivor report; crash injection at every write-protocol boundary; rebuild byte-identity with prior file retained; degraded mode blocks past GATING; crash-mid-LANDING recovery
  • [] npm run test:journeys — stub SDLC end-to-end with zero quota: failed gate → same-session correction (no call spent) → landing via TTY; breach abort naming paths; review inversion with no substitute; awsf land refusing non-TTY; awsf review replacing one unevidenced review at one call and refusing an evidenced one at zero cost; the human gate un-bypassable with exactly one call site
  • [] npm test — the full suite in one invocation, leaving git status --porcelain clean
  • [] npm run typecheck — tsc --noEmit + vue-tsc both green
  • [] npm run lint — oxlint green
  • [] Architecture meta-tests (inside test:unit): no node:child_process outside the broker · no SQLite write outside projector/migrations · state/ pure · no shell:true · dependency allowlist matches both package.json files · no push/auto-delete path · no landing/approval/retry/cancel/config route in the API table · no credentials in journals, DB, responses, or fixtures · no committed runtime artifacts
  • [] awsf doctor — exit 0 healthy; exit 1 listing an injected orphan by PID, mutating nothing
  • [] awsf db rebuild — fresh DB from journals, views identical, prior file retained
  • [] live — one bounded probe per real provider (M4), captured as fixtures, ≤ 1 call each
  • [] live — both pilots landed within their ceilings, calls counted and categorized (M8)
  • [] Portability matrix — every cell filled only from the machine it names; README claims match cells
🔁 The plan is not complete until every box is checked and every command passes. If a step is genuinely impossible as written, mark it [f] with the design correction — never fabricate evidence.

Questionables

Ten decisions — the original nine, plus Q10 raised on 2026-08-06. Each carries a recommended default and the consequence of choosing otherwise. Clicking a verdict in the browser records a proposal that travels via Export Feedback; the text here is updated only by an agent session applying that feedback. Q1 gates Task 1.

DECIDED Q1 — Can agy (Antigravity) emit a machine-parseable stream?

Decided (2026-08-04): Ship probe + stub; verify agy later — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. Task 1 is unblocked; see Amendments.

Recommended default: ship the Antigravity adapter as a probe returning blocked with E_ADAPTER_UNVERIFIED; resolve before Task 1. Nobody has run agy and read its output. Designing a parser around an unread CLI contract is how fixture fiction starts — the fixture-integrity rule exists precisely to forbid it. The probe default costs nothing: the interface has a slot, the slot is empty and honest, and the moment someone captures one real agy session the adapter graduates by the same fixture-first path as T13/T14. Choosing "verify now" costs one manual bounded run of agy and a saved transcript — perfectly reasonable if you have ten minutes, and it converts Q1 into a T15 input. Choosing "drop it" simplifies T15 but forfeits the third subscription's slot and the proof that new providers drop in without orchestration changes.

DECIDED Q2 — Dashboard in v1 or v1.1?

Decided (2026-08-04): Dashboard in v1, as milestone M7 — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: v1, as M7 — after owner controls, before pilots. Observability is half the reason AWSF exists (it is the entire SSSF inheritance), and the M7 acceptance — a live stub run visible before it completes — is the direct fix for the "work was invisible" complaint. The ordering already protects delivery: the dashboard cannot block the kernel because it comes after it. Deferring to v1.1 pulls M8 earlier at the cost of running both pilots watching JSONL tails instead of swimlanes — and pilots are exactly when you want the glass wall. CLI-only contradicts the accepted architecture and is listed for completeness.

DECIDED Q3 — Reuse MAW's $XDG_STATE_HOME layout or start a fresh one?

Decided (2026-08-04): Fresh awsf state namespace — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: a fresh awsf/ namespace under the platform state dir; MAW's records stay untouched. The AWSF attempt layout (journal + status + envelopes + gates + private continuity) is richer than MAW's record shape, and migrating MAW's live records buys nothing — the two systems can coexist during the transition, and MAW's history remains readable by MAW. Choosing reuse means writing a record-shape migrator and accepting that a bug in it corrupts the only copy of MAW's history; that is real cost for sentimental benefit.

DECIDED Q4 — Workflows as TS modules or declarative YAML?

Decided (2026-08-04): TypeScript modules under recipes/ — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: TS modules under core/src/workflow/recipes/, one file per workflow, validated by the compiler. There are six fixed recipes; a YAML workflow format means writing and testing a second schema, a second loader, and a second failure mode for a catalog that changes (per the six-not-twelve rule) only when a repeated real use case appears. The recipes stay data-shaped TS (as const phase lists), so a future YAML front-end is a mechanical translation if multi-project or non-engineer editing ever demands it. Choosing YAML now buys config-file editability at the price of a parser, a validator, and prompt-injection plumbing for a need that has not appeared.

DECIDED Q5 — Is the composite fusion adapter in v1 scope?

Decided (2026-08-04): Fusion adapter in v1.1, contract reserved in v1 — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: v1.1 — but its contract ships in v1. M3's call-budget already enforces the composite rule (declare full cost in advance: two workers + a fuser = three reservations), and the transport: 'composite' value is in the DDL, so the adapter drops in later without touching orchestration — which is the whole point of the adapter boundary. Implementing it in v1 adds a third real-adapter surface to M4 and more live spend before the pilots prove the basics. Choosing v1 is defensible if a pilot task genuinely wants fusion output; it costs roughly one extra M4-sized task and its fixtures.

DECIDED Q6 — The macOS state root

Decided (2026-08-04): ~/Library/Application Support/awsf on macOS — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: ~/Library/Application Support/awsf — the platform convention — resolved by platform-paths.ts and verified on the actual Mac in T27. MAW left this undecided in its own portability table (README.md:65); AWSF decides it now so the Mac's first run has no ambiguity. Honoring $XDG_STATE_HOME when explicitly set is included either way, so shell-config purists lose nothing. Choosing XDG-everywhere makes three platforms uniform at the cost of surprising every macOS backup/permissions tool that expects Application Support.

DECIDED Q7 — Is the pricing catalog populated at all?

Decided (2026-08-04): Ship pricing table empty — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: ship empty. Out of the box a Claude-routed phase renders — subscription, never $0.00, and mixed totals say partial — the safe rendering is the default rendering. Populating pricing.models upgrades the same phases to ≈ $0.21 (est.) with the authority label visible; that is an owner action with a dated effective_date, taken when you actually want API-equivalent comparisons. Populating at setup costs maintenance of a price table that goes stale (the 2026-07-30 price cuts made every earlier figure wrong) in exchange for estimates you may not yet need.

DECIDED Q8 — Where do managed worktrees live?

Decided (2026-08-04): Per-machine worktree root outside repo and state root — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: a per-machine worktree root declared in machine-local config, outside both the repository and the state root — the same resolution MAW's Q4 reached. Worktrees are the third ownership class (disposable execution); they do not belong in the portable config (absolute paths are rejected there), in the repo (junk-drawer risk), or in the state root (mixing rebuildable runtime with disposable checkouts confuses both retention stories). One tree per attempt keeps collisions unrepresentable. A fixed sibling directory is simpler but breaks on the case-insensitive-filesystem row of the matrix and litters project parents.

DECIDED Q9 — How much SSSF visualizer code is literally reused?

Decided (2026-08-04): Pattern-reuse with fresh code; attribute any literal ports — owner accepted the recommended default via the exported planf3-review v1 block. See Amendments.

Recommended default: reuse the patterns (shared/types.ts placement, rowid-cursor polling, path-segment rejection, readonly connection) in fresh AWSF code, and add an MIT attribution header (© 2026 IndyDevDan) to any file where SSSF code is carried literally — LineFramer-style ports in adapters/stream/ are the likely cases. SSSF's visualizer is Bun-served and typed to SSSF's seven tables; AWSF's API is node:http over a different schema, so wholesale forking imports more mismatch than it saves. Forking wholesale is fastest for M7's first render but pays a translation tax on every AWSF-only surface (StateRibbon, OwnerGateCard, budget chips) afterwards.

DECIDED Q10 — Does a post-LANDED publish / pull-request path exist?

Decided (2026-08-06): No publish path in v1; the contract is reserved for v1.1 — owner accepted the recommended default in session, ahead of the browser review layer rather than through an exported planf3-review v1 block. Recorded here for the same review treatment as Q1–Q9. See Amendments.

Recommended default: build no push in v1, and take the pull request's actual value — a reviewable diff, a record of what was checked, an explicit human approval — from the surfaces that already exist. The OwnerGateCard already is the pull-request page: diff stat, gate results, review verdict, the exact candidate SHA, and the command to type. T34 closes most of the remaining gap with a PR-shaped landing summary that touches no network. What a real pull request adds beyond that is a browsable permanent record living on a remote — genuinely useful, and genuinely not worth what it costs here yet. Choosing to add awsf publish in v1 is defensible only in the narrow post-LANDED, human-initiated form: the commit is already landed and already human-approved, so it opens no automated route into the canonical repository. But it costs the strongest sentence in Explicitly Not Built — "any push implementation" would have to be restated as "no push path from any pre-LANDED state", and its meta-test rewritten from a string scan into a state-aware one. Trading a mechanically-checked invariant for a convenience is a purchase to make deliberately, at v1.1, with the pilots' evidence in hand — not as a side effect of adding a backlog. Choosing "never" forecloses a capability the adapter boundary could hold cheaply, and is stricter than the reasoning requires.

Risks and Rejected Alternatives

Risks this plan carries knowingly

RiskExposureMitigation already in the plan
node:sqlite is younger than CPython's sqlite3Feature gaps or bugs surface mid-M2Driver boundary + startup feature probe; journal-as-truth makes any DB disposable via rebuild
Strip-only mode forbids enum, parameter properties, decoratorsA careless dependency or pattern breaks the no-build-step loopas const unions throughout (the fusion harness already proved this); M1 asserts a bare node --experimental-strip-types load
M3's failure modes are silent by natureA subtle ordering bug ships unseenG1–G3 ordering; kill-injection tests; the budgeting note sends the strongest model here
agy remains unverifiedThe third subscription stays parkedQ1 probe default; no design decision depends on its answer
Windows native writes deferred to WSL2Friction on the Windows machine for write workflowsHonest by design; dashboard + read-only work natively; revisit only with a real CREATE_SUSPENDED helper
Benchmark and pricing figures go staleRouting table misleadsCompanion catalog is dated, sourced, and marked operator-confirm-before-spend; instrumentation replaces it with local data
Scope creep in the dashboardM7 swallows weeksComponent table is the contract; anything not in it is v1.1; the one write endpoint is meta-tested
Two-workspace type driftAPI and client disagreedashboard/shared/types.ts imported by both sides — SSSF's own proven pattern

Rejected alternatives — and why they stay rejected

  • Python (extend SSSF). Its own code is pi-only (agents.py:61-63) — adopting the runtime means rewriting its entire provider layer anyway, for a stack that cannot reach the most-valued subscription.
  • Hybrid Python + TS. The envelope schema defined twice will drift; SSSF already invented shared/types.ts to stop exactly this — extend the idea one hop and the second runtime disappears.
  • Zero-dependency core with a hand-rolled validator. The schema must be machine-emittable as JSON Schema for prompt injection; a bespoke validator would grow that capability badly. The discipline survives as a pinned, meta-tested allowlist instead.
  • PLANNING/BUILDING/DOCUMENTING as lifecycle states. They are phases; promoting them makes the matrix workflow-dependent and untestable.
  • PREPARING/READY split. A transient distinction that adds an edge class without adding a guard — collapsed into PREPARED.
  • Adapter-owned spawn(). An adapter that spawns can bypass the barrier; the broker centralization is the single most important structural correction in the fusion.
  • WebSockets. Cursor polling matches SQLite insertion order, serves live and history through one mechanism, survives restarts, and works after the workflow process exits.
  • Hard LOC caps as CI failures. The old 1,500-line cap began producing homemade YAML parsers and line-compression tricks; fitness functions catch real defects, budgets stay advisory.
  • Native Windows write support in v1. Claiming suspended-process parity without a native helper would be exactly the quiet-failure pattern this plan exists to kill.
  • Twelve workflows. Six cover the real use cases; each addition is demand-driven.
  • An orchestrator/ directory. Naming a directory that is how one grows back.

Notes

Critical dependency chain

M1 → M2 → M3 form the spine and are strictly serial (G1, G2). M4's two adapter tasks are parallelizable across sessions after M3 (and the cross-building rule pairs them naturally with opposite providers). Inside M5, T16 → T17/T18 (parallel) → T19 → T20. M6 → M7 → M8 are serial. The longest path is the spine plus T19/T20 — protect it; dashboard work can absorb any waiting.

Counting

Ten milestones (M0–M9), thirty-four tasks (T1–T34) plus M0's authoring/review task — of which M0–M8 and T1–T30 are v1; M9 and T31–T34 are post-v1 and gated behind M8. Estimated production code 11,000–15,000 lines, tests 10,000–15,000 — advisory budgets, not gates. Quota spend across the whole plan before pilots: two bounded live probes.

Attribution

super-simple-software-factory is MIT-licensed (© 2026 IndyDevDan). Where SSSF or fusion-harness code is carried literally (stream framing, visualizer patterns), the file carries an attribution header; where only the pattern is reused, this plan's citations are the record.

What would change this plan

  • A verified agy stream (Q1) adds a third real adapter to M4 by the same fixture-first path — no structural change.
  • API keys or local models arriving later drop in as adapters (startHttp on the broker is already in the interface); the no-orchestration-change rule is the acceptance test for that day.
  • If node:sqlite's feature probe fails on any destination machine, the driver boundary confines the blast radius to one file plus a documented fallback decision — the journal never depends on it.

2026-08-04 — Milestone 1 / Task 2 complete

core/src/config/{schema.ts, load.ts, effective-config.ts} and the committed default awsf.config.yaml. schema.ts is a TypeBox awsf/v1 schema covering the full surface: project, runtime, adapters, routing (review: "invert-provider", no_fallback: boolean), agents (name/model/thinking/color/purpose/prompt/harness/tools/writes, doc-commented for the two hard-won rules — tools is a capability list and not a sandbox, and an agent's harness-extension tools must be named or they are silently filtered), workflows, gates (a commands_pass variant carrying argv plus a structural variant for the other ten kinds), risk (tier_ceilings + paths), policy.protected_paths, observability.redaction_level_default, and pricing (empty array by default, per Q7). Known-identifier sets (adapter kinds, workflow ids, gate kinds) live in schema.ts as as const tuples but membership is checked in load.ts, not baked into the TypeBox shape, so the loader can carry the "unknown adapters/workflows/gates" rejections independently of the structural schema. load.ts parses YAML, runs TypeBox validation first (ConfigSchemaError), then a fixed order of semantic rejections: absolute machine paths (POSIX/UNC/drive-letter/home-relative, scanned over every string leaf in the parsed document) → credential-shaped values (reusing the same pattern set as the credential-pattern meta-test, plus a Bearer-token and a JWT-shaped pattern) → unknown adapter kind / undeclared harness reference / unknown workflow id (including runtime.default_workflow) / unknown gate kind → tier ceiling not in {1,3,5} → routing.no_fallback !== true. Each rejection is its own ConfigError subclass so tests assert on instanceof/code rather than message text. effective-config.ts builds a redacted snapshot (structurally identical to AwsfConfig, string leaves that still look like a path or credential replaced with "[REDACTED]" as a defense-in-depth net beyond the loader) and toConfigSnapshotJson() for the sessions.config_snapshot_json column. The proposal's §7.3.5 config example was not present anywhere in this repository (only referenced) — the schema and default YAML were derived instead from every field this plan itself specifies for T2 (Ownership and Trust Boundaries, the Envelope & Gate Contract's eleven gates, the tier/ceiling table, the five adapters and six workflows, the visual design contract's agent roster) and cross-checked against awsf-plan-build-prompts.md's D-decisions; flagging this rather than silently inventing an unfounded "proposal quote". Added 42 unit tests under core/test/unit/config/ (schema/load/effective-config), all green; full npm run test:unit is 42/42. npm run lint clean. npm run typecheck shows no new errors — the only failures are the pre-existing node:* typings gap flagged in T1's amendment. Code committed as 5bf5fbe. T2's checklist flipped to [x]; M1 header remains [wip] (T3–T5 outstanding). built on: claude:sonnet-5.

2026-08-05 — Milestone 1 / Task 3 complete

core/src/contracts/ — fourteen modules, one of them a barrel. envelope-base.ts holds ArtifactClaim, the five EnvelopeBase fields, and phaseEnvelope(), which composes each phase envelope by spreading the base properties rather than $ref-ing them, so every emitted JSON Schema is one self-contained document — an agent prompt cannot follow a reference to a sibling file. The six phase envelopes (plan/build/test/review/document/scout-output.ts) match the Envelope & Gate Contract field for field, including ReviewFinding.severity as the four-value ordered set, with severityRank() for the verdict_consistent and L16 "≥ medium" guards. BuildOutput deliberately has no candidateSha, and a test asserts its absence rather than trusting the omission. normalized-events.ts carries the 12 kinds as an as const union (no enum anywhere — strip-mode-safe), the six notice codes including sqlite-projection-failed, the eleven E_* run error codes, provenance on model.resolved, and a per-kind TypeBox schema for all twelve so the journal can validate what it stores. StoredEnvelope is the host wrapper, with wrapEnvelope() as its only constructor so the payload/valid/violations relationship cannot be assembled inconsistently: valid ⇒ payload and no violations, invalid ⇒ null payload and retained violations, and an "invalid" envelope carrying zero violations throws. json-schema.ts emits and injects; parse-envelope.ts parses; registry.ts is the single schema-id lookup, so an unregistered envelope is unusable rather than silently unvalidated.

Five judgment calls the plan left implicit, recorded here rather than buried in the code:

  • Fence and prose salvage succeeds; it does not fail. The contract reads "prose and code fences are rejected — the parser strips fences and takes the outermost {…} before rejecting", so salvage is attempted first and only a payload surviving neither path is rejected. The parse result records extraction: "exact" | "fence-stripped" | "outermost-object", so the phase engine can tell "the model emitted clean JSON" from "the host had to dig it out" at the moment it decides whether to spend a parse-retry. It is not carried onto StoredEnvelope: that interface is normative and this task does not widen it, and the verbatim output is retained at rawOutputPath regardless, so nothing about what the model actually said is lost. The outermost-object scan is first-{-to-last-} and deliberately not brace-balanced: anything cleverer starts guessing which of several objects the model meant, and a wrong guess is worse than a clean rejection the model can be re-prompted about.
  • The 256 KiB cap is measured in UTF-8 bytes, by a hand-written utf8ByteLength(). TextEncoder has no type declaration under lib: ES2023 without @types/node (outside the D2 allowlist), and a cap that silently measured UTF-16 code units would let a multibyte payload sail straight past it. A test checks the hand-rolled counter against the platform encoder across BMP, astral, and lone-surrogate samples.
  • The wire/stored split is asserted mechanically, not merely documented. HOST_OWNED_FIELD_NAMES enumerates every StoredEnvelope field in both camel and snake case — the one exception being the wire envelope's own schema literal, which the model must emit — and a test asserts that no phase envelope schema declares any of them, plus a second test asserting the deny list has not fallen behind the wrapper it guards. So a future envelope cannot quietly start asking a model to echo its own phase_id.
  • Artifact-path containment is a schema pattern, not only a gate. Absolute POSIX, drive-letter and UNC paths, backslash separators, .. segments, ./ prefixes and doubled separators are rejected structurally, so the rule travels with the emitted JSON Schema into the agent's own prompt instead of only firing after the fact. Containment against the real worktree root stays a gate concern; this is the structural floor beneath it. The rule is applied to every declared path field in every envelope — ArtifactClaim.path, both changedFiles, documentedAreas.documentPath, ScoutOutput.findings.file, ReviewFinding.file, and PlanOutput.implementationSteps.files. The first pass applied it to only four of the seven, leaving review findings and plan step files as bare strings, so the same traversal path was rejected by one envelope and accepted by another; a regression test now pins all of them together.
  • Tool-settlement and sequence invariants live here, as a pure checker. validateEventSequence() reports contiguous seq, exactly one terminal and it last, no settlement without a request, no double settlement, no reused id, and — the load-bearing one — no tool still open at any terminal, run.cancelled included, since cancellation settles open tools first. It returns every violation rather than throwing on the first, so a trace can show the whole picture of what a parser got wrong. T16's event sequencer implements against it.

Two smaller notes. phaseEnvelope() and eventSchema() have inferred return types: annotating them means restating the property merge as an intersection, and an intersection collapses the overridden schema/kind literal to never — the first pass did exactly that and it surfaced as Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'never' across every fixture. And injectOutputSchema() throws when a template lacks {output_schema}: a prompt that silently ships without its schema is precisely the failure this task exists to make impossible, so it fails at compile time rather than at parse time.

The no-handwritten-schema meta-test (core/test/unit/meta/no-handwritten-schema.test.ts) scans prompts/ for six patterns — inline JSON Schema keywords, an inline "type": "object", an awsf.*-output/v1 literal, a transcribed EnvelopeBase or phase field, a pasted TypeScript interface — plus any JSON code fence. It also carries a self-check asserting its own patterns still match a known-bad specimen, and a check that prompts/ is actually reachable by the walker, so it cannot rot into vacuous green. Trivially green today (prompts/ holds only scaffolding); load-bearing the moment T20 writes real prompt text into it.

Added 134 tests under core/test/unit/contracts/ plus 4 in the new meta-test; full npm run test:unit is 188/188 green (was 50). npm run lint clean. npm run typecheck shows no new errors from these files — the only remaining failures are the pre-existing node:* typings gap flagged in T1's amendment and its implicit-any cascade in the older meta-tests. node --experimental-strip-types loads core/src/contracts/index.ts with no build step. T3's four checklist boxes flipped to [x]; the M1 header stays [wip] (T4–T5 outstanding). 2026-08-05 — Milestone 1 / Task 4 complete (suites RED, by design)

Five suites at the paths the acceptance checklist names — core/test/unit/{transitions, rejection-order, actors, ceilings, evidence}.test.ts — plus two shared non-test modules. _lifecycle-tables.ts is the Lifecycle Contract transcribed by hand: the ten states, the twenty-four legal edges with their Actor and ✦ columns, all seventy-six illegal ordered pairs enumerated literally as four class lists, the eleven-step rejection order, the ceilings and the correction allowance, the six-value reason-source allowlist, and the blocker-code vocabulary. Nothing in it is derived from core/src/state/**, which is the entire point: a suite that imports its expectations from the implementation proves nothing. _lifecycle-harness.ts declares the transition contract T5 must satisfy (TransitionInput / TransitionResult / BudgetState / SpawnRequest, and the tiers.ts and errors.ts surfaces) and carries one fully-guard-satisfying input per legal edge, so every field in those fixtures is load-bearing: remove one and the edge must start failing.

The RED mechanism is deliberate. Module references are lazy, non-literal dynamic import()s. Non-literal so tsc does not report a file that is absent by design (typecheck shows no new errors from these files — only the pre-existing node:* typings gap from T1's amendment); lazy so each test fails on its own with ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND naming the missing file, instead of the whole file failing to load and hiding how much of the contract is unwritten. Result: 96 new tests, 92 failing with Cannot find module …/core/src/state/task-machine.ts, and 4 passing — the pure table-consistency checks (27 + 10 + 6 + 33 = 76; no pair in two classes; no pair in neither the legal set nor a class; the ✦ column equals the set of edges entering RUNNING/REVIEWING). Full npm run test:unit is 284 tests, 192 pass, 92 fail; npm run lint clean. core/src/state/task-machine.ts does not exist and no state-machine code was written — G1 is intact and armed.

One place the plan's two normative tables disagree, resolved in favour of the counts. Read literally, rejection step 2 (“from-state is BLOCKED/LANDED/CANCELLED”) fires before step 3 (self-transition) and would therefore claim BLOCKED → BLOCKED, LANDED → LANDED and CANCELLED → CANCELLED, making the class counts 30 TerminalAttempt / 7 AlreadyInState. The class table says 27 / 10 with the arithmetic spelled out (“3 × 9”, “all ten X → X pairs”), 76 decomposes as 27 + 10 + 6 + 33 only under that reading, and the same split is repeated in the T4 checklist, the acceptance checklist and the build prompt. The suites therefore read step 2 as narrowed to from is terminal AND from ≠ to, and rejection-order.test.ts pins the narrowing explicitly with the reasoning attached rather than leaving T5 to guess. The reasoning column supports this: it justifies steps 2 and 3 each against step 5 (“illegal pair”) and never against one another — the overlap was not considered when the order was written.

Nine judgment calls the plan left implicit, recorded here rather than buried in the suites:

  • The spawn rule is enforced as a closure, both directions. The plan calls “spawn ⇔ entering an executing state” a closure and says that is what makes the rule testable rather than enumerative. So a spawn declared on any edge outside L4/L10/L11/L16/L19 raises IllegalSpawnSite, and a spawn-site edge that declares no spawn raises it too — an edge into RUNNING that reserves nothing would be a provider launched off the books. IllegalSpawnSite is deliberately not inserted into the eleven-step order (the plan does not place it), so it is only asserted on inputs where no ordered rejection applies; an illegal pair that also attempts a spawn still hears the pair complaint.
  • Tier is the integer 0/1/2, not the config's T0/T1/T2. sessions.risk_tier INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (risk_tier IN (0,1,2)) — the DDL is the state layer's vocabulary, and it makes L11's “tier ≥ 2” and L12's “tier < 2” literal comparisons instead of a rank lookup. awsf.config.yaml's risk.call_ceiling: {T0, T1, T2} stays the configuration surface.
  • The budget mirrors the DDL columns. BudgetState is calls_spent, calls_reserved, corrections_auto, corrections_owner plus the risk.correction_allowance limits and the attempt number — so the pure machine reasons over exactly what the session row stores. callsSpent is documented and tested as task-lifetime, never attempt-lifetime.
  • Actor → tranche: host draws auto, owner and human draw owner. This is what makes “L10's second correction is owner-only” true, and what keeps step 9's rationale (“the other actor's tranche may remain”) meaningful. The allowance limits are read from the input rather than hard-coded, and a test proves a configured {auto: 2} is honoured.
  • The Actor column is read literally — no widening. L16 is the owner's edge and refuses a human; L19 and L20 are human edges and refuse an owner. The DDL carries three distinct actors and the plan is precise about which edge takes which; inventing a “human ⊇ owner” rule would quietly widen the one boundary this milestone exists to prove. The TTY requirement attaches to the edge, not the actor: the eight human edges (L3, L6, L9, L14, L18, L19, L20, L22, whose guards read “explicit, interactive” or “interactive TTY”) demand it; no host or owner edge does.
  • Steps 6/9 (correction budget) and 11 (ceiling) are scoped to the edges that consume them — otherwise L13, whose own guard requires the correction budget to be exhausted, could never be taken, and a task at its ceiling could never gate, land or block its way to a terminal state. A test pins that second half explicitly.
  • The blocker vocabulary is closed and per-edge. The plan names L21's four codes and L24's four outright and describes L2, L8, L13 and L17's causes in prose; those are transcribed into EDGE_BLOCKER_CODES (17 codes across seven blocking edges) and each edge accepts only its own subset. L5 gets L8's fault vocabulary and not preflight-failed, since preflight must already have passed to reach PREPARED. The names themselves (crash, silence, quota-exhausted, …) are this task's invention, kept distinct from the adapter-level E_* run error codes T3 defined; T5 should adopt them as written or amend this entry.
  • Every declared SHA is held to the same 40-hex rule — ^[0-9a-f]{40}$, matching the pattern T3's envelope contracts already enforce, applied to baseSha, candidateSha, headSha, review.reviewedSha and the landing's shaDisplayed. This is the direct descendant of T3's “apply the rule to every declared path field” correction: one unguarded field is one place the rule does not hold.
  • L20's review and journey requirements are tier-conditional. Required at T2 (“T1 plus opposite-provider review and end-user journey”), and the suite asserts they are not invented below it. protected is not modelled as a separate flag; the landing's protectedApprovalsValid covers the plan's “protected approvals valid” clause and is required unconditionally.

Coverage by suite: transitions 19 tests — the 10 × 10 matrix generated programmatically, exactly 24 accepts asserted equal to the L-table's pairs, exactly 76 rejects, the class → error mapping asserted pair by pair and again per class list, every rejection required to name the ordered pair it refused, awsf retry proven not to be a transition, and the spawn closure in both directions. rejection-order 13 — all ten adjacencies with purpose-built double violations, all eleven rejections shown reachable in isolation, and ten non-adjacent transitivity checks. actors 17 — the full 24 × 3 actor matrix, the L20 gate refusing host and owner outright and refusing a human on piped stdin, the tranche rules, and the reason-source allowlist swept over near-misses ("HUMAN", " git") as well as conversational sources. ceilings 16 — the three ceilings, the pre-spawn check per spawn edge per tier, reservations counted alongside spend, composite full cost, all six shipped workflows' minimum call counts against their tiers, and spend carried across attempts. evidence 31 — every Guard-column component per edge in both directions, including L16's style-note refusal, L21's four codes with every other source and code refused, and the clock sweep proving no timer can produce AWAITING_OWNER → BLOCKED.

T4's five checklist boxes flipped to [x]; the M1 header stays [wip] (T5 outstanding). Committed as e0aa2f6. built on: claude:opus-5.

Addendum, 2026-08-06 — two out-of-band commits in the same session, outside T4's deliverable. The owner asked to close the package-lock.json loose end T1's amendment left open ("generated but not committed by this session, write-protected in this environment"). Two findings made this more than housekeeping. First, nothing vouched for the file: no-credentials-in-fixtures.test.ts walked only core/test/fixtures/, and dependency-allowlist.test.ts reads the three package.json manifests and never the lockfile — so the one tracked file with no invariant-9 net under it was about to be the lockfile. Second, gitignoring it instead would have been the wrong fix: the manifests pin every direct dependency exactly (no carets), but without a committed lockfile every transitive dependency floats and D2's allowlist meta-test cannot see any of them, which is a real hole under the reproducibility G7 depends on.

So no-credentials-in-fixtures.test.ts was extended from one test to six, to match what AGENTS.md invariant 9 actually claims — "in fixtures or anywhere else": the root manifests (package.json ×3, awsf.config.yaml) get the credential-pattern sweep, and the lockfile gets four structural checks — it exists, every package resolves to https://registry.npmjs.org/ with no user:pass@ embedded, no _auth/_authToken/password/token key appears, and no resolved or version value is an absolute or file: path. Structural rather than regex on purpose: the file is mostly base64 integrity hashes, and a generic credential pattern swept over base64 is a false-positive generator. The first run failed on two entries — the @awsf/core and @awsf/dashboard workspace links, whose resolved is the repo-relative workspace directory rather than a registry URL. That was tightened rather than loosened: links are permitted only when they point at a workspace declared in the root package.json, read from the manifest instead of hard-coded, so adding a third workspace cannot silently widen what the test tolerates. Note the "lockfile exists" assertion means a clone that loses the file now fails loudly.

Verdict recorded for the record: the lockfile is clean on all four structural checks. Committed as ea084b8 (the lockfile, 1798 lines) and 430f55d (the meta-test extension) — both staged and committed by the owner directly, because this environment's damage-control guard blocks any command naming package-lock.json, which is the same wall T1 hit and the reason the file sat uncommitted for three tasks. Full npm run test:unit is now 289 tests: 197 pass, 92 fail — the 92 being T4's suites on the still-absent task-machine.ts, exactly as this task requires. npm run lint clean. No plan markers changed; this touched no milestone deliverable.

2026-08-06 — Milestone 1 / Task 5 complete (M1 closed; G1 satisfied)

core/src/state/{errors, tiers, guards, task-machine, phase-machine}.ts, written to turn T4's five RED suites green without touching what they assert. The suites were treated as the spec: nothing in src/state/ was written to be convenient, and where the implementation and a suite disagreed the suite won unless the suite was demonstrably wrong (one case, below).

The order of the checks is the module. transition() evaluates the eleven rejections strictly in the plan's order and throws the first that applies, with the spawn-site closure inserted between steps 5 and 6. That placement is a decision, not an accident: IllegalSpawnSite is not one of the eleven, and it must sit after pair legality (an illegal pair that also attempts a spawn must hear the pair complaint, because a spawn declaration is not the reason DRAFT → RUNNING is refused) and before the budget and ceiling checks (a reservation on an edge that cannot spawn is malformed, not expensive). T4's suite pins the first half; the second half follows from step 11's own rationale — the economic complaint must never mask a structural one.

Steps 6, 9 and 11 are scoped to the edges that consume them, exactly as T4's amendment warned: the correction-budget checks run only on L10/L16/L19, so L13 — whose guard requires both tranches spent — remains reachable; the ceiling check runs only where a spawn is declared, so a task at its ceiling can still gate, review-record, land and block its way to a terminal state instead of being stranded. host draws the automatic tranche and owner/human the owner tranche, which is what makes "L10's second correction is owner-only" fall out of the arithmetic rather than being special-cased. The allowance is read from budget.allowance throughout — configuration, never a constant.

T4's two open questions, answered as it asked. Step 2 is implemented narrowed to from is terminal AND from ≠ to, giving the 27 / 10 / 6 / 33 split the class table states with its arithmetic spelled out; the narrowing carries the reasoning in a comment at the check itself, not only in this document. EDGE_BLOCKER_CODES is adopted verbatim from T4 — the seventeen codes across seven blocking edges, each edge accepting only its own subset, L5 carrying L8's fault vocabulary and not preflight-failed. No code name was renamed or added.

The state layer imports the contracts rather than restating them. guards.ts takes SHA_PATTERN from contracts/test-output.ts and severityRank/BLOCKING_SEVERITIES from contracts/review-output.ts, so the 40-hex rule and the severity ordering exist once in this repository and the "≥ medium is a defect, below it is an opinion" boundary cannot drift between the schema a reviewer is prompted with and the guard that spends a call on its output. That is a deliberate dependency on core/src/contracts/ and on nothing else: no execution/, adapters/, git/ or sqlite import exists anywhere under state/. The purity fence, vacuous for four tasks against an empty directory, now walks five real files and passes.

Guards return every violation at once rather than throwing on the first. A caller fixing one missing field per round-trip is how a correction budget gets spent on nothing, and InsufficientEvidence carries the full list on .violations for the journal.

One T4 test was demonstrably wrong and was fixed. rejection-order.test.ts's transitivity case labelled "7 over 10 — the wrong actor holding only a style note" never set an actor: withEvidence("L16", …) preserves the L16 fixture's actor, which is owner — the one actor L16 permits. As written the input carried no actor violation at all, so it could only ever have produced the step-10 complaint it exists to outrank, and it would have passed against an implementation with no step 7 whatsoever. Fixed by adding actor: "host", the actor the label names, with the reasoning attached at the call site. Nothing else in the five suites was altered — no expectation was relaxed, no assertion removed.

The phase submachine lives in phase-machine.ts with its own suite, core/test/unit/phase-machine.test.ts (19 tests, new in this task — not one of T4's five). Eight states, twelve edges transcribed from the plan's diagram by hand, and the full 64-pair sweep asserting the other fifty-two are refused. The load-bearing rule is checked on the resume, not on the correction: CORRECTING → RUNNING compares adapter, provider, model and provider session id and throws SessionIdentityBroken naming every field that moved, so a cold restart cannot arrive disguised as a correction and be discovered later as a suspiciously context-free reply. CORRECTABLE_CAUSES is {schema-violation, gate-violation} — invalid JSON and correctable gate violations sharing one allowance — and permission breaches, host failures, transport errors, crashes and silence throw UncorrectableViolation rather than consuming it. "Success must be earned" is structural: initialPhaseState() is QUEUED, settleAbnormalExit() returns FAILED from every live state, and a test asserts SUCCEEDED is reachable from VALIDATING and from nowhere else.

Results. npm run test:unit is 308/308 green — 289 tests from T1–T4 (the 92 that were RED are now green, none rewritten to be easier) plus the 19 new phase-submachine tests. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings and 0 errors across 53 files. npm run typecheck shows zero new errors from core/src/; the two new entries are node:test/node:assert/strict module resolution in the new test file, the same pre-existing @types/node-outside-D2 gap flagged in T1's amendment that every other test file in this repository already produces. node --experimental-strip-types core/src/state/task-machine.ts loads bare, and so do the other four modules independently. Committed as eda3032. T5's four Testing-Strategy boxes and the M1 header are flipped to [x]: every box under M1 is now checked. G1 is satisfied — the 24/76 matrix and the eleven-step rejection order are green against real code, so M3 is unblocked, and it is unblocked in the only order that proves the barrier gates anything. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-06 — Tickets split out; M9 (Work Intake) and Q10 added after surveying an external software factory

What was surveyed, and how far it may be trusted. The owner supplied a video transcript and a cloned repository — Owain Lewis's factory, an open-source software factory — and asked what, if anything, was worth taking. Both were read strictly read-only on 2026-08-06; neither was executed, so every claim about its behavior is transcript-derived and belongs in the same audit class as this plan's "NOT re-executed" rows, while claims about its structure were verified by reading the source. Two corrections worth recording because they change how much weight the source carries: the repository is Go (control plane + React UI), not Rust as the video states; and its own docs/workflows/design.md and docs/github-ingest/design.md are marked superseded by a target architecture that renames its entire product model. The system shown in the video is mid-rewrite by its own author — so its principles were taken and its object model deliberately was not.

What it does that v1 could not, and the one idea worth adopting. Stripped of GitHub, the transferable idea is narrow and real: the unit of work is a durable, refined, individually-addressable ticket with a state, and the pipeline pulls from a queue of them. This plan had no such concept anywhere — a grep for ticket, issue, backlog, pull request, and CI returns nothing — because v1's unit of work is a CLI invocation a human types. Meanwhile this plan's own thirty tasks had been living as prose inside one 77 KB companion file: not countable, not filterable, not boardable. That is the gap, and it is the only one the survey found that a v1 invariant was not already covering better.

Done now, no plan change required. awsf-plan-build-prompts.md § Section B was split into specs/tickets/T01.md … T30.md plus a README.md. Every build prompt is carried byte-identical — verified programmatically against the source, not by eye — with frontmatter added above it: id, title, milestone, tier, state, depends_on, workflow. Two of those fields are derived rather than read off this plan, so both derivation rules are written down in the README where a reviewer can edit the rule instead of re-deriving it downstream: tier is 2 for tickets whose failure is silent by nature, whose subject is a safety boundary, or which spend live quota (T09–T12, T17, T21, T29–T30) and 1 otherwise; workflow follows from tier via this plan's own workflow table. tier is an integer, not T0/T1/T2, because the risk tiers and the task ids would otherwise collide unreadably. depends_on encodes the real parallelism from Notes § Critical dependency chain (T13/T14 both on T12; T17/T18 both on T16), not a blind chain. States mirror the markers here: T01–T05 done, T06–T30 todo — and the plan's markers remain the source of truth, so a flip here is a flip there in the same commit.

One finding the split surfaced, recorded rather than fixed. T04's Definition of Done is a RED suite, and no recipe fits it: build and plan-build-test both end in a tests phase that gates on green. Its ticket is marked workflow: build with the mismatch flagged in the body. This is demand evidence for a RED-tests recipe variant, deliberately not resolved by inventing a recipe — the six-not-twelve rule applies to findings as much as to wishes.

Added to the plan: Milestone M9 (T31–T34), post-v1, gated behind M8. T31 a TypeBox Ticket contract plus a file-backed store; T32 the intake recipe and awsf ticket; T33 awsf backlog plus the read-only /backlog route; T34 the PR-shaped landing summary. The placement is the argument: a backlog is not built before the machine that consumes it has landed two real pilots, and M9 sits after M8 so no ordering gate moves and M1–M8 are untouched. intake is the seventh workflow the Phase Contract said would only ever be added on a repeated real use case — authoring thirty tickets by hand is that use case, so it is earned and countable rather than speculative, and it still does not ship in v1. Two component-table rows are reserved and marked M9 rather than built, so M7's "the component table is the contract" defense against scope creep holds exactly as written. The Validation Commands section was deliberately left alone: M9 is post-v1 and carries its own checklist, and adding its commands to the global gate would imply v1 adoption depends on them.

Q10 raised and decided the same day: no publish/pull-request path in v1, contract reserved for v1.1. Pull requests were the owner's explicit question, and the honest answer is that a pull request requires a push, which pillar 5, D11, and Explicitly Not Built remove structurally and assert by meta-test. What a pull request is actually for — a reviewable diff, a record of what was checked, an explicit human approval — the OwnerGateCard already delivers; T34 adds the paste-able artifact. A post-LANDED, human-initiated awsf publish remains defensible later precisely because the commit is landed and approved before it runs, but it would force restating "any push implementation" as "no push path from any pre-LANDED state" and rewriting a string-scan meta-test into a state-aware one. That trade is a purchase to make deliberately with pilot evidence, not a side effect of adding a backlog. Unlike Q1–Q9 this verdict came from the owner in session rather than through an exported planf3-review v1 block; it is recorded as DECIDED with that provenance stated, so the distinction survives.

Rejected from the survey, with reasons, so they are not re-proposed. GitHub Issues as the ticket store — adds a network dependency, credentials on the runner, an untrusted-input surface, and a second source of truth competing with journal-as-truth, for a single operator whose own companion file was already the backlog. Label-driven daemon polling — contradicts "no background monitors or watchdog daemons" outright, and the source's own token-efficiency defense does not transfer: the scarce resource here is a five-hour subscription window, not metered tokens, so an autonomous loop spending one on a mediocre ticket is a real loss rather than a cheap poll. Repository fan-out across a fleet — one operator, one project. Autonomous merge — contradicts pillar 5, and the source's own author restricts it to personal projects. The Current-Source Audit table was left unchanged: it is the audit of the three source repositories this system fuses, and a surveyed-but-unadopted fourth repository belongs in this Amendment, not in that table.

Upstream, outside this repository. The planf3 skill's workflows/create-plan.md step 9 now emits specs/tickets/ — one file per unit, zero-padded ids, prompts carried verbatim, derived fields documented — alongside the concatenated build-prompts file rather than instead of it, so every future plan inherits addressable tickets without losing the single-scroll surface a human copy-pastes from. Counting after this Amendment: ten milestones, thirty-four tasks. No v1 invariant was weakened; Explicitly Not Built is unchanged. Committed as 98dff1f; npm run test:unit is 308/308 green and npm run lint clean across the change (specs only — no production code was touched). built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-06 — Ticket/plan agreement enforced by meta-test (AGENTS.md invariant 12)

The gap this closes. The tickets split earlier the same day left specs/tickets/ and this plan free to drift: nothing stopped a marker flipping here without its ticket's state following, or a build prompt being edited in one file and not the other. core/test/unit/meta/ticket-plan-sync.test.ts now fails the build on either. One honest limitation, stated rather than papered over. This plan carries no per-task status marker — only milestone <h3> markers and per-task checklists — so a ticket's state cannot be compared against a per-task marker that does not exist. It is checked against both signals that do: a task inside an [x] milestone must be done, a task inside a [] milestone must not be, and where a task carries its own checklist, all-boxes-[x] and done must agree in both directions. The bug was invisible to the split's own verbatim check, which compared prompt bodies and never parsed the frontmatter it had just written; it surfaced within seconds of the first mechanical read. The planf3 skill's step 9 gained the quoting rule and an instruction to parse emitted files before declaring the step done.

2026-08-06 — Milestone 2 / Task 6 complete (M2 opened, in progress)

core/src/persistence/{platform-paths, journal, status-store, attempt-lock}.ts, the first modules under core/src to touch the filesystem at all — every task through T5 was pure by construction, so this is where the state-purity fence's boundary (state imports nothing impure; persistence is impure and lives on the other side of it) first does real work.

State roots resolve per Q3/Q6. resolveStateRoot() honors $XDG_STATE_HOME the moment it is explicitly set (non-empty, on every platform), falls back to ~/Library/Application Support/awsf on macOS, %LOCALAPPDATA%/awsf on Windows (throwing rather than guessing if the variable is absent), and the XDG default ~/.local/state/awsf everywhere else — a fresh awsf namespace, never MAW's. The attempt tree — <state-root>/projects/<project>/tasks/<task>/<attempt>/{journal.jsonl, status.json, private/continuity.json, raw/<run-id>.jsonl, attempt.lock} — matches the Ownership section's row exactly.

Byte-prefix immutability is structural, not policed. Journal opens its file once with the "a" flag (O_APPEND) and never reopens it for truncation; every append() writes one line and calls handle.sync() (fsync) before resolving, and source_seq is recovered from the file's own last line on construction, so a restarted host resumes the sequence rather than reissuing a number a crash already wrote. The proof test captures the file's bytes after ten appends, drives ten more, and asserts the original bytes are an exact prefix of the new ones — nothing rewrites what fsync already made durable.

status.json is never partially observable because it is never written in place: every write goes to a uniquely-named temp file in the same directory and is then rename()d over the target, and rename is one atomic syscall on both POSIX and NTFS. Five hundred concurrent writers and five hundred concurrent readers were driven against the same file with Promise.all (real interleaving through libuv's thread pool, not a sequential simulation); every one of the five hundred reads parsed as complete, valid JSON. private/continuity.json goes through the same atomic-write primitive with mode 0600 instead of the default, verified by statSync().mode on POSIX.

The write protocol is one function, exactly as specified: runWriteProtocol() in attempt-lock.ts runs lock → read current status → validate (a caller-supplied function, so this layer stays decoupled from task-machine.ts's full TransitionInput shape, which belongs to the orchestration layer a later task wires in) → journal append+fsync → atomic status replace → an optional project step (absent here; T7 supplies the real SQLite projector through the same hook) → unlock, all inside one critical section. AttemptLock serializes same-process callers through an in-process queue (what the 500-writer proof exercises) and additionally acquires a wx-created lock file on disk, so a second process holding the same attempt directory fails loudly rather than corrupting it.

Sealing is durable within the lock's lifetime. seal(state) marks an AttemptLock sealed; every subsequent withLock() — including ones already queued behind the call that sealed it — throws SealedAttempt naming the terminal state, matching "the fix is awsf retry, not a different pair" from T5's TerminalAttempt. runWriteProtocol's optional sealWhenTerminal hook lets a caller seal automatically the moment a write lands a terminal status, without this layer hard-coding what "terminal" means for any particular status shape.

No new dependency. Every file imports only node:fs/promises, node:fs, node:path, node:os, and node:crypto — D2's allowlist (@sinclair/typebox, yaml) is untouched, and no-destructive-paths.test.ts stays green because release uses the async unlink, never unlinkSync/rmSync. Constructor parameter-property shorthand (constructor(private readonly x: …)) was avoided throughout after first tripping ERR_UNSUPPORTED_TYPESCRIPT_SYNTAX under --experimental-strip-types — it is not erasable, unlike simple type annotations — matching the explicit-field style state/errors.ts and config/load.ts already use.

Results. npm run test:unit is 337/337 green (was 317; twenty new: 6 platform-paths, 4 journal, 4 status-store — including the 500/500 concurrency proof — 6 attempt-lock/write-protocol). npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across the new files. npm run typecheck adds no new class of error beyond the pre-existing, repo-wide @types/node gap every node:-importing file already produces (confirmed against a stashed baseline: tsc --build already exits 1 at HEAD); no dependency was added to close it, since D2 forbids one. 2026-08-06 — Milestone 2 / Task 7 complete (M2 still [wip])

core/src/observability/{sqlite, projector, queries}.ts and migrations/0001-initial.sql — the DDL from the Observability Contract section, adopted byte-for-byte, pragmas included. node:sqlite (Node 22.23.1 here; unflagged, still experimental) proved to support everything the schema needs: STRICT tables, json_valid(), and WAL, checked directly against a scratch connection before trusting any of it.

The driver boundary. sqlite.ts is the only module (besides projector.ts and migrations/) allowed to call .exec/.run/.prepare on a SQLite handle — sqlite-write-fence.test.ts, already in place since T6, enforces it; queries.ts stays outside the fence by construction, importing only the DatabaseSync type from sqlite.ts rather than node:sqlite itself, even though it calls .prepare() for every read. openDatabase() runs probeFeatures() and runMigrations() on every non-readonly open; a readonly: true open (the API's future read path) does neither, matching the Concurrency rule's "one writer per workflow process."

Migrations, and the one wrinkle the plan's verbatim DDL forces. Migration files are read by a NNNN-name.sql filename convention, sorted by their numeric prefix, and applied strictly after the database's current PRAGMA user_version. Refusal is tested both directions in sqlite.test.ts: a database artificially bumped to user_version = 99 is refused with DatabaseNewerThanBinary naming both versions; a brand-new user_version = 0 file is migrated forward to 1 without complaint. One real obstacle: SQLite refuses to change journal_mode/synchronous ("safety level") from inside a transaction, but migration 0001 opens with exactly those two pragmas, verbatim, ahead of any CREATE TABLE. runMigrations() splits each migration file's statements and runs the two safety-level pragmas once, immediately, ahead of BEGIN IMMEDIATE — everything else, including the trailing PRAGMA user_version = 1, runs inside the transaction exactly as specified. Flagging this as the one place the "each migration applied in BEGIN IMMEDIATE" line and SQLite's own transaction rules were in tension; the DDL itself was not altered.

Idempotent projection, by cursor rather than by row. projectEvent() compares a journal record's source_seq against the session's own last_projected_seq and treats anything already at or behind that cursor as a no-op — one mechanism covers every event kind, including folded tool calls, rather than a per-kind INSERT OR IGNORE. thinking.delta still advances the cursor without ever reaching the events table, keeping invariant 9 intact at the projection layer too. Tool-call folding matches the contract exactly: tool.requested inserts one tool_call row in running (event id keyed on run + tool-call id, not on the journal record, so the completion can find it); tool.completed updates that same row's status, ended_at, and merges the result into payload_json via SQLite's own json_patch() rather than a read-modify-write in application code.

Degraded mode never throws, proven by forcing a real failure. The projector suite doesn't fake a thrown error — it pre-inserts a row that collides with the projector's own next event_id, so the subsequent INSERT genuinely fails with a SQLite constraint error. projectEvent() catches it, best-effort sets sessions.observability_degraded = 1 in its own separate write (so a failure that also breaks that write still can't throw), and returns a { ok: false, degraded: true, notice: { code: "sqlite-projection-failed", … } } outcome rather than raising — assert.doesNotThrow wraps the call directly. last_projected_seq is left untouched on failure, so a caller that fixes the underlying problem and retries the same record will reprocess it rather than silently skipping it.

Session creation and phases were deliberately left out of scope. createSession() exists (an INSERT OR IGNORE row, needed so the projector suite has a session to attach events to) but the state-machine → SQLite wiring — turning a real TransitionInput into a sessions row and a transitions row — belongs to the orchestration layer T6's Amendment already deferred, not to this task; T7's own Stop-When is about the driver, migrations, and event projection, not about who calls it. phases projection is likewise not written yet; nothing in T7's Proves line requires it.

An agent-identity slip, caught and fixed before it could compound. The first commit for this task carried a Co-Authored-By: Claude Sonnet 5 trailer — a plain lapse against AGENTS.md invariant 11 and the very lesson this repository's own history had to learn twice already (see the earlier Amendments' two git filter-repo passes). no-agent-coauthor.test.ts caught it on the very next npm run test:unit run; the commit was amended immediately, before anything was pushed, and the trailer-free commit is the one recorded below.

Results. npm run test:unit is 352/352 green (was 337; fifteen new: 6 driver/migration, 9 projector — including double-apply on both plain and folded rows, and the forced-failure degraded-mode case). npm run lint clean, 0 warnings. npm run typecheck adds twelve more instances of the same two pre-existing error classes (the repo-wide @types/node gap, and ImportMeta.dirname/.url not being declared without it — already present in core/test/unit/meta/_walk.ts) and no new class (134 → 146 errors, confirmed against a stashed baseline). specs/tickets/T07.md flipped to done in the same pass, keeping the ticket/plan sync meta-test green.
