2026-08-06 — Milestone 2 / Task 8 complete (M2 is [x]; G2 satisfied)

core/src/persistence/replay.ts and core/src/observability/rebuild.ts, plus the three simulation suites the plan names. The rule that decides every recovery case, stated once and applied everywhere: the journal is truth and the status is a derivation of it, so a disagreement between them is repaired from the journal when a crash explains it and refused by name when it does not.

The kills are real kills. runWriteProtocol gained one seam — an onStep hook and an exported WRITE_PROTOCOL_STEPS list (locked · validated · appended · status-written · projected · sealed · unlocked) — and _crash-child.ts uses it to SIGKILL its own process from inside the protocol. Nothing is simulated: no finally runs, no handle is flushed on the way out, the lock file is still there afterwards, and the parent asserts on the child's exit signal so an injection that silently failed to fire shows up as a failed test rather than a passing one. The step list lives in the implementation rather than in the suite, so the matrix cannot drift into killing a protocol the code no longer runs.

What each boundary leaves, and why exactly one of them needs a repair. A kill at locked/validated leaves an attempt one write shorter; at status-written/projected/sealed a complete one; at unlocked a complete one with no lock. Only appended is interesting: the record is durable and the status has not caught up, and recovery proves that case unambiguous by re-deriving the status the PREVIOUS record produces and finding it byte-equal to what is on disk. Every reconstruction is compared against a control run driven uninterrupted with the same identity in its own state root — JSON.stringify(recovered.status) against the control's status.json bytes, not a hand-written expectation. Each case then reclaims the lock, repairs the status, and drives one more write, so "recovered" means resumable and not merely describable.

Refusals name a key, not a file. badKey is an exact locator — journal.jsonl#line:2 for an unparseable interior line, journal.jsonl#line:2.source_seq for a gap ("expected 2, found 7"), status.json#lifecycle_state or status.json#last_source_seq for a status that disagrees with the journal on precisely that key. The codes are L21's own vocabulary (record-corrupt, unknown-state), so a refusal here transcribes onto the AWAITING_OWNER → BLOCKED edge without inventing one. A trailing unterminated line is not a refusal: Journal fsyncs before append() resolves, so an unacknowledged tail was never promised to anybody and discarding it is exact reconstruction. Recovery never writes — every corrupt file is asserted byte-identical by SHA-256 before and after, and repair is the caller's explicit act (writeStatus, reclaimLock).

Two additions to T6's lock, both forced by recovery. The lock file now carries its holder ({ pid, acquiredAt }), because a lock file that names nobody is the ambiguous-pid hazard by construction — the choice would be between deadlocking forever and stealing a lock a live host might hold. reclaimLock() removes one only when the holder still matches what recovery observed, and throws LockHolderChanged otherwise. Sealing, being in-memory, dies with the host: the suite proves a resumed host re-seals from the recovered status, so a terminal attempt does not quietly accept writes after a crash.

Rebuild: retained by hard link, swapped by rename. rebuildDatabase() reads every journal first (a corrupt one is refused before a file is even created), writes a candidate beside the target, projects, then validates — PRAGMA integrity_check, plus every session's last_projected_seq against its journal's last source_seq — and only then swaps. Retention is a link() of the live file to awsf.db.superseded-<stamp> followed by a rename() of the candidate over it: the prior bytes are retained AND there is no instant at which awsf.db does not exist. The -wal/-shm sidecars are moved aside in the same act — a stale WAL beside a brand-new database is how a "successful" rebuild silently serves the old file's pages — and the candidate is checkpointed with wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE) before closing, since it is swapped in as one file. A refused rebuild leaves the candidate on disk and the live file untouched, proven by digest. Nothing in this module deletes a database.

Byte-identity needed an ordering rule. events.event_row is the dashboard's cursor and an AUTOINCREMENT, so replaying attempt-by-attempt would renumber the cursors of anything that ran concurrently. Rebuild therefore creates sessions in (startedAt, sessionId) order and then projects every record of every journal merged back into recorded_at order, reproducing the insertion order the live database saw. The identity suite seeds two attempts whose writes genuinely interleave in real time, captures every read in queries.ts (SELECT * throughout, so a column added later is compared without the fixture being taught about it), deletes awsf.db, rebuilds, and compares with Buffer.compare — cursors included.

Degraded mode, with real corruption. The suite checkpoints the WAL back into the main file, overwrites its first page with garbage, and drops the connection's page cache with PRAGMA shrink_memory, so the very next projection reads the damage off the disk and fails with SQLite's own "file is not a database". No exception is faked. The run continues to its last write with journal and status intact; each failed projection returns a sqlite-projection-failed notice that the host writes to the journal through the write protocol (the notice's own failed projection deliberately does not emit a notice, or the first one would be buried under a cascade). assertAdvancementPermitted() then refuses REVIEWING/AWAITING_OWNER/LANDING/LANDED while degraded and permits RUNNING/GATING/BLOCKED/CANCELLED — a correction is not an advance, and halting must never be blocked. It lives in rebuild.ts, deliberately not in state/: an observability fact is not an input to the 24-edge matrix, and this is a hold on a legal transition rather than a new rejection class. After the rebuild the flag is clear (a rebuilt projection is a healthy one; the flag is current health, the journaled notice is history and survives), and the corrupt file is retained with an identical digest.

One T7 contract clarified. ProjectionOutcome.degraded now means "this session's observability is degraded" and is true on every failure; the separate flagPersisted says whether sessions.observability_degraded could actually be written. The distinction is load-bearing here: a file corrupt enough to swallow its own flag column is exactly the case the plan cares about, and the degraded-mode suite asserts flagPersisted === false throughout — which is why the journaled notice, not the row, is the durable flag. sqlite.ts gained integrityProblems() and closeDatabase(), keeping every .exec/.prepare call rebuild needs behind the driver boundary.

Scope left honestly open. Nothing in core/src calls assertAdvancementPermitted(), observabilityDegraded(), recoverAttempt(), reclaimLock(), or rebuildDatabase() yet — outside their defining modules there are zero callers, so G2's guarantee is proven in simulation and not yet enforced on any production path. That is expected rather than a defect: the layer that persists a transition is the one T6's and T7's Amendments both deferred, and it arrives with the workflow runner (M5) and the owner CLI (M6); recovery-on-startup and reclaimLock() arrive with the same runner and with awsf doctor (T22); rebuildDatabase() arrives as awsf db rebuild (T22). It is recorded here because the failure mode is silent — a hold nothing calls breaks nothing, and would be discovered only by a task completing invisibly, which is the exact defect this milestone exists to make impossible. rebuildDatabase() takes each attempt's identity from its caller rather than parsing status.json, because that document's schema belongs to T22's CLI and not to this module; discoverAttempts() finds the attempt tree structurally, reading no file's contents. The simulation supplies identity from the attempt path, so nothing outside the attempt directory is needed to rebuild everything a dashboard displays. Also flagged: for sessions whose events interleaved in real time, identity rests on recorded_at ordering, which is exact at millisecond resolution and would tie-break deterministically (by session, then source_seq) in the sub-millisecond case.

Results. npm run test:sim is 29/29 green in ~15s (16 crash-injection, 5 rebuild-identity, 6 degraded-mode; 9 real child-process kills). npm run test:unit is 352/352 green — unregressed, the count unchanged from T7. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across 74 files. npm run typecheck goes 146 → 191 errors, all inside the eight pre-existing classes and no new class (confirmed against a git stash -u baseline; the additions are the same @types/node gap every node:-importing file already produces). specs/tickets/T08.md flipped to done in the same pass. T8's four boxes and the M2 header are [x]: every box under M2 is now checked. G2 is satisfied — crash recovery reconstructs-or-blocks at every write-protocol boundary, so the M3 barrier is cleared to release a provider. Committed as d17389a. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-07 — Milestone 3 / Task 9 complete

Entry verified before any code. npm run test:unit 352/352 and npm run test:sim 29/29, both run green against bd87590 first — G1 and G2 confirmed by execution, not by reading the markers.

The split with state/tiers.ts is the design. T5 already owns the arithmetic — what a ceiling is (ceilingFor), what a composite costs (compositeCost), whether a number fits (fitsCeiling), whether a workflow's minimum fits its tier (assertWorkflowFitsTier). core/src/execution/call-budget.ts deliberately re-implements none of it and owns the ledger instead: the mutable, task-lifetime account that arithmetic is applied to, and the only place a call is ever committed. CallCeilingExceeded is imported from state/errors.ts rather than reinvented, so a budget refusal reads identically whether it came from the pure machine or the ledger.

Why reserve() is synchronous, and why a test says so. Node runs one turn at a time, so the hazard the reservation exists to close is not a data race — it is an interleaving: a launch reads the budget, awaits the barrier, and commits against a number that has since moved. reserve() therefore performs the fitsCeiling check and the commit in one turn with no await between them; making it async, or awaiting anything inside it, would silently reopen the hole. That is a property of the source that no state assertion can see, so a unit test pins it directly (reserve and settle must not be AsyncFunctions) and the file header states the constraint for whoever edits it next.

The property suite is falsified, not merely passing. Sixteen seeded storms (mulberry32, seeds fixed in a constant list so a failure is reproducible) × three tiers, 2–40 concurrent launches each, every launch yielding the loop at least twice — once between reserving and settling — with random costs, a 35% registration-failure rate, and partial composite landings. The invariant is asserted at every observable moment (after each reserve, mid-launch, after each settlement), not only at rest: a ledger that overshoots and corrects itself before the storm settles has still overshot. Two mutants were injected to prove the suite is load-bearing: (1) committed ignoring callsReserved — 6/6 property tests fail; (2) the commit deferred one microtask past the check, i.e. the exact check-then-await hole — 5/6 fail. One test exists solely to prove the storms are not vacuous: it asserts that with no releases, exactly 3 of 40 T1 attempts are admitted and 37 refused.

Composite settlement is a three-way, not a two-way. The plan names two outcomes — spend on GO, release on registration failure — and for a single call that is complete. For a composite it is not: two workers can reach GO while the fuser's registration fails, and both whole-unit outcomes would then lie. settle(id, spent) is therefore the primitive and spendOnGo/releaseOnRegistrationFailure are its endpoints; spent plus the released remainder always equals the declared cost, so a reservation never evaporates and never inflates. The composite still reserves as ONE indivisible declaration (reserveComposite(2) → one reservation of 3, not three of 1) — reserving one at a time is precisely the failure the advance declaration exists to prevent, and a test asserts a single-call reservation fitting is not evidence the composite fits.

Corrections are budget-neutral by construction, not by convention. recordPhaseTransition() never calls reserve or settle — it cannot touch the call ledger — and it draws the phase allowance instead. authorize() is the single task-level entry point: it runs transition() against the live snapshot and reserves exactly result.spends.calls, so GATING → RUNNING reserving one call and DRAFT → PREPARED reserving none are the same mechanism rather than two conventions. A task with its ceiling fully spent is proven still able to correct in-session; a refused edge is proven to draw no allowance either.

Two boundaries the plan implies but does not spell out. The correction allowance is per phase and the ceiling is per task, so beginPhase() refreshes the tranches and provably leaves the call columns alone — a five-phase workflow gets five allowances and exactly one ceiling. And beginAttempt(n) refuses while any reservation is held (ReservationOutstanding) rather than guessing: releasing would forgive a call that may have been spent, spending would bill one that may never have launched, and neither is knowable in memory. That is the same reconstruct-or-block posture M2 took, applied to money.

The workflow-fit hook is a free function. admitWorkflow(workflow, tier, committed) is exported standalone so core/src/workflow/compiler.ts (T20, M5) can call it before any ledger exists for the run; CallBudget#admitWorkflow() binds it to the live tier and committed total. It adds one refusal beyond assertWorkflowFitsTier's tier rule, in the same error class: a retry of a T1 task with 2 calls behind it cannot admit a 3-call workflow even though T1's ceiling would have fitted it at attempt 1.

Scope left honestly open. Nothing outside its own tests imports call-budget.ts yet — the launcher barrier that will call spendOnGo on GO and releaseOnRegistrationFailure on a failed registration is T10, and the compiler that will call admitWorkflow is T20. The T9 Stop-When line says the workflow-fit rejection is "wired to the compiler"; the compiler does not exist in M3, so what shipped is the hook it will call, exported in the shape the compiler needs, and this is recorded rather than claimed as wiring. Reservations are deliberately not rehydratable from CallBudgetOptions.carried (spend and correction counters are): a reservation is in-flight state, and a host that died holding one leaves the journal to reconcile, not a counter to restore. Nothing here writes to the journal or the sessions row either — snapshot() returns exactly the four DDL columns plus the allowance, and the layer that persists them is the same one T6/T7/T8 all deferred to M5/M6.

Results. npm run test:unit is 391/391 green (was 352; thirty-nine new: 33 ledger unit tests, 6 property tests). npm run test:sim is 29/29 green — unregressed. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across 77 files. npm run typecheck goes 191 → 196 errors, all five inside the pre-existing repo-wide @types/node module-resolution class and no new class (confirmed against a git stash -u baseline); zero come from core/src/. specs/tickets/T09.md flipped to done in the same pass. T9's two boxes are [x] and the M3 header moves to [wip] — not [x]: T10 (broker, barrier, stub adapter), T11 (process controller and the three ports) and T12 (stream layer) are outstanding, G3 stays armed, and no live provider probe is authorized. 2026-08-07 — M3 / Task 10 complete: the launcher barrier, the broker, and the stub adapter

Entry gate, verified before any code (G1). T9's two boxes were already [x]; the T4/T5 transition suite was run explicitly and is 115/115 green (24 accepts, 76 typed rejections, the eleven-step order, actors, ceilings, evidence, the phase submachine), with npm run test:unit at 391/391 and npm run test:sim at 29/29 as the pre-change baseline. The barrier is only worth building on a matrix that is already proven, and this is that check having actually run.

What the barrier is, as five named steps. core/src/execution/launcher-barrier.ts is platform-neutral and pure — it never spawns, signals, or touches a file — and it sequences launched → identified → registered → spent → released. BARRIER_STEPS is exported and the simulation sweeps it, so the suite cannot drift into killing a protocol the implementation no longer runs (the same discipline WRITE_PROTOCOL_STEPS introduced in T6). Two orderings inside it are load-bearing and both are stated in the source and pinned by tests. First, register precedes the release token — that is the barrier itself: a provider process can only exist downstream of a durable record of it. Second, spendOnGo precedes the release token rather than following it. The plan's diagram draws "GO · reservation → spent" on one arrow, but the two failure modes are not symmetric: dying between spend and GO over-counts by one call, which is conservative; dying between GO and spend lets a provider run for free, which is a ceiling that does not hold. The safe decomposition is the one that shipped, and the refund rule is stated once and applied everywhere — a call that provably never ran is returned; a call that may have run is billed. A release() that throws after the spend therefore keeps the call and still destroys the tree, because the host cannot know whether the token reached the child.

The kill-host proof, as a matrix with a control. core/test/simulation/launcher-barrier.test.ts spawns a real host (_barrier-host.ts) that drives the real broker against a real provider executable and SIGKILLs itself from inside the barrier's own step hook. At each of the four boundaries before the token — launched, identified, registered, spent — the assertions are that nothing from that run is alive anywhere in /proc and that the stub's side-effect file was never created, re-checked after a settling window so a slow starter cannot slip past. The mechanism is that the gated child blocks on a readSync of fd4; when the host dies the kernel closes the pipe, the read returns zero bytes, and the child exits 65 without ever exec'ing. The question this matrix cannot answer on its own is whether the absence of a file means anything, so a fifth case kills the host immediately after released and asserts the side-effect file does appear — without that control, "no provider ran" would be a claim the suite could satisfy by never launching anything. Registration failure is proved separately end to end: the tree is terminated (terminated: true, enumerated survivors: []), the reservation comes back released with callsSpent 0 and committed 0, no status.json was written, and the provider never ran.

process_start_identity, and why execve is what makes it work. The launcher reports <boot-id>:<pid>:<starttime-jiffies> (field 22 of /proc/self/stat, with the boot id in front because jiffies-since-boot repeat across reboots). execve replaces the image, not the task, so the identity computed before the exec is still the provider's identity after it — the simulation asserts exactly that, comparing the registered identity against what the provider writes about itself, and finding the same pid, the same pgid, and the same start time. The field is nullable with a startIdentitySource that explains a null, so a platform without a reader says so instead of offering a PID dressed up as an identity; sameProcess() fails closed on a null, a missing process, or a changed identity, so "I am not sure" is never the same answer as "yes".

Spawn-site enforcement is proved by the absence of a child, not by a thrown error. The broker's five sites are derived from the L-table's ✦ column (LEGAL_EDGES.filter(e => e.spawnSite)) rather than restated, and a test pins the derivation to L4/L10/L11/L16/L19. The sweep points the broker at a launcher path that writes a marker file when it runs, attempts a launch at all 95 ordered pairs that are not spawn sites, asserts IllegalSpawnSite on every one, and then asserts the marker does not exist — a rejection that arrives after spawn() has already returned has already failed. Two complaints are kept distinct on purpose: an illegal site raises IllegalSpawnSite, while a registration that misnames its own edge ("GATING → RUNNING is L10, but the registration declares L4") raises SpawnRegistrationInvalid — sharing one message would have made a caller typo read as an architectural violation. The same pre-flight refuses a missing reservation id, a runtime shell that is not false, a command line where an argv array belongs, an unresolvable executable, and a prompt found in argv.

Three placements worth recording, because two of them are early. (1) core/src/adapters/interface.ts is in the File Manifest under T13 but is created here — the stub adapter needs ProcessSpec/ModelInfo/HarnessAdapter/TransportBroker to exist, and inventing a private shape for T13 to replace would have been worse. (2) core/src/execution/platform/posix.ts is in the manifest under T11 and now holds the Linux enumeration and the TERM→grace→KILL ladder. It began inside the broker and was moved when the advisory LOC budget flagged the file at 468 lines — the warning was correct that the broker was doing two jobs, and the plan already names the second one's address. T11 is not thereby done: the ProcessSupervisor interface, the darwin and win32 ports, the single contract suite run against all three, the forced-enumeration-failure case, and the liveness/silence monitor are all still outstanding. (3) The stub provider executable lives at core/test/fixtures/providers/stub/stub-provider.mjs, not in core/src/, for a concrete reason: its grandchild-spawner script must spawn, and node:child_process is importable nowhere in core/src except the broker. A stub that needed an exemption from that fence would weaken the invariant it exists to help test. core/src/adapters/stub.ts therefore takes providerPath as a required option with no default, so core/src never reaches into the test tree.

Signalling refuses twice before it signals once. terminateGroup() will not send a signal it cannot prove is aimed at its own tree. If the PID now carries a different start identity, no signal is sent at all: Linux keeps a struct pid alive while anything references it as a process group, so a PID number cannot be reused while its group still has members — a changed identity therefore proves an empty group, and kill(-pgid) would have reached a stranger's. If the group enumerates empty, nothing is sent either; enumerating first is also what makes the ladder safe once the leader has exited, since a non-empty group proves the pgid number is still the one we were given. The grace period is polled rather than slept, so "up to 2 s" becomes "as long as it actually needs". The residual race — the last member exiting between the enumeration and the signal — is not closeable with the APIs Node exposes; pidfd_send_signal is what would close it, and that is recorded in the source as a T11/T27 platform-port question rather than papered over. Enumeration throws EnumerationUnavailable off Linux instead of returning [], because an empty list is a claim that everything died.

The stub is scriptable in the eight ways the ugly cases require — success, timeout (noisy and endless, so it trips a hard timeout and must not trip the silence window), silence (silent and endless), overload, malformed, model-line, no-model-line, grandchild-spawner. The script is the model (stub/<script>), so an unknown one fails closed with E_MODEL_UNRESOLVED before a spec exists rather than resolving to something near it. buildSpec() is pure and asserted byte-for-byte, the prompt appears only in stdin, and the provider counts the bytes it received on fd0 so the simulation can show the prompt arrived without ever entering a process table. Two small additions beyond the letter of the task: the broker passes the run id as the launcher's last argv element (never read by the launcher) so a human at a ps can tell which run a child blocked at the barrier belongs to; and a new adapter-fence meta-test asserts the other half of the contract's sentence — nothing under core/src/adapters/ imports child_process or calls global fetch — with a self-check proving both patterns match what they forbid.

One defect found by review rather than by a failing test, recorded because it would have surfaced as somebody else's bug. The handshake accumulated the child's stderr so a launcher that died before reporting could say why, and left that data listener attached afterwards. Nothing in this task noticed — T10 only ever reads stdout — but a live data handler puts a Node stream in flowing mode, so the ProcessTransport handed to T12's stream layer would have been an iterable whose opening bytes had already been consumed by a handshake that ended long before. Every listener the handshake attaches is now removed on settlement, the stub provider writes a diagnostic line to stderr on every script (real providers do, and a host that loses it loses the only explanation some failures give), and the simulation drains transport.stderr concurrently and asserts it arrives whole. The committed mode of the provider fixture is asserted too (100755 in the index): core.filemode is false on the development mount, so the working-tree bit proves nothing about what a fresh clone gets, and without the guard a lost mode bit would fail at execve three layers from its cause.

Scope left honestly open. Nothing outside the tests calls the broker yet: the process controller that will drive it is T11 and the workflow engine that will drive that is M5, so this is a barrier with a proof and no production caller, recorded rather than implied. StubAdapter.parse() ships a deliberately minimal line decoder so the barrier work has a real event stream to prove itself against; T12 still owns the LineFramer (per-instance decoders, framing past the output budget), the EventSequencer, and the output budget, and its acceptance rows are not claimed here. The timeout and silence scripts exist and are exercised as descriptors, but no monitor consumes them yet — the silence window and the record-update-failed flag are T11's. The grandchild case asserts only what T10 owes: that a child the host never spawned joins the registered process group, which is why kill(-pgid) is the right instrument. Windows PID stability is untouched: the launcher refuses with exit 67 where process.execve is unavailable, before reporting an identity, so a host never registers a process whose PID it could not have kept. Of the M3 acceptance rows, this task's mechanisms now prove the kill-host row and the architecture-fence row; the rest stay open for T11 and T12, and no acceptance row was ticked (no build session has ticked one — the plan's markers remain the source of truth).

Results. npm run test:unit is 440/440 green (was 391; forty-nine new: 17 barrier and wire protocol, 15 spawn-site and platform, 14 stub adapter, 3 adapter fence). npm run test:sim is 39/39 green (was 29; ten new, of which five are real host kills at real barrier boundaries). npm run test:journeys and npm run test:contract remain empty — no journey or contract file exists yet, and T11 is what fills the latter. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across 91 files. npm run typecheck goes 196 → 298 errors with no new class (confirmed against a git stash -u baseline): the one new error code is TS2304, every instance of which is a global @types/node would declare (AbortSignal, AbortController, TextDecoder, TextEncoder, setTimeout, clearTimeout) — the same root cause as the TS2307/TS2580/TS2584 already in the baseline. Zero errors come from core/src/ outside that gap; nine real type defects the first typecheck exposed in this task's own code were fixed rather than absorbed into the pre-existing count. Two further defects were found by review and by a test written after the code, not by a red suite: the stderr listener above, and a fault raised at the launched boundary escaping the barrier's protected block — onStep is the host's own code, and a hook that threw there would have orphaned a gated child holding a reservation nobody returned. Both are fixed and both now have a test. specs/tickets/T10.md flipped to done in the same pass. Code committed as 4161c1b.
