2026-08-07 — M3 / Task 11 complete: the process controller, the three platform ports, and the liveness monitor

Entry gate. T10's four boxes were [x] before a line was written here, and its proofs were re-run rather than assumed: npm run test:unit 440/440 and npm run test:sim 39/39, both green on the working tree as it stood. Nothing in this task is allowed to be true only because the previous task said so.

The decomposition: two questions, one ladder. A PlatformPort answers exactly two things — who is in there? (census()) and send this signal (signalGroup()) — and answers neither by guessing. Everything else lives in core/src/execution/process-controller.ts: the identity comparison, the TERM → grace → KILL → enumerate ladder, the survivor report, and the CPU sum the monitor watches. That split is deliberate and it is the opposite of the obvious one. The order of the ladder is the part that has to be right — enumerate before signalling, report from what was measured rather than from what was attempted — and three per-platform copies of it would be three chances to get that wrong in a way only one machine could reveal. The ports differ in what they can read; they do not differ in what a cancellation means.

Grouping is declared, not assumed — and the POSIX inference does not travel. Each port states a grouping: process-group for Linux and macOS, ancestry for Windows, which has no process groups and must walk parent links from the registered root. This matters at exactly one place. When a recorded PID is now held by a different process, POSIX proves the old group is empty — the kernel keeps a group alive while anything is in it, so the number could not have been recycled otherwise — and the report is {skipped: "identity-changed", survivors: [], terminated: true}. Under ancestry that proof does not exist: a recycled root cannot be walked back to the tree it used to head, so the port raises EnumerationUnavailable instead of inheriting an inference it cannot support. The ancestry walk also refuses any parent link a child could not have had (a child cannot start before its parent), and keeps a link whose start times are unknown — an over-long survivor list is loud, a short one is the lie this whole layer exists to stop.

A defect the contract suite found while it was being written. Killing a process ORPHANS its children: their parent link now points at a PID that is gone. So a ladder that re-walks the tree after the KILL loses exactly the survivors the report exists to name — on Windows, cancelling a three-deep tree and failing to kill the grandchild would have reported terminated: true with an empty survivor list while the grandchild kept running. The fix is in the ladder rather than in a port: it carries forward everyone it has ever seen in this tree, with their start identities, and re-checks each of them against every census. A PID handed out again inside the grace window is therefore not counted as something that refused to die, and a survivor stays visible after the process that connected it to the root has died. The case is now the suite's a survivor of BOTH signals is reported row, and it runs against all three ports.

Enumeration failure is an error on every port, and the ladder lets it out. census() throws EnumerationUnavailable where it cannot see — /proc unreadable or absent, ps exiting non-zero, the CIM query unrunnable, or (the case that would otherwise be invisible) no census command supplied at all. terminateTree does not convert that into a report: a report is a claim about the machine, and a supervisor that cannot see is not entitled to make one. The forced failure is asserted per port, against census, groupMembers, groupCpu, observeIdentity and the ladder, and the ladder case also asserts that nothing was signalled — a supervisor that cannot see must not be swinging either.

One contract suite, three implementations, literally. core/test/contract/process-supervisor.test.ts runs every case three times. _supervisor-world.ts owns ONE synthetic process table and renders it into whichever shape the port under test parses: /proc/<pid>/stat files in a temp directory (with a deliberately ugly comm containing spaces and brackets, because a parser that splits before dropping it reads a process name as a process group), BSD ps lines with lstart last, and pipe-separated CIM rows with FILETIME creation stamps. The parsers under test are the real ones; only the machine is fake, and a world can be made blind the way its own platform actually goes blind. The suite's last section runs the same ladder against REAL processes through this host's own port — a detached group whose leader spawns a child the host never registered — and it is not skipped by platform name: it runs wherever the host port can take a census, so T27 executes it on macOS and Windows by running the file and changing nothing.

A port may not spawn, so the census arrives as an injected capability. node:child_process is importable in exactly one module (AGENTS.md invariant 3), and a platform port asking ps or PowerShell for the process table would have needed an exemption from the fence that makes the registration guarantee mean anything. So the ports take a CommandRunner instead; the broker supplies runSystemCommand, which is the second and last thing in this repository that creates a process. It is synchronous on purpose — the ladder polls a census inside a grace period, and an enumeration that could interleave with its own next poll is a survivor list assembled from two different moments.

A silent fix for a silent failure: identities off Linux. The gated launcher reads /proc and has nothing to read anywhere else, so off Linux it honestly reports startIdentity: null — and a null is precisely what sameProcess refuses to match. Left alone, every cancellation on macOS would have been a no-op that reported success. The broker now fills the gap from its own platform port immediately after the handshake, and that is the one moment when doing so is safe: the child is still blocked on a control channel whose write end this process holds, so it cannot have exited and its PID cannot have been recycled between its report and the observation. Where no port can supply one either, the honest null survives and the ladder refuses to signal on it — reporting skipped: "identity-unverifiable" with the survivors it could actually see, which is a different claim from "it is gone".

The silence window's signature is the invariant. LivenessMonitor.noteOutput takes a byte COUNT, not a string and not an event. The window is specified to trip on process/output activity only — never conversational markers — and the cheapest way to keep that true forever is to make the content unavailable to the class: there is no parameter through which a milestone line, a thinking token or a heartbeat message could arrive and be mistaken for progress. A provider that talks without working is silent as far as this file is concerned, and one that works without talking stays alive because the group's CPU counter moves. A test asserts the module imports nothing from the event vocabulary at all, because that import is the door the predecessor's defect walked through. A census the monitor cannot take is counted and is not a heartbeat — fail closed, so a broken enumerator cannot keep a wedged provider alive.

Three placements, recorded because two are choices the manifest does not make. (1) The liveness monitor is named in T11's Outputs but given no filename, and it lives at core/src/execution/liveness-monitor.ts rather than inside the controller: it is a policy about time, and the controller is a mechanism about processes. (2) The PlatformPort contract began in launcher-barrier.ts beside TerminationReport and EnumerationUnavailable, and was moved into process-controller.ts when the advisory LOC budget flagged the file at 488 logical lines. The warning was right that the file had acquired a second contract; the barrier keeps what the gated child needs, the controller keeps what a port must implement, and the ports import the latter type-only so nothing cycles. (3) stub-provider.mjs's stayAlive now starts a repeating timer only for the scripts that are meant to be BUSY. A "silent" provider that woke every 50 ms still moves the process group's CPU counter, which is exactly what the silence window watches — the fixture would have been quietly disproving the thing it exists to prove.

No platform claim is recorded anywhere. The darwin and win32 ports are written on WSL2/Linux against the plan's own per-platform ladder, they compile, and they pass the same contract suite the Linux port passes against a census the suite supplies. That is the whole of the claim. Their source headers say UNVERIFIED in the first sentence, the README's platform matrix is untouched and still PENDING in every cell including Linux's, and live verification is T27, per machine. The win32 port also carries the plan's v1 position rather than working around it: there is no Job Object helper, so termination is taskkill /T, which walks the same parent links the census walks — and when it misses something, the enumeration after it reports the survivor and terminated is false. That is the design working, not a gap in it.

Scope left honestly open. Nothing in production drives any of this yet. The broker cancels through the controller, which is real wiring, but the liveness monitor has no caller: the workflow engine that will start one per run is M5, and the stream layer that will feed noteOutput with the bytes it already frames is T12. The monitor's silenceMs, timeoutMs and graceMs are constructor options whose defaults match awsf.config.yaml; the code that reads that file and passes them in does not exist, and a unit test pins the default against the committed value so the two cannot drift apart unnoticed while it is being written. The hard timeout is a monitor input, not a policy — which per-phase ceiling supplies it is M5's. The residual signalling race (the last member exiting between the enumeration and the signal) is not closeable with the APIs Node exposes; pidfd_send_signal would close it on Linux and that stays recorded in the source as a T27 question rather than papered over. Of M3's acceptance rows, this task's mechanisms now prove the grandchild-cancellation row, the enumeration-failure row and the silence-window row; no acceptance row was ticked, since no build session ticks them and the plan's markers remain the source of truth.

Results. npm run test:contract is 45/45 green — the suite did not exist before this task, and thirty-nine of those are one contract case run three times. npm run test:sim is 46/46 green (was 39; seven new: three real grandchild cancellations, four liveness trips against real providers). npm run test:unit is 452/452 green (was 440; twelve new liveness-monitor tests on an injected clock, because a 2700-second window is not something a suite can sit through and a suite that shrinks it is measuring a different monitor). npm run test:journeys remains empty. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across 101 files, and the advisory LOC budget no longer flags any file in core/src. npm run typecheck goes 298 → 333 errors with no new class (confirmed against a git stash -u baseline): every one is the pre-existing repo-wide @types/node-outside-D2 gap, and zero come from core/src/ outside it. specs/tickets/T11.md flipped to done in the same pass. T11's three boxes are [x], and two of task 12's Testing-Strategy boxes — the grandchild/enumeration-failure row and the contract-suite row — are [x] because they describe this task's work; the three that describe T10's kill-host proof and T12's stream layer are untouched. The M3 header stays [wip]: T12 is outstanding, G3 stays armed, and no live provider probe is authorized. Code committed as ac407d2. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-07 — M3 / Task 12 complete: the stream layer, and M3 with it — G3 is satisfied

Entry gate. T10 is [x], which is what T12's Entry names, and T11 was [x] too. Both were re-run rather than assumed before a line was written: npm run test:unit 452/452, npm run test:contract 45/45, npm run test:sim 46/46, all green on the tree as it stood.

Three stages, and two rules that turned out to be their own files. core/src/adapters/stream/ holds line-framer.ts, output-budget.ts and event-sequencer.ts as the manifest names them, plus usage.ts and model-identity.ts. The last two were extractions, not inventions: the advisory LOC budget flagged the sequencer, and it was right twice. Normalizing a provider's usage report is about one event's payload while the sequencer is about order; and "what the host is willing to record as the model that answered" is a rule T13 and T14 both need and neither should get its own opinion about. After both, the sequencer still warns at 517 logical lines against a 450 threshold — 425 of them code — and it is recorded rather than split again: what is left is one class doing one job, and a third cut would have traded cohesion for a line count in a suite that warns and never fails.

The framer is a literal port, and the attribution is on the file. LineFramer, its per-instance streaming decoder, flush, frameLines and NO_LINES are carried from fusion-harness/core/adapters.ts:98-143, and truncateToCodePoint from :81-96, both under an MIT header naming © 2026 IndyDevDan — which is what Q9's resolution asks for at exactly this address. Carried rather than re-derived because a re-derivation would have been a second, subtly different copy of a boundary condition nobody wants two opinions about. AWSF's own additions are a byte counter and hasPending. The decoder is per instance and that is the whole of the invariant: the suite proves it by holding two framers mid-code-point simultaneously and interleaving their chunks, which is the shape of the bug a module-level decoder produces and which no single-framer test can see. A second test frames the same stream at every chunk size from one byte upward and asserts the lines never change — the property is that a run must not depend on where the kernel happened to cut the pipe, and the interesting cut sizes are exactly the ones a hand-picked case misses.

The budget's real specification is what it may NOT refuse. OutputBudget carries both dimensions of awsf.config.yaml § runtime, pinned to that file by a test so the two cannot drift while the loader that will pass them in is still being written (M5's). It can refuse provider chatter; it can never refuse run.started, a settlement a tool call already obliged, or a terminal — those are charged unconditionally and reserved out of the cap in advance, so a run that overruns still ends. A budget that could drop a terminal would convert "this run said too much" into "this run never ended", which is strictly worse and silent, and it is the defect this milestone exists to remove. The framer's counterpart rule is the same one from the other side: framing bounds nothing and keeps reading past the cap, so the terminal is still there to be observed. One slot is held back for the truncation notice so refusing an event never has to overrun the cap to explain the refusal; at the two-event floor even that has nowhere to go, and the cap wins — truncation goes unannounced rather than overrunning a budget the caller asked for, and a test pins that case rather than leaving it to be discovered.

Tool settlement is explicit on every path, not only on the one the plan names. Cancellation settles every open call cancelled first, before the terminal — that is the case the twelve-event list calls out. But a terminal arriving over an open call on any path is the same defect, so complete() and fail() settle too, with outcome: "error" and a snippet saying the terminal arrived while the call was open. That is a real settlement in the stream, visible in order, and not the thing the invariant forbids: what "a terminal never closes tool calls implicitly" rules out is a consumer having to infer the closure, and an explicit error settlement is the opposite of an inference. A settlement for a call the host never opened does not become an unpaired tool.completed — validateEventSequence would read that as tool-settled-without-request and would be right — it becomes an unknown-provider-event notice.

Fail-closed model identity, and the second provenance wired rather than described. An identity the harness cannot represent — empty, a sentence where an identifier belonged, 200 characters — fails the run with E_MODEL_UNRESOLVED instead of resolving to something near it. So does a run that reaches run.completed having never named a model at all, which is what the no-model-line stub script has existed for since T10 without anything consuming it. The escape is deliberate and narrow: a caller may supply an attributedModel, and then a run whose stream never spoke resolves to it with provenance: "route-attributed" — the plan's own second provenance, so the UI can distinguish an inference from a confirmation instead of being handed one shape for both. Absent that, fail-closed is the default, and the stub supplies nothing. A run that names two different models fails E_MODEL_MISMATCH; naming the same one twice is a repeat, not a second event. E_MODEL_MISMATCH had no producer before this task.

All six notice codes now have a producer. non-json-output and unknown-provider-event from the adapter's decoder, output-truncated from the budget, malformed-usage from usage normalization, clock-skew from the sequencer, and sqlite-projection-failed from the projector since T7. Clock skew is emitted once per run, not once per line: a provider with a broken clock stamps everything, and a notice per line would bury the stream it is describing. It fires on an unreadable timestamp as well as a backwards one, and in neither case does anything reorder — seq is host-minted and never derived from providerAt, which is retained and displayed exactly as sent.

One interface change, made because the alternative was a lie. HarnessAdapter.parse gains an optional AbortSignal. The reason is a fact the live suite established rather than a preference: killing a process group closes its pipes cleanly, so a cancelled run reaches EOF looking exactly like a provider that stopped talking, and the first version of the live test proved it by producing E_TERMINAL_MISSING for a cancellation the suite had just performed. The stream cannot tell them apart and never will; only the host that cancelled knows. With the signal, a run whose stream ends under an abort settles as run.cancelled carrying the caller's own reason — and its open tool calls are settled first. Without one, a stream that stops without a terminal is still a failure, which is the right default because that is what it is. execute() passes through the signal it already holds.

The stub adapter is rewired onto all three stages, so nothing here is a library with only a test for a caller. Its own contribution is now the middle stage and nothing else — decoding its line vocabulary — and every run-level invariant belongs to the sequencer, which is the point: no adapter can get one of them subtly wrong on its own, and T13 and T14 inherit them rather than re-implementing them. One existing test changed as a consequence and is called out rather than quietly fixed: the chunk-boundary case had no model line and now fails closed, so a model line was added to keep that test about framing instead of letting it become a second copy of the fail-closed rule, which has its own test.

Scope left honestly open. The liveness monitor still has no production caller: LineFramer.bytes exists as the hook noteOutput wants and nothing calls it, because the thing that owns a monitor per run is M5's workflow engine. attributedModel ships with no configured user — the stub deliberately supplies none, and the adapters that will are T13 and T14. frameLines has no caller in core/src either; it is part of the port and the entry point M4's fixture replay will use. Nothing here writes to the journal or the projector: the sequencer returns events and the layer that persists them is the same one T6 through T11 all deferred.

Results. npm run test:unit is 510/510 green (was 452; fifty-eight new: 13 framer, 13 output budget, 26 sequencer, 6 stub-level integration). npm run test:sim is 49/49 green (was 46; three new against real provider processes, including a run cancelled while its stream is being read). npm run test:contract is 45/45 green — unregressed. npm run test:journeys remains empty. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across 110 files. npm run typecheck goes 333 → 353 with no new class: every error code appearing in this task's files (TS2304, TS2307, TS2339) already appears in files it did not touch, all of it the pre-existing @types/node-outside-D2 gap. Eight narrowing errors this task's own tests introduced were fixed rather than absorbed into that count — assert.ok cannot narrow while node:assert does not resolve, so the suites use the inline discriminant pattern the rest of the repository already uses. specs/tickets/T12.md flipped to done in the same pass. Code committed as 9c7bad3. T12's three remaining boxes are [x] and the M3 header is [x]: every box under M3 is now checked, including the kill-host row, whose proof is T10's and was re-run green here. G3 is satisfied — the stub adapter and the process simulations are green, so a live provider probe is authorized for the first time in this plan, and M4 may begin. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-07 — Recorded, not fixed: model-fusion-dual-harness is the factory's first external work target

The priority, written down so a later session cannot quietly reorder it. AWSF is finished first; the model-fusion-dual-harness defects below are worked with the finished factory, not alongside it. Nothing in M1–M8 depends on that repository being fixed, no task is added here, and no marker moves — this entry exists so the intent survives the session that formed it, and so a future session reading the plan does not mistake "Source C" for "something we are also maintaining".

Three relationships, kept distinct, because blurring them is the easy mistake. (1) Design source — the plan's Source C, read-only, and the origin of AWSF's twelve event kinds, its LineFramer (literally ported with attribution in T12) and the null ≠ 0 rule. This is the only live relationship today. (2) Worker route — awsf.config.yaml already carries fusion: { kind: composite-fusion, enabled: false } and the DDL carries transport: 'composite', and Q&A decided the fusion adapter is v1.1 with only its contract shipping in v1 (the call-budget's three-reservation rule). The slot is reserved; the adapter is not written. (3) Work target — a repository AWSF is pointed at, which is post-v1 and is what this entry records. All three are about the same directory and none of them implies the others.

The assumption that would otherwise waste a milestone. AWSF gives observability to runs AWSF itself launches: bytes enter the pipeline through the broker's transport, and the journal is written by the host that started the process. M7's dashboard will therefore not show a model-fusion-dual-harness session that model-fusion-dual-harness started on its own. Making those two sessions observable needs one of exactly two things — the composite-fusion adapter of relationship (2), so AWSF is the launcher; or porting AWSF's pipeline into that repository as ordinary work. Recorded now because the wrong assumption is cheap to hold and expensive to discover at M7.

The truncation finding, with the line between read and proven drawn explicitly. That repository has two different byte ceilings on two different paths, and the pair matches the reported symptom — answers stored whole, answers truncated when displayed. Storage: core/limits.ts:14, MAX_PROVIDER_ANSWER_BYTES = 64 MB, used at fusion-harness.ts:667. Display: core/limits.ts:16, MAX_PUBLIC_PANEL_BYTES = 100_000, with the same figure as DEFAULT_PUBLIC_OUTPUT_BYTES at core/rendering.ts:13, applied at core/rendering.ts:309 and fusion-harness.ts:446. A second candidate sits on the same call as the first: fusion-harness.ts:667 also sets maxEvents: 4096, and the event cap truncates independently of the byte cap — a run that trips it emits an output-truncated notice, so the presence of that notice tells the two apart. What was done: the constants and their call sites were read. What was not done: the harness was not run and the truncation was not reproduced. These are places to look first, not a diagnosis, and a future session must re-establish the cause rather than inherit it.

Where this becomes work, and why it is not a task here. M9 (T31–T34) builds the intake machinery — the Ticket contract, the file-backed store, the intake recipe, awsf backlog — and it is where a defect in another repository properly becomes a typed, dependency-ordered ticket. It is deliberately not added to this plan's thirty-four now: those tasks are AWSF's own build, and admitting another project's defects into them would make "M3 is [x]" and eventually "v1 is complete" untrue statements about the factory. The right shape is a ticket after T31/T32 exist, against a factory that has already landed M8's two pilots.

Provenance, since T12 now carries code from there. The local fusion-harness/ checkout is the owner's fork model-fusion-dual-harness (37 of its 38 commits are the owner's), tracking upstream disler/fusion-harness, MIT © 2026 IndyDevDan. The MIT notice is why core/src/adapters/stream/line-framer.ts and output-budget.ts carry attribution headers naming the file and line range they were carried from: MIT permits the copy and requires the notice to travel with it. Everything else in AWSF that mentions that repository cites it without carrying text, and owes no header.

2026-08-07 — M4 / Task 13 complete: the Claude Code adapter, and the first live provider call this repository has ever made

Entry gate, re-run rather than assumed. T13's Entry is "M3 is [x] (G3)", and G3 is the gate that authorizes a live probe at all — so it was verified before a line was written, not after. npm run test:sim came back 48/49 on the very first run of the session and 49/49 on the thirteen consecutive runs after it — eight of those thirteen were a deliberate hunt, run later the same day specifically to reproduce the failure, and none did. M3 is green and G3 is satisfied. The flake is recorded rather than waved off, and with what is actually known about it rather than a shrug, so that nobody repeats this hunt from scratch:

  • It is not identified. The failing case did not name itself in the summary output, and the run was not captured to a file at the time. Every run since has passed, so there has been nothing to grep.
  • It is bounded to tests 1–47. The failing run's tail shows subtests 48 ("a provider killed mid-line loses no bytes") and 49 ("a real pipe's chunk boundaries do not change the run") both ok, so whatever failed is earlier in the file order.
  • It looks like timing, not logic. The failing run took 126.2 s; every clean run since has been 102–108 s. The one failure happened on a run roughly 20% slower than baseline, on the session's first invocation against a cold WSL2 filesystem cache — and this suite is full of grace periods, silence windows and TERM→grace→KILL ladders, which is exactly the class of test that fails when the machine is slow rather than when the code is wrong.

That is a classification, not a diagnosis, and it does not clear the suite: an intermittent test that only fails under load is still a test that will fail in CI on a bad day. Pinning it down means running the suite under artificial load until it trips, which is open-ended and was not worth today. It is a real finding for T27 or for whoever next touches core/test/simulation/, and the cheap first move for them is to capture every run to a file so the next occurrence names itself.

The descriptor is pinned to the plan's table and reconciled against the real CLI. buildSpec emits --print --output-format stream-json --include-partial-messages --verbose --model <m> --no-session-persistence --effort <lvl> --append-system-prompt-file <f> plus the profile's permission flags, in that order, and the descriptor test asserts the whole array rather than membership — a test that accepted any permutation would accept an argv nobody reviewed. Every flag was checked against claude --help on 2.1.224, and one of them is not in the help output at all: --append-system-prompt-file is undocumented but real. It was confirmed at zero quota cost by running the full argv with a deliberately nonexistent prompt file, which makes the CLI fail on the missing file after parsing every flag and before any API call. All three tool profiles were confirmed the same way. This technique is worth keeping: it verifies a launch surface without spending a call.

ONE live call, and what it was shaped to buy. The probe was a single sonnet run at --effort low with the read-only profile, prompted on stdin to glob a three-file throwaway directory and answer with a count. It was shaped to exercise the entire vocabulary in one call, and it did: system/init, system/status, rate_limit_event, seven stream_event kinds, two assistant messages, the synthetic user turn carrying a tool_result, and result. 11 087 bytes on stdout, nothing on stderr, exit 0. The capture script is committed at core/test/fixtures/providers/claude/capture-probe.ts and it does not hard-code an argv — it calls buildSpec() and spawns what that returns, so the argv under test and the argv that produced the bytes are the same argv by construction, not by a promise in a comment.

The parser was written after the capture, never before it. That ordering is the fixture-integrity rule in practice, and it changed the design in three places a documentation-first parser would have got wrong. (1) --include-partial-messages means text arrives twice — once as text_delta and again assembled in the assistant message — so the assembled blocks are deliberately NOT re-emitted, or every answer would appear twice in the trace. (2) The CLI's result reports a modelUsage map naming claude-haiku-4-5 alongside the claude-sonnet-5 that answered, because it issues internal sub-requests; identity is therefore read from system/init alone, since cross-checking against assistant.message.model would risk E_MODEL_MISMATCH on a run that never changed models. (3) Every message_delta carries its own usage block, and this two-turn run made two API calls to answer one prompt — so usage is emitted once, from the terminal, because summing them would double-count.

Quota came back as captured evidence rather than an assumption. The single biggest thing the probe bought was unplanned: the CLI volunteers a rate_limit_event on every run, carrying {status, resetsAt, rateLimitType} — a real five-hour window with a real epoch-seconds reset. So the quota path is grounded in bytes, not in a guess about an error string. It is emitted as a quota event always, exhausted or not, because "four hours left on the five-hour window" is the fact the owner needs before a run rather than after one. A status the CLI does not call allowed additionally ends the run with E_QUOTA_EXHAUSTED carrying the reset. What is still not captured is an exhausted window, because this subscription was not exhausted — so the parser treats anything not allowed as blocking, which is correct whatever the exhausted vocabulary turns out to be, and the derived fixture says so in writing.

Four fixtures, and the line between captured and derived is drawn in a file and enforced by a test. PROVENANCE.md names the exact command, the argv, the prompt, the cwd, and the byte count, then states for each sibling fixture exactly which bytes were substituted. probe-readonly-tool.jsonl is verbatim, including this machine's home directory in plugins[].path — editing it would make every other claim on that page unverifiable, and a home directory is not a credential (apiKeySource is "none"). The empty stderr file is committed because it is empty. probe-readonly-tool.cancelled.jsonl is a byte PREFIX cut mid-line, and a test asserts capture.startsWith(prefix), so that fixture cannot drift into fiction. The two quota fixtures carry one and two stated substitutions respectively; the second one's limit-message text is representative, not captured, following the reviewed reading in my-agentic-workflow/src/providers/claude-code.mjs:14-15, and what the suite proves there is the host's mapping and not the provider's wording.

Same-session continuity: what was proven, and what was deliberately not built. The parser reports the provider session_id and the resolved model, and assertSameSession refuses a "correction" that answered in a different session (E_BACKEND_FAILURE) or on a different model (E_MODEL_MISMATCH) — no new error code was invented for either. No correction transport was built, and the reason is a real constraint worth recording: the plan pins --no-session-persistence, which forecloses --resume entirely, so a same-session correction has to be a second send into a still-live process — and ProcessSpec.stdin is a single string written once, which cannot express a second turn. The candidate flag is --input-format stream-json (documented, and verified to parse), but its multi-turn semantics are unverified and verifying them costs a second live call, which this task's budget does not have. It is left as an explicit input to the correction loop's owner (M5) rather than shipped as an argv path nobody has run.

Three placements the manifest does not make, recorded as choices. The manifest names core/src/adapters/claude-code.ts; three files exist. env.ts holds the allowlist and the E_REDACTION value scan — a port of fusion-harness/…/launch.ts:33-62 under the MIT header Q9's resolution asks for, carried because T14 needs the identical rule and neither adapter should have its own opinion about it. system-prompt-file.ts holds the 0600 rule for the same reason, and the mode is set with an explicit chmod after the write rather than left to the open mode, because the open mode is masked by umask and a permissive umask would quietly produce a 0644 file that looked deliberate. claude-code-stream.ts is the split the advisory LOC budget asked for: the adapter had reached 756 logical lines against a 450 threshold and was visibly doing the two jobs its own header names, so decoding moved into a ClaudeStreamDecoder and the adapter kept describing, launching, and the run loop. It is now 342 and no longer flagged. cost-display.ts is the fourth, and small: formatCost IGNORES the amount for costAuthority: "unavailable" rather than merely not using it, so a caller holding the CLI's total_cost_usd estimate cannot get it displayed on a route that has already said it cannot price itself.

Scope left honestly open. Nothing in production drives this adapter yet — there is no registry mapping kind: claude-code to it (T15), no catalog to give contextWindow a real number instead of the honest null, and no workflow engine to call execute. reasoningRelation is unknown and reasoningTokens is null: Anthropic does not state whether thinking tokens sit inside output_tokens or beside them, and the terminal's usage block does not report them at all, so both stay unmeasured rather than assumed — which is exactly what T14's checklist asks for ("included-in-output for pi, unknown elsewhere"). No acceptance row was ticked; no build session ticks them.

Results. npm run test:unit is 554/554 green (was 510; 44 new). npm run test:contract 45/45 and npm run test:sim 49/49, both unchanged by this work. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across 117 files, and the advisory LOC budget flags nothing new — the two warnings it does emit are the pre-existing event-sequencer.ts one recorded in T12 and the empty dashboard/src. npm run typecheck goes 407 → 387 errors with no new class and none from the new files: every remaining one is the repo-wide @types/node-outside-D2 gap first flagged in T1. The credential sweep over core/test/fixtures/ was widened to .jsonl and .md in the same pass, because bytes nobody wrote by hand are exactly the bytes that sweep exists for. specs/tickets/T13.md flipped to done in the same commit as the markers. T13's two boxes are [x]; the M4 header moves [] → [wip] — T14 and T15 are outstanding, and one of M4's two budgeted live calls is now spent. Code committed as afc1f82. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-07 — T13's cross-building review is OUTSTANDING, deferred to the ChatGPT Plus reset; due before T15 starts

What the rule asks, and what happened instead. The Conventions' cross-building rule reads "where practical, use the opposite provider to review or implement an adapter whose assumptions concern that provider (T13 ↔ GPT session, T14 ↔ Claude session)", and T13's own build prompt is more specific still: "prefer the GPT route to implement, or have GPT review before marking done", because "stream-json parsing, identity provenance, quota-error mapping — assumptions a same-family session tends to confirm rather than test". T13 was implemented on claude:opus-5 and has had neither. This entry exists so that fact is committed rather than remembered.

Why T13's marker is still [x] and not [wip]. The task's Stop-when is met in full and was verified, not asserted: descriptor tests byte-exact on the argv, all parser tests green on CAPTURED fixtures, one bounded live probe committed and replayed, same-session continuity proven against the fixture protocol. The GPT route lives in the prompt's provider-selection preamble, not in the stop condition — so the marker is earned and the review is a separate, still-open obligation. Recording it as [wip] would misstate the work; leaving it unrecorded would lose the obligation. Hence this.

The specific risk, stated so the review can be cheap and bounded. The parser AND its 44 tests were written by the same session, so an assumption that is wrong is confirmed twice rather than caught once. Four decisions carry that exposure and are what a GPT reviewer should attack first. (1) Text de-duplication — the assembled text blocks on each assistant message are dropped as duplicates of the streamed text_deltas. That is correct only while --include-partial-messages stays pinned in the argv; a text block that never streamed would be silently lost rather than doubled, which is the worse of the two failure directions. (2) Identity from system/init alone, deliberately not cross-checked against assistant.message.model, because the CLI issues internal sub-requests on other models (the capture's own result names a claude-haiku-4-5 beside the answering claude-sonnet-5). This trades away real E_MODEL_MISMATCH detection to avoid false positives — a judgement, not a fact. (3) Usage from the terminal only, never summed across message_delta: right for this two-turn capture, but unexamined for a run whose terminal usage is absent or partial. (4) Quota's fail-safe rule — anything the CLI does not call "allowed" blocks — which is deliberately correct-whatever-the-vocabulary, but has never been run against an actually exhausted window, and whose sibling fixture carries a representative, uncaptured limit-message string.

Why it was deferred rather than done. The owner's ChatGPT Plus quota is exhausted and resets 2026-08-08. The same exhaustion blocks T14's own live probe — the pi/Codex adapter is the ChatGPT Plus route, its Stop-when requires one bounded live probe, and M4's exit budgets exactly two live calls of which only the Claude one is spent. So T14's parser cannot be honestly written today either: writing it before its capture is precisely the invented-fixture failure the fixture-integrity rule forbids, and T13 demonstrated the cost of the reverse order by having three parser decisions change once real bytes were in hand. The remainder of M4 is therefore gated on the quota reset, not on any missing work.

The deadline is T15's entry, and it is a real gate rather than a preference. T15 requires "T13 and T14 are [x]" and is where M4's exit checklist lives, so the review must land before T15 begins — after that point the catalog, the registry and the provenance plumbing are all built on top of whatever the parser assumed. Whoever runs it needs one GPT session and no live Claude call: the fixtures are committed, PROVENANCE.md states which bytes are captured and which are derived, and the whole review is a read of core/src/adapters/claude-code-stream.ts against core/test/fixtures/providers/claude/probe-readonly-tool.jsonl. If it finds a defect in the shared files — env.ts, system-prompt-file.ts, cost-display.ts — the fix lands in T14 too, since T14 imports all three unchanged. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-07 — T13 review pass: seven defects fixed, four of them same-family blind spots the suite confirmed rather than caught

Why this ran before T14 rather than after. T14 imports env.ts, system-prompt-file.ts and cost-display.ts unchanged and mirrors the decoder's shape, so a defect in any of them is one fix today and two after the second adapter lands. A local review at high effort over afc1f82..38b6236 returned twelve findings; the four sharpest were reproduced against the real decoder before any of them were believed, and seven were fixed. This does not discharge the cross-building obligation recorded in the entry above — it was still a Claude session reviewing a Claude session — but it is the part that did not need the exhausted GPT quota.

Two of the defects were contradicted by their own docstrings, which is the finding under the findings. #ensureStarted carried a comment promising "a run whose first line is anything else still started", while both non-JSON branches returned above it — so a CLI that fails to authenticate, prints prose to stdout and never emits a single JSON line produced notices and a terminal with no run.started at all. Nothing caught it: validateEventSequence checks seq contiguity, terminals and tool pairing, and has no opinion about openings. Likewise mapUsage's comment said it existed to avoid "a malformed-usage notice on every healthy run and train everyone to ignore it", and it produced exactly that on every result carrying no usage block — which is the normal shape of an error result. Both had a test asserting the surrounding behaviour and neither test looked at the case. That is what a same-family session buys: the assumption is written twice, in the code and in the test that guards it, and the docstring describing the intended behaviour is not evidence that the code has it.

Two were timestamps that were confidently wrong rather than absent. RESET_IN_MESSAGE wrote the optional s and the optional at as one alternation, so the plural form matched the s first and captured "at 2026-08-08T00:00:00Z" — a resetAt that satisfies the schema's minLength: 1, fails loudly nowhere, and returns NaN from anything that tries to Date.parse it to schedule a resume. And the epoch suffix matched up to thirteen digits while epochSecondsToIso multiplied unconditionally by a thousand, turning a millisecond stamp into +058570-10-02T00:00:00.000Z. Both are now bounded, and the range check lives in epochSecondsToIso so it also guards the rate_limit_info.resetsAt path, which no regex ever touched. The rule adopted: an unreported reset is a gap, a nonsense one is a lie, and the gap is the honest failure.

The quota rule was failing closed in the wrong direction. The first draft blocked on any rate_limit_info.status other than the exact string "allowed", reasoning that this is correct whatever the unexhausted vocabulary turns out to be. It is not: a warning-class status whose own name says the request was allowed would have ended a healthy run, and since quota is structurally never a retry, the phase is simply lost. What makes the looser rule safe is not a guess about the vocabulary — it is that the blocking decision here was never load-bearing: a genuinely exhausted window fails the API call, and #result maps that error text to the same E_QUOTA_EXHAUSTED. So allowed is now read as a prefix and everything else still blocks, keeping the unknown-status case failing closed where that is the right direction. No exhausted window has still ever been captured.

One was a platform defect that would have made Windows unusable. assertPrivateSystemPrompt tested (mode & 0o077) !== 0. On win32 — a supported platform, with a port and a %LOCALAPPDATA% state root already written — statSync().mode reflects only the read-only attribute regardless of the ACL, so that expression is unconditionally true and the function would have thrown E_REDACTION for every system prompt including the ones writeSystemPromptFile had just created: no request carrying a system prompt could ever have launched there. It is now an explicit carve-out with the reasoning in the source, and deliberately not a claim — what privacy actually holds on Windows comes from the profile directory's ACL, which this code neither sets nor reads, and per the cross-platform note that is T27's to verify on the machine rather than this session's to assert.

One is a deliberate deviation from the reviewed reference, taken with the owner's agreement. The no-tools profile now carries --disallowed-tools Bash,Write,Edit,NotebookEdit as well, where fusion-harness's builder (launch.ts:72) passes --tools "" alone. The argument is the one the read-only profile already makes, applied to the profile that needs it most: the zero-cost probe confirmed the flags parse and says nothing about how they gate, and if an empty value is ever read as "flag not set" rather than "allowlist with nothing in it", the strictest profile in this adapter silently becomes the widest one with Bash in it. One argv pair removes the assumption.

Five findings were left open on purpose, and are recorded here rather than dropped. (1) run.completed.exitCode is hardcoded 0 — the real code is on transport.exit and is never read, so a CLI that emits a success result and then exits non-zero is recorded as clean. null would be the honest value; awaiting the exit inside the read loop is the change that would make it measured, and that is a shape change to parse rather than a patch. (2) parse never consumes transport.stderr, so a provider that dies writing its reason there produces E_TERMINAL_MISSING and nothing else — real, but stub.ts does the same, so it belongs to whatever layer owns the transport loop and not to this adapter. (3) isAvailable blocks a path-shaped executable that buildSpec accepts without complaint. (4) ModelRequest.tools is declared on the shared interface and silently ignored here. (5) The decoder's tool mirror can diverge from the sequencer's when the output budget refuses a request mid-run, producing an unknown-provider-event that blames the provider for the host's own budget decision.

Results. npm run test:unit is 562/562 green (was 554; eight new, kept together under a "Regressions" heading so the shape of what a same-family review misses stays visible rather than scattered). npm run test:contract 45/45 and npm run test:sim 49/49, both untouched. npm run lint clean; npm run typecheck gains no new error class. No fixture was regenerated and no live call was spent — every case above is replayed from bytes already committed or from hand-built streams that make no claim about the provider's protocol, only about the host's handling of it. Committed as 3f353ed. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-08 — Task 14 complete: the pi/Codex adapter, and the two rules that INVERT T13's because the provider does

Order was the discipline, and it was followed literally. The descriptor tests were written and green (24/24) before any live call; the ONE bounded pi probe was spent next, banking it early against a concurrent GPT session draining the freshly-reset five-hour window; and parse() was written only after 13 399 bytes of real stdout were on disk. Nothing in the decoder was written from documentation about what pi would emit — which matters, because two of its rules would have been written backwards from T13's shape if they had been guessed.

Usage is summed, where T13 must not sum. The Claude CLI reports a running usage block on every message_delta and a true total on its terminal, so T13's decoder reads the terminal only and refuses to add. pi reports usage per turn and reports no total anywhere — agent_end carries messages and willRetry and nothing else — so the captured two-turn run (985+14 tokens, then 1018+5) is only fully described by the sum, and reading "the last one" would have understated it by half. Each turn's report is emitted as its own usage event at the moment the provider made it, rather than as one aggregate at the end; that is what makes a run cancelled after its first turn still carry that turn's measured usage instead of nothing.

Cost is money here, and formatCost's money branch finally has a caller. costAuthority: "provider" is a claim this route can back: pi computes a price per turn from the rate card it ships and reports it ($0.005345 + $0.005240 on the probe). It reaches no normalized event, because the twelve kinds carry tokens and never a price — a cost field on usage would make every route that cannot price itself look like it reported zero, which is the exact failure cost-display.ts exists to prevent. It rides the run record beside the session id, and renders through formatCost("provider", …). The pair of tests that pin this are the ones worth keeping: an unreported cost is —, and a zero pi actually quoted is $0.00.

One claim is labelled as a reviewed reading rather than a measurement, deliberately. The checklist asks for reasoningRelation: included-in-output "(its measured convention)". This capture did not measure it: the probe ran at --thinking low and every turn reported reasoning: 0, which is consistent with either relation. The value ships anyway, on two pieces of evidence from pi 0.81.1's own source — reasoning is read out of output_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens, a breakdown of the output_tokens that output is read from (openai-responses-shared.ts:364-370), and pi says it in words for the sibling API sharing the vendor's semantics ("OpenAI completion_tokens already includes reasoning_tokens", openai-completions.ts:1134). PROVENANCE.md and the decoder's own header both say which of the two it is. Three other decoded shapes are in the same position and are named there too: thinking_delta, the error/aborted stop reasons with errorMessage, and responseModel. The alternative — leaving a reasoning model's whole thinking stream to fall into the unknown-event branch — would have buried the trace it describes.

Three descriptor refusals this CLI needs and T13's does not. pi --model accepts a provider/id form, so a selector with a slash would name a provider from inside a flag that has already been told which provider to use, and --provider would lose; it also accepts an id:<thinking> shorthand, so a colon would set the thinking level from the model field and silently outrank the --thinking the agent's config put on the line. Both are refused with E_MODEL_UNRESOLVED rather than sanitized. And --append-system-prompt takes text or a path and decides with existsSync, so an unreadable file does not fail — it appends the path string itself as the system prompt. That is why assertPrivateSystemPrompt is load-bearing on this route in a way it is not on T13's, where the flag is --append-system-prompt-file and would error.

T13's no-tools deviation deliberately does NOT carry over, and the reason is in the source so nobody "fixes" the asymmetry. That deviation exists because Claude Code's narrowest profile is --tools "" and an empty value ever read as "flag not set" turns the strictest profile into the widest. pi's narrowest profile is --no-tools, a boolean flag with nothing to misread, so the reviewed argv is kept exactly. The tool names are pi's own (read,grep,find,ls, lowercase) and a test says so: shipping one CLI's vocabulary to the other narrows nothing.

Two changes reached back into T13's files, both to keep the two adapters one implementation rather than two. summarize/safeJson moved to core/src/adapters/stream/snippet.ts now that there is a second caller — the same extraction T12 made for usage.ts and model-identity.ts, for the same reason. And run.completed.exitCode is now null in both decoders instead of 0: that was the first of the five findings T13's review left open, and writing a knowingly-wrong 0 into a second decoder to preserve symmetry with the first would have been the wrong kind of consistency. The finding is not fully discharged — measuring the code means awaiting transport.exit inside the read loop, a shape change to parse — but "not reported" is now what both say instead of a number neither measured. The other four remain open for both routes.

Scope left honestly open. Nothing in production drives this adapter yet — no registry maps kind: pi-codex to it (T15), no catalog gives contextWindow a real number instead of the honest null, and no workflow engine calls execute. No correction transport exists here either, and for the same structural reason T13 recorded: the plan pins --no-session, which forecloses --continue/--resume, and ProcessSpec.stdin is a single string written once. pi does have a candidate path T13's CLI lacks — --session-id <id>, "creating it if missing", with --no-session dropped — but its behaviour is unverified and verifying it costs a second live call this task's budget does not have. It is an input to M5's correction loop, not an argv path shipped unrun. assertSameSession and the session record exist and are proven against the fixture protocol, exactly as T13's are.

The advisory LOC budget flags pi-codex-stream.ts at 497 logical lines against 450, and it is recorded rather than split. The number is worth showing because it changes the judgement: 315 of those lines are code and the rest are docstrings, where T12's still-flagged event-sequencer.ts is 517 with 425 of them code. Splitting a 315-line cohesive decoder to satisfy a metric that is counting its prose would trade cohesion for a number. The one seam that was real — payload summarizing shared with the other decoder — was taken.

Results. npm run test:unit is 630/630 green (was 562; 68 new, all in core/test/unit/adapters/pi-codex.test.ts). npm run test:contract 45/45 and npm run test:sim 49/49, both untouched by this work — and test:sim was green on the first run, with no sign of the flake recorded under T13. npm run lint clean, 0 warnings across 122 files. npm run typecheck goes 388 → 418 errors with no new error class: the code set is identical before and after, every new one is the repo-wide @types/node-outside-D2 gap first flagged in T1. The credential sweep over core/test/fixtures/ passes and the capture carries no machine path at all — the only path in it is the throwaway cwd. specs/tickets/T14.md flipped to done in the same commit as the marker. T14's box is [x]; the M4 header stays [wip] — T15 is outstanding — and both of M4's two budgeted live calls are now spent, which is the whole budget and exactly the expected spend. Code committed as ef20b3c. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-08 — T14's cross-building review is OUTSTANDING too, on the mirror-image side; both are due before T15

The rule, and what happened. The Conventions' cross-building rule pairs T13 with a GPT session and T14 with a Claude session, and T14's own build prompt names the Claude route as preferred for exactly the reason that applies here: the assumptions concern GPT/pi, so a Claude session is the one that tends to test them rather than confirm them. T14 was implemented on claude:opus-5, so the implementation half of its rule is satisfied — the first of the two adapters for which that is true.

What is still owed is the review half, and it is the same shape as T13's. A Claude session implementing a GPT-facing adapter still wrote both the decoder and the tests that guard it, which is the failure mode T13's review pass documented at length: the assumption gets written twice, and the docstring describing the intended behaviour is not evidence that the code has it. Four cases in this suite are the ones a reviewer should attack first, because they are where a same-session author would confirm rather than test: the per-turn usage sum (a rule inverted from the sibling adapter, so an inherited instinct is actively wrong here), the reasoningRelation claim the capture could not measure, the four shapes decoded from pi's source rather than from bytes, and the agent_settled-not-agent_end terminal choice, which rests on willRetry meaning what its name says.

Both reviews are due before T15 starts, for the reason already recorded for T13: T15's Entry requires T13 and T14 [x], and it is where the catalog, the registry and the provenance plumbing get built on top of whatever these two parsers assumed. Neither review needs a live call — both fixture sets are committed, both PROVENANCE.md files state which bytes are captured and which are derived, and each review is a read of one decoder against one fixture directory. A defect found in the shared files — env.ts, system-prompt-file.ts, cost-display.ts, stream/snippet.ts — now lands in two adapters at once, which is an argument for doing them together rather than one at a time. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-08 — T13's cross-building review LANDED, on the GPT route at last; nine fixes, six of them reconciled into T14 in the same pass

The obligation recorded twice above is now half discharged, and by the route the rule actually names. A GPT session read claude-code-stream.ts against its committed fixture and returned nineteen items — thirteen findings and six explicit soundness confirmations, which is itself worth noting: a review that only ever finds is a review nobody can calibrate. Four claims were reproduced against the real code and the real fixture before anything was believed, and that mattered — two of them turned on facts about the capture that neither the first pass nor its own local review had ever checked.

The finding under the findings, again, and it is the same one T13's local review named. Two of the defects were justified in their own docstrings by claims the committed fixture refutes. The decoder declined to re-read assistant.message.model "because the CLI issues internal sub-requests on other models" — but both top-level assistant messages in the probe name claude-sonnet-5, and the claude-haiku-4-5 that also ran appears only under result.modelUsage. There was no false positive to avoid; the rule simply discarded the one signal that catches a model changing under a run. And usage was read from the terminal alone "because summing would double-count" — which is true, the two message_delta reports sum to exactly the terminal's totals — but each of those deltas also carries output_tokens_details.thinking_tokens: 0, which the terminal does not carry at all. So the host recorded reasoningTokens: null for a metric the provider had reported twice: null ≠ 0 failing inside the file that exists to enforce it. The deltas are now carried rather than added, and a test pins both halves — the reasoning count arrives, and the totals stay 52 rather than 104.

One finding was a hang, not a lost message, and it is the reason this pass could not wait for T15. Neither adapter ever read transport.stderr, and the broker's own listener is removed at settlement on purpose (#handshake says so in a comment), so after launch nobody was reading it. A provider writing past the pipe buffer — 64 KiB on Linux — blocks, and a blocked child never reaches the part where it writes its result. T13's own review had recorded this as finding (2) and deferred it as "belongs to whatever layer owns the transport loop"; that layer now exists, core/src/adapters/stream/transport-loop.ts, and all three adapters use it. It drains, keeps a bounded 8 KiB tail so a provider that dies explaining itself is still quoted in the failure, and measures the exit code by holding the decoded terminal until the process exits — which closes finding (1) properly rather than at the honest interim null both decoders had reached that morning. The expiry timer is deliberately not unref'd, and the comment says why: a child that closes stdout and then hangs leaves that timer as the only thing pending, and an unref'd timer is precisely the one the event loop declines to wait for.

Six of the thirteen were in files T14 shares or mirrors, so they were fixed once. That is the whole argument for running this before T15 rather than after: the shared-file half — system-prompt-file.ts, env.ts — lands in two adapters at once, and the mirrored half would otherwise have to be found twice. The sharpest of them is the Windows carve-out. It sat above the statSync, so on win32 there was no existence check either — and on the pi route that is not a missing safeguard but a silent substitution, because --append-system-prompt takes text OR a path and decides with existsSync: a file that is not there is appended as its own path string, and the run goes out with C:\…\system-prompt.md as its system prompt with nothing anywhere saying so. Existence is now checked on every platform and the carve-out covers the mode bits alone. The other cross-cutting fix worth naming is the quota regex: a bare limit reached matched "context token limit reached", turning a full context window — which the operator fixes by sending less — into E_QUOTA_EXHAUSTED, which is structurally never a retry, so the phase was simply lost. Both routes' real refusal texts still match, on usage limit.

Two findings were trimmed rather than taken whole, and the reasoning is recorded so a later reader can disagree with it. The allowed-prefix rule was called fail-open; it is, but only for an invented status, and the reason the prefix exists — a warning-class status must not cost a phase — still holds. So the prefix stays and no longer exempts a status that also reads as exhausted, with the separators normalized first because a status is snake_case and the quota rule reads prose. And the no-tools finding is a test-wording overclaim rather than a behaviour defect: the deny list narrows four tools, not all of them, and the test should not have implied otherwise.

Left open, with reasons rather than silence. ModelRequest.tools is still ignored by both adapters — it is a policy question (does a request-level list intersect the profile, or advise it?) that belongs to T17's permission profiles, not to a parser patch. What protection a Windows ACL actually gives a system prompt in a world-readable directory is still T27's to verify on that machine; this pass fixed the part that is platform-neutral and left the platform claim unmade. The session-id consistency check and the tool-mirror divergence under budget exhaustion remain open at the severity the review assigned them.

Results. npm run test:unit is 652/652 green (was 630; 22 new, grouped under a cross-building-review heading in each adapter's suite so the shape of what a same-family session misses stays visible). npm run test:contract 45/45 and npm run test:sim 49/49, both green after the stub adapter picked up the same stderr drain — it is where every journey test runs, and the last place that should be able to hang on plumbing the production routes have fixed. npm run lint clean across 123 files. npm run typecheck 418 → 428 with no new error class. The advisory LOC budget now flags both decoders at ~503 logical lines; both are ~315 code lines with the rest docstrings, and both are recorded rather than split, for the reason already given under T14. No fixture was regenerated and no live call was spent. Committed as b65dea0.

2026-08-08 — T14's cross-building review LANDED too: BOTH obligations discharged, and it refuted two claims T14's own test names asserted

The rule is now satisfied on both adapters, and this one was run rather than waited for. A gpt-5.6-sol session, launched through the very argv this adapter builds, read pi-codex-stream.ts against the committed capture and against pi's own source, and returned eight findings plus six soundness confirmations. Four claims were reproduced before any of them were believed. Two of them were things this task had written into its own test NAMES, which is the sharpest possible form of the failure the cross-building rule exists to catch: the assumption was stated in the code, restated in the test, and restated a third time in the test's title, and all three said the same wrong thing.

The cost was not the provider's. T14 shipped costAuthority: "provider" and a test called "ChatGPT Plus measures tokens AND prices them", and rendered $0.01 as a confirmed price — the headline claim of the whole task, and the plan's own Proves row for it. It is wrong. calculateCost (packages/ai/src/models.ts) is arithmetic and nothing else: rate per million from the model's entry in pi's local model store, multiplied by the token counts. OpenAI reports the tokens and reports no charge. The figure is therefore a catalog-estimate and now renders ≈ $0.01. The irony is worth recording rather than smoothing over: T13 built cost-display.ts specifically so a subscription route could not show $0.00 as though it had been measured, and T14 then let an arithmetic estimate wear a confirmed price's clothes on the very next adapter — the one whose stated purpose was making cost authority visible.

The identity was not the stream's. T14 shipped provenance: "stream-authoritative" under a test called "the model that answered comes from the STREAM, not from the argv that asked". openai-codex-responses.ts builds the assistant message with model: model.id and provider: model.provider — pi's own local configuration, the values this adapter's argv had just supplied — and never reads response.model back off the API. responseModel, the field pi documents as the concrete model "when different from the requested model", is set only on the openai-completions path and never on this one. So the stream is the argv coming back, and the correct value is route-attributed. No fixture could have caught this: the capture names the model that was asked for, which is exactly what both readings predict. It took a reader who would go and check what the CLI does with the field.

And one live regression from the previous day's pass, reproduced before it was believed. Holding the terminal back to measure the exit code — itself a fix from the T13 review — lost the terminal entirely when the stdout iterator threw after the provider had already settled: the sequencer was terminal, so the catch path's cancel and fail both returned nothing, and the run reached its consumer with no terminal event at all. A five-line repro produced terminal-missing on the real capture. Both adapters now route every exit from the read loop through one #settleHeld, which is a method precisely because two inline copies is how the second path got forgotten. A fix from one review created the defect the next review found — which is the argument for running these before the milestone gate rather than after it.

Three more taken, briefly. continuity is now "none" on both adapters: --no-session / --no-session-persistence foreclose re-entry, so a correction through either is necessarily a cold start, and declaring otherwise would tell M5's escalation ladder that a correction costs tokens rather than a tier call — a pricing decision resting on a capability that does not exist. assertSameSession and the session record stay, because they are what makes the value re-earnable the day a correction transport is built. A bare rate limit no longer reads as exhaustion on the pi route, because pi itself disagrees: isTerminalRateLimitError exists to separate a plain 429 it RETRIES from the usage and billing forms it treats as final, and since quota is never a retry, calling a transient throttle exhaustion loses a phase to a condition that would have cleared. T13's route keeps the alternation, since its CLI has no such split — two providers, two readings, and a test on each side now says so. And the quota reset anchors when the refusal is read rather than at the terminal, because pi's "~37 min" was computed against its clock at the moment of refusal and any later anchor reports the reset late.

One finding was about this document's own honesty. The reviewer noticed that the local pi checkout is 0.80.3 while the installed CLI that produced the capture is 0.81.1, and that T14 had written both down as 0.81.1 — so every source citation in the decoder and in PROVENANCE.md was labelled with a version it had not been read from. Corrected in both places, with line numbers dropped in favour of file references where the exact line would go stale first. It is a small error and a load-bearing one: the whole value of "reviewed reading of the provider's source" is that a later reader can go and check it.

Two were declined, with their cost stated in the source rather than in a changelog. toolcall_start stays undecoded: opening a tool call there would make a mid-argument cancellation visible, but the id is all that exists at that point — the arguments arrive over the deltas that follow — so every run would trade its tool inputSummary for visibility into a rare case. And compaction usage stays unread: the review named compaction_end.result.usage, and CompactionResult in the source available here has summary, firstKeptEntryId, tokensBefore, estimatedTokensAfter and details — no usage at all. Decoding a field path that is not in the source I can read is exactly the invented-shape failure the fixture-integrity rule forbids, so the gap is recorded, in the decoder and in PROVENANCE.md, as a reason for a future capture rather than as code.

What the review CONFIRMED, which is why its findings can be trusted. Six decisions were checked against pi's source and found correct, and the first is the one this task was most worried about: the per-turn usage sum is right — pi emits one turn_end per completed assistant call, message_end carries the same message, and agent_end carries a transcript and willRetry rather than an aggregate. Also confirmed: agent_settled is the correct terminal (emitted once in _runAgentPrompt's finally, after retries and compaction, where agent_end can repeat), the three source-read event shapes are decoded correctly, the captured tool pairing and host-minted ids are correct, and the null/zero and millisecond-bounds handling holds. reasoningRelation: "included-in-output" stands as a reading of pi's mapping — with the reviewer noting, unprompted, that "the capture's zero does not prove this, as provenance correctly admits".

2026-08-08 — M4 / Task 15 complete: catalog truth, registry wiring, and the honest Antigravity slot

The distinction this task makes is deliberately one-way: a selector chooses a route; it never proves an answer. catalog.ts accepts only the committed family aliases — claude:, codex:, and the parked antigravity: — and rejects bare, unknown, or doubly-qualified selectors rather than guessing a family. It carries contextWindow: null for all three. That is not an omitted number: no current catalog evidence in this repository establishes a ceiling, and inventing one would cause a later context meter to present a limit as measured. The resulting identity is unknown until a normalized model.resolved event arrives; then its stream-authoritative or route-attributed provenance travels unchanged from decoder through the journal/projector event record. In particular, the catalog cannot turn pi's route-attributed echo into stream evidence by looking at the same selector a second time.

The registry is a boundary, not a second router. Durable config chooses an adapter id and kind; registry.ts maps the reviewed claude-code and pi-codex kinds to their reviewed implementations, passes only the configured executable name, and honors enabled: false before constructing anything. It neither reads quota nor chooses a provider from a model name — both would recreate the prohibited fallback route at a lower layer. fixture returns no production instance because its adapter requires test-owned absolute fixture paths; composite-fusion returns none because its reservation/registration shape is deferred to v1.1. Pretending either was constructible from durable config would make an empty slot look runnable.

Q1 is now enforced twice, because a warning in prose is not a launch barrier. The Antigravity adapter has no argv and no parser: isAvailable() returns blocked plus E_ADAPTER_UNVERIFIED, and every attempted build/parse/execute path raises that same closed contract code. Separately, the config schema admits an explicit verified flag and the loader rejects kind: antigravity with enabled: true unless it is exactly verified: true. The second check matters even though the adapter stays blocked today: a future implementation must not become launchable merely because somebody changes the class and overlooks the configuration policy.

What verified: true means — and does not mean. It is an operator attestation that the documented procedure happened, not a claim that this session verified the CLI. The header specifies the required sequence exactly: run one bounded agy session with a small read-only prompt; capture raw stdout and stderr without editing either; read the captured output; only then decide whether a machine-parseable stream exists and write a fixture-first parser. No agy invocation occurred here, no raw bytes were added, and the flag was deliberately not put in the committed disabled configuration. The actual unblocking change remains a future capture plus review, not flipping one boolean.

Tests target the false-confidence paths, not just construction. The catalog suite proves that only family aliases resolve, that malformed/unknown selectors do not, and that the absence of a model.resolved event is stored as unknown rather than the requested model. It also proves a disabled configured adapter has no registry result and that Antigravity reports the exact blocked code. The loader suite proves both directions of the configuration gate: enabled/unverified fails with ConfigUnverifiedAdapterError, while an explicit attestation is structurally accepted for the day a separately verified implementation exists. Existing byte-exact descriptor and prompt-in-argv meta-tests continue to cover the two real adapters; no provider fixture was regenerated.
