2026-08-13 — T30 scope amended before any call: the live tier-2 binding was never assigned to a task

Preflight for pilot 2 found that pilot 2 could not start. Driving-session preflight against the T30 build prompt, before a single provider call was reserved, established that no live tier-2 execution path exists. awsf run resolves to runProductionCommand, whose supported set is build and plan-build-test only (core/src/cli/commands/production-run.ts:68) and which rejects any attempt where status.tier !== 1 or recipe.tier !== 1 (:316); awsf rework carries the identical restriction (core/src/cli/commands/rework.ts:74,294). Both workflows T30 is permitted to drive are tier 2 by their own recipes (build-review.ts:10, simple-sdlc.ts:11). The only tier-2 route that runs at all is the stub at run.ts:69, which touches no provider and spends no call. The command would have thrown ProductionWorkflowUnsupported at the first attempt.

Three modules that exist, are tested, and are wired to nothing. core/src/workflow/review-routing.ts — oppositeProvider and runMandatoryReview, the one-retry-then-block inversion D10 requires — is imported by exactly one file, core/test/journeys/review-inversion.test.ts. core/src/gates/review.ts (verdict_consistent) and core/src/gates/journey.ts (journey_passes) are likewise imported only by tests. In production, journeyApproved is hardcoded true (production-run.ts:839, rework.ts:870, run.ts:46) and requiredReviewPresent hardcoded false (production-run.ts:838), so the landing guards at core/src/state/guards.ts:284 — which correctly refuse a T2 landing whose review is missing or whose journey is unapproved — are handed constants instead of facts. The acceptance row for pilot 2 proposes to prove the inversion from sessions.review_provider versus worker_provider; those two columns are read by core/src/api/routes.ts:346-347 and written by nothing.

This is unassigned scope, not a defect, and the record should say so. T20's checklist proves the opposite-provider review, verdict_consistent, and review-unavailability blocking on stubs, and M5 closes by stating that real write-capable runs are permitted "from M8's pilots only". The acceptance row covering the delivered production seam names T1 explicitly and is satisfied by core/test/journeys/production-runner.test.ts. No task in this plan ever carried the live T2 wiring; T30 is simply the first place it is needed, and it did not appear in T30's own Outputs. The guards, the router, the two gates, and the recipe were all built correctly — what is absent is the production path calling them.

Why the work is carried inside T30 rather than in a new ticket. A predecessor task was the first preference and is not expressible. core/test/unit/meta/ticket-plan-sync.test.ts, which mechanically enforces invariant 12, loads tickets by /^T\d\d\.md$/, requires ids matching /^T\d\d$/, requires the ticket numbers to form a contiguous prefix T01..Tnn, reads plan tasks by /<h4>(\d+)\./, and requires depends_on to point backwards by string comparison — so the task number carries ordering, an id such as T29a would be silently skipped by the loader and invisible as a plan task, and tickets T01–T34 already exist with no free number between T29 and T30. Appending the binding above T34 would have left the real dependency inexpressible; inserting it as a new task 30 would have renamed five ticket files and every reference to them, including committed records that name T30 as pilot 2. The owner chose to amend T30's scope: the code is still ticketed, still checklisted, and still has its own acceptance rows.

What changed in this commit, and what deliberately did not. Task 30's plan block gains a two-part Entry, Outputs, and Proves, plus five new checklist rows covering tier-2 admission, router-enforced inversion, verdict_consistent with the two projection columns actually written, evidence-backed requiredReviewPresent/journeyApproved, and the journeys that earn them. specs/awsf-plan-acceptance.md gains four matching rows above the pilot-2 row. specs/tickets/T30.md carries the amended build prompt byte-identically from Section B, with a scope note recording the evidence above; its state remains todo. No marker was flipped and no work was performed. Part 1 is hand-built and spends no AWSF call; the ≤ 5 call ceiling continues to govern Part 2 alone, whose minimum is two calls — one builder, one reviewer — since build-review's other two phases are host-owned. M8 remains [wip], T30 remains todo, AWSF is not adopted, and all M9 work stays gated. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-13 — T30 Part 1: the live tier-2 binding, and four places the lifecycle contract corrected the implementation

What was wired, and what was deliberately not built. awsf run now admits build-review and simple-sdlc: the T1-only recipe set became a tier/recipe agreement check, and the five persisted transitions that hardcoded tier: 1 now carry status.tier. The reviewer phase resolves through runMandatoryReview/oppositeProvider, verdict_consistent is applied to the review envelope, sessions.review_provider and review_verdict are written, and the two constants at the old production-run.ts:838-839 are gone. No new machinery was written: every module called here already existed and was already unit- and journey-tested, unimported outside core/test. New surface is one file, core/src/cli/commands/journey.ts, plus one CLI command.

The contract rejected the first design, and it was right. The review was initially run as one more phase inside RUNNING, followed by L12 to the owner. L12's guard refuses that outright — “a T2 task may not skip the opposite-provider review” — because the lifecycle already says what a T2 run looks like: L11 GATING → REVIEWING, which is a spawn site, then L15 REVIEWING → AWAITING_OWNER carrying the verdict. The review is therefore held until the host gates have actually passed and is run against the exact candidate they cleared. A second guard caught the consequence: createCompiledPhaseLaunchVerifier refuses an agent-phase registration from any state but RUNNING, so the review registers against its edge (L11) the way the first call registers against L4. Neither guard was weakened.

Two blocker vocabularies, and a refusal to widen either. The first draft invented review-inversion-invalid and review-unavailable as blocker codes. L5 rejected them — “an unrecognized code is not a blocker, it is an unexplained halt” — so a preflight inversion failure now classifies as phase-abort, exactly like every other route failure, with the detail string naming InvalidReviewInversion. Then L17 rejected phase-abort, because review-unavailable is the one word its vocabulary accepts, guarded by a retry count of at least one. Both readings are now honoured rather than reconciled by editing core/src/state/**, which is a protected path and whose vocabulary is a lifecycle contract, not a convenience. A consequence is recorded plainly: a reviewer that answers inconsistently — a stale SHA, a finding outside the candidate — has no terminal edge at all, so the L15 transition refuses, the failed verdict_consistent gate stays on the record, and the attempt remains REVIEWING for the owner to cancel (L18) or rework (L16). The host does not invent a terminal state for it. Exhausted quota during a review behaves the same way: it is not a transport fault, so it earns no retry and is not forged into a blocker.

Two defects found in existing code while binding it. First, the projector's agent-start and agent cases updated sessions.worker_provider unconditionally, so a review call would have overwritten the very column the inversion is proved against, leaving a session whose worker and reviewer both read as the reviewer. Agent evidence now carries a purpose, absent on older journals and read as worker there, and usage still accumulates across both sides while route identity does not. Second, the provider pair cannot come from the config's adapters[].provider field: it is optional and the committed default omits it on the Claude route, which would have made every inversion check fail closed for the wrong reason. The pair is built from what the adapters report through getModelInfo.

Owner rework stays T1, stated as a boundary rather than left as an accident. awsf rework re-runs exactly one writable builder phase and is built around one route, one permission session, one process record, and one phase id. At T2 the candidate it produces has never been read by a reviewer, and verdict_consistent binds a review to the exact SHA — so admitting it would strand an attempt that gates cleanly and can never satisfy L20. It now refuses with ReworkTierUnsupported, naming cancel + awsf retry, which re-runs the whole workflow including its review and carries the spend forward. The plan checklist row that promised rework would admit T2 was corrected to say this instead; it was written before the command was read closely, and the honest fix is the row, not a silent partial implementation. T30's own build prompt keeps L19 rework as a valid pilot outcome — at T2 that outcome is now reached through retry, at the truthful cost of re-running the review.

Verification. Nine new journeys in core/test/journeys/t2-production.test.ts, all on scripted adapters at zero quota, covering the inverse-provider happy path (two calls, both session columns, the gated verdict), the same-provider refusal at zero cost, the one-retry-then-block with the provider sequence observed rather than assumed, quota's non-retry, both verdict_consistent rejections, the journey refusing a non-candidate revision and a declined attestation recording nothing, and a landing refused until the journey is attested and permitted after. Full suite 963/963 — 812 unit, 45 contract, 55 simulation, 51 journey — with lint clean and the repository typecheck at the same declaration baseline (the six added lines are the identical node:* diagnostics every test file in this repository emits). Part 2 — the pilot itself — has not run. T30 remains todo, M8 remains [wip], no pilot record exists, and AWSF is not adopted. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-13 — T30 Pilot 2 stopped after one builder call: a deterministic failure exposed the missing live correction transport

The pilot spent one call, produced a real candidate, and stopped before review exactly where a red deterministic gate requires. Owner quota ground truth at preflight was 100% ChatGPT weekly remaining and Claude at 0% of its current five-hour session / 11% weekly used; those observations were recorded as evidence and never read by routing. The fixed worker route was codex / openai-codex, requested as codex:gpt-5.6-sol and resolved to gpt-5.6-sol with provenance route-attributed. Session 02d62036-196d-4212-97cf-77d3c8bf83b2, attempt 1 of the owner-authorized corrected task id t30-herdr-visible-fusion-corrected, started from Fusion Harness base 3bb8ef6f15a88c8d5d72ae50d893c1d61e200b01 and produced retained candidate 9f8d8a8d40ca17c7e2f87a30b71e632ab91a2a95. The host-created commit is owner-authored and changes six authorized paths: 537 insertions and 11 deletions. The builder envelope claimed the full glob suite was 240/240 green; the host then ran the exact configured argv against that exact candidate and measured 240 total, 239 pass, 1 fail, exit 1. The attempt reached BLOCKED through phase-abort. No Claude reviewer call ran, no review inversion was claimed, no journey was exercised or attested, and nothing landed.

The first quota-friction datum is concrete rather than hypothetical. The single builder call recorded provider-authority usage of 147,290 input, 26,424 output, 2,938,880 cache-read, zero cache-write, and 7,475 reasoning tokens; input plus output is 173,714. The subscription route supplies no provider-authoritative charge, so no $0.00 is invented. A normal awsf retry would carry the spent call but mint a fresh attempt from canonical Fusion Harness HEAD, with a fresh provider session and no failed-candidate context. That would buy the whole builder again before the mandatory review, despite the existing candidate being close enough to pass 239 tests. The owner declined that blind retry: preserving the hard five-call ceiling is not enough if the implementation burns a second large context window to rediscover a failure the host already measured.

The failed gate also exposed an evidence defect in the retry experience. The retained configured-command evidence is the final bounded 4,000-character segment. It truthfully contains the 239/240 totals but begins in the middle of test 223 and omits the failing test's identity and diagnostic. The builder's own earlier green run and the host's later red run therefore disagree, while the one piece of evidence needed for an efficient correction is absent. The host was right to treat the envelope as a claim and block; it was not yet equipped to hand the same session an actionable failure. A future correction request must carry bounded, scrubbed command, exit, candidate SHA, gate checks, and useful failure output — including a failure near the beginning followed by a long green tail — rather than merely repeating the last bytes.

Why the requested same-builder fix cannot be retrofitted onto this historical call. The production Pi adapter truthfully advertises continuity: none, launches with --no-session, and the production runner authorizes one send before explicitly refusing a second. The provider process exited and no resumable provider session was retained. assertSameSession, the phase correction engine, private continuity storage, and call-neutral correction economics already exist, but only fixtures exercise them; production-run.ts blocks even matching same-session declarations because no verified live correction transport exists. Consequently no implementation written after this stop can wake session 02d62036's worker, and claiming otherwise would be a cold restart disguised as continuity. The retained candidate and journals remain evidence, not a tree to hand-edit.

Setup deviations are disclosed and preserved. The first exact-id task, t30-herdr-visible-fusion, was mistakenly created from the AWSF working directory and prepared against AWSF SHA e6e57ec; it spent zero calls and remains retained because the session may not delete or rewrite history. The owner explicitly authorized the corrected task id. That corrected run was first invoked before its state-root prompt files existed; it refused from PREPARED at zero calls, after which the missing prompt files were supplied and the real run began. The pre-authored journey remains unexecuted at records/journeys/t30-herdr-visible-fusion.md. The dashboard proof did succeed: the detached loopback dashboard returned HTTP 200 with its security headers, and the owner directly confirmed the T30 state, timeline, builder lane, and accumulating activity while the call was still running.

Owner decision and boundary. Pilot 2 is paused rather than retried. Before another T30 call is spent, a separate owner-side Claude Opus/high-effort session will hand-build and verify the missing Pi same-provider-session deterministic-gate correction path at zero AWSF cost: exact session/model/provider identity, private continuity reference, bounded actionable failure evidence, allowance-bounded corrections, full re-gating of each new candidate, and only then a fresh inverse Claude review of the green SHA. The implementation may not weaken L8/L10/L13, make correction unbounded, silently cold-start, substitute providers, or review a red candidate. Its detailed handoff lives outside the repository at /home/santiago_marin/.local/state/awsf/prompts/t30-continuity-prerequisite-handoff.md. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30/Pilot-2 acceptance marker stays unchecked, and M9 remains unopened. Nothing was pushed or deleted. built on: pi.

2026-08-13 — T30 prerequisite: verified same-provider-session correction, both directions, at zero provider cost

What this is, and what it deliberately is not. This is the hand-built repair the previous Amendment named as the precondition for another Pilot 2 call, and it changes exactly one thing about what AWSF can do: a deterministic configured-command failure at an exact candidate is now a correctable gate violation inside the phase that produced it, answered by the same provider conversation, instead of a phase abort that costs the whole builder again. It spent zero AWSF provider calls. No pilot ran, no candidate landed, and no T30, M8, acceptance, or adoption marker moved.

The continuation protocol was read off the installed CLIs, never inferred. pi 0.81.1 offers --session-id <id> ("use exact project session ID, creating it if missing") resolved by exact id within the current cwd against --session-dir; the adapter now uses both, and --no-session had to go because it wins outright — main.js:206-208 returns an in-memory session before --session-id is consulted, which is precisely why the earlier continuity: none declaration was honest at the time. Claude Code 2.1.232 offers --session-id <uuid> on the way in and --resume <uuid> on the way back, and --fork-session is never passed, because "create a new session ID instead of reusing the original" is the cold restart this whole mechanism exists to refuse. The host mints one v4 UUID, which is the intersection of what both CLIs accept, so one code path corrects either route. Both directions are supported and both are tested: a Pi builder with a Claude reviewer, and a Claude builder with a Pi reviewer.

The two CLIs fail differently, and the weaker proof is labelled rather than dressed up. A pi resume of a session it does not have warns on stderr and creates one — a cold start wearing the resume's name — so assertResumable runs on the host, before the launch, against the host's own --session-dir, and requires the exact id, the exact cwd, and at least one prior assistant turn. Claude Code has no --session-dir: relocating its store would mean remapping HOME or allowlisting CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR, and both move the credential lookup along with the transcript. So that route's evidence is provider-refuses-unknown-session, established by the launch rather than before it, backed by the stream's own session identity on the way out. Its transcript persists where that CLI normally keeps it. That is a real difference in privacy between the two routes and it is disclosed rather than smoothed over.

The accounting is the plan's own, and the plan is amended where it was silent rather than reinterpreted. The escalation ladder already prices an intra-phase correction in tokens and GATING → RUNNING (L10) at one tier call, and the phase submachine already requires CORRECTING → RUNNING to resume the same adapter, provider, model and provider session. What it did not say is whether that resume may be a new OS process — and on every CLI on these routes it must be. It may, under conditions this Amendment fixes: the provider conversation is re-entered and proven, the launch is registered before GO exactly like any other, it is bounded by risk.correction_allowance rather than by the ceiling, and it reserves nothing. A third broker authorization class, phase-correction, carries that: it inherits the whole compiled-phase proof, additionally requires the phase's origin reservation to exist and be already spent, refuses round 0, and re-checks the tranche against the configured allowance. L8, L10 and L13 are untouched; no new lifecycle edge exists; awsf rework remains T1-only.

The evidence defect is fixed at its cause. The command runner retained output.slice(-4000), which is the wrong shape for test output specifically: runners print the failure where it happens and the totals at the end, so one window holds the count or the cause but not both. The complete output is now retained host-private at mode 0600, and what reaches a model is a bounded head / first-failure / tail rendering with the omitted-character count stated. The failure window is grown outward from the marker line so a tight budget squeezes the ends and not the cause. A regression test replays the pilot's exact shape — a red test near the beginning of a 240-test run followed by a long green tail — and asserts both that a trailing window loses not ok 6 and that this one keeps its identity, its diagnostic, and the totals.

Two contract corrections were made deliberately and are stated here rather than left to read as oversights. First, the continuity check between config and adapter is now one-way: config that asks for more than the route can do still fails closed, but config that asks for less is a narrowing the owner is entitled to make. The two-way check was right only while none was the only truth an adapter could tell; keeping it would have forced every configured agent onto a capability it may not want, and the reviewer is the standing counter-example — a reviewer that could be corrected is a reviewer that could be argued with. Second, path gates on a candidate-producing phase are now measured from the attempt base rather than from the last commit, because a corrected phase makes a second commit and "since the last commit" would wave through a candidate that had written anywhere at all in round one; a review phase keeps the per-phase view, since charging it with the builder's diff would fail its own writes: [] on the change it was asked to read.

Privacy, and the route the locator escapes by. The public surface names a conversation by a host handle (continuity:<phase>); the provider locator lives only in private/continuity.json at mode 0600. The separation is not decorative: identity errors embed the identity in their messages, and a blocked attempt writes that message into status.json as blocker.detail. One real leak was found by the test that looked for it — the locator rides argv, because that is the protocol, and the barrier records argv verbatim into processes.command_json, so a feature that worked would have published it to the journal, the SQLite projection and the dashboard. Locators are now redacted by exact match at every process-record persist; the flag stays visible and the value does not.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,032/1,032 — 851 unit, 45 contract, 69 simulation, 67 journey, up from 963 — with lint clean. New coverage: byte-exact continuity argv for both adapters including the absence of --no-session, --no-session-persistence and --fork-session; every way a pi resume would silently cold-start, each refused; the private store's mode, reload, double-open refusal and route-change refusal; the correction launch class proved to run a real registered process that charges no call, with fourteen refusals covering an unspent origin call, round 0, a missing verifier, a spent or zero allowance, and every way continuity could have been faked; the bounded-evidence regression in the pilot's exact shape; and a sixteen-case end-to-end journey in both provider directions proving a red command at candidate A is corrected in the same conversation, candidate B is built on top of A with every gate rerun, the review runs only after green gates and is bound to B, and callsSpent is 2, not 3. Fail-closed cases: a switched session, a switched model, a failed resume transport, an exhausted allowance, and a correction that writes outside its globs all block with one call spent, no cold restart, no third turn, and no review of a red candidate. Typecheck is 733 diagnostics against a baseline of 703, every added one in the same four ambient classes the whole repository already emits because the dependency allowlist excludes @types/node; zero new error classes. No live provider probe was performed and none was authorized.

Boundary. This repair alone does not land Pilot 2. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 acceptance marker stays unchecked, M9 remains unopened, and the two retained historical T30 tasks are untouched. Nothing was pushed and nothing was deleted. The next owner-side driving session must preflight a clean canonical HEAD, use a fresh owner-chosen task id, and configure the builder harness.continuity: same-session — without it the route keeps the pre-continuity behaviour and a red gate blocks on L8 as before. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-14 — The diff-scoped reviewer this plan promised now exists, and a gate refuses a review without it

This closes a plan/code contradiction, not a missing feature. The States table has said since authoring that REVIEWING runs a diff-scoped reviewer. The implementation supplied no diff: production-run.ts set a code phase's previous to its TestOutput and ran the deferred review with that same envelope, so the mandatory opposite-provider reviewer was told "review the exact candidate" while receiving a test-command exit code, a bounded TAP tail, no diff, no file list, no source, and — under profile: no-tools — no way to go and look. Its accept was structurally guaranteed whenever findings was empty, and findings was empty because nothing was inspectable. candidatePathsBetween() had computed the exact changed-file set from Git the whole time and passed it only to verdict_consistent, never to the prompt. This is commit 1 of the owner-approved design record at specs/awsf-candidate-reuse-and-review-evidence.md (revision 2, adjudicated the same day). It is hand-built and spent zero AWSF provider calls. No lifecycle change: the ten states, the twenty-four legal edges and the 100-pair matrix at 24 legal / 76 rejected are untouched, and core/src/state/** was not opened.

The compiler gives the reviewer exactly one slot, and that is what decides the shape. compileAgent renders {previous_envelope} and {output_schema} and nothing else, and renderPrevious substitutes one envelope's JSON. There is no second placeholder, and adding one would be a new prompt-compilation concept rather than a repair. So the evidence is a composite envelope, awsf.review-context/v1, carrying four groups: intent (the owner's verbatim request, goals, non-goals, acceptance criteria, test strategy), identity (base and candidate SHAs, the Git-observed changed-file list, insertions, deletions, stat), diff (a bounded diff, its truncation accounting, the names of any wholly omitted files, the digest of the FULL diff, and a host-private reference to it), and the complete TestOutput nested whole. Nesting rather than curating is deliberate: the reviewer used to receive that envelope entire, so any field a curated copy dropped would be evidence the reviewer stopped getting. A review-context code phase composes it immediately before the reviewer in both T2 recipes; being a host phase it buys no call, so minimumCalls and every tier ceiling are unchanged at 2 and 4.

Diff bounding needed its own discipline, and the obvious reuse was the wrong one. command-evidence.ts windows head / first-failure / tail and finds its middle window with TAP-shaped markers; a diff has no failure marker, because every line is equally the subject. gates/review-diff.ts is written fresh around four rules: bound per file, on whole hunks, because a truncated hunk is worse than an omitted one — it looks complete; deletions are never dropped before additions, enforced rather than preferred (a file whose removals did not fit shows no additions at all, since "this candidate only added things" is exactly the false picture the rule exists to prevent); every wholly omitted file is named, so the reviewer's limitations can say what it did not see; and the digest is always of the full diff, retained host-private at raw/review-context-<sha>.diff, mode 0600. Hunks are attributed to paths by asking Git per path rather than by parsing diff --git headers — a filename containing a quote, a newline or a non-ASCII byte would otherwise be attributed to the wrong file, and diffOmittedFiles would then name the wrong thing.

The new gate has two halves and both are required, because one of them cannot prove what it claims. A context carrying the right candidate SHA and the right filenames with diff: "" is a valid envelope, so a two-field check would pass it and "structurally impossible" would be false. So: a pre-GO fitness precondition runs inside the evidence phase — contract validation, the changed-file set against Git in both directions, at least one hunk whenever the candidate changed anything, surviving removals whenever the candidate removes lines, the digest against the file re-read from disk, a non-blank request, non-empty acceptance criteria, gate evidence measured against this candidate and passing, and the whole envelope inside the 256 KiB wire limit. It fails as ReviewEvidenceUnfit before L11 holds a call, so a review that could not have been evidence is never bought. Then review_evidence_present, the thirteenth gate, runs after the phase against the recorded compiled prompt: it compares the compiler's own serialization by digest, and re-checks identity against Git rather than against the context alone. The first proves the evidence was fit; the second proves it was delivered. Neither is satisfiable by an empty diff.

The reviewer is widened to readonly, and what that does not confine is stated rather than glossed. The reference factory's reviewer is told to judge the code on disk and never the builder's summary of it, and AWSF's reviewer could not open a single file. It now runs profile: readonly, allow: [read, grep, find, ls]; writes: [] and continuity: none are unchanged, so repositoryReadOnly holds, writes_within_globs still makes any observed path a breach, and a reviewer that could be re-prompted — argued with — still does not exist. The AWSF readonly ceiling contains no exec and no bash, so this reviewer cannot run git diff; the host-composed diff is precisely the capability the profile cannot supply and is retained alongside it rather than replaced by it. What readonly confines is writes, and it confines reads not at all: the bwrap namespace is built on --ro-bind "/" "/", so the whole host filesystem is readable inside every sandboxed phase, and a model-driven Read that can name an absolute path reaches whatever that mounts. One cheap confinement is added with it — an empty --tmpfs over the state root, emitted immediately after the whole-filesystem bind and before this phase's own runtime is rebound, which hides every attempt directory including private/continuity.json (0600 protects against other users, not against a process running as the owner). bwrap applies binds in argv order, so the order is the control and the descriptor test asserts it exactly. The residual is accepted, not solved: with bwrap present everything outside the state root remains readable, and without it nothing is confined but the adapter's tool allowlist — the badge already says which regime a phase ran under, and a reviewer's own limitations cannot be trusted to disclose what it read. This is recorded as an accepted confidentiality risk of the T30 experiment rather than as a property the design provides.

Two things were deliberately not done. The outputRef values inside the nested TestOutput are not made openable: they resolve against the attempt directory while the provider's cwd is the worktree, and handing a reviewer an absolute path into the attempt directory would work directly against the mask above — so they stay host provenance, the inline outputTail is what the reviewer has, and the prompt says so. And the pre-GO fitness result is not persisted as a gate row: the record of a passing check is the composed context envelope itself, and the record of a failing one is the phase failure that stops the run. That keeps exactly one meaning for a review_evidence_present row — it belongs to the review phase and to nothing else — which is what the design record's later eligibility rule reads.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,081/1,081 green — 894 unit, 45 contract, 70 simulation, 72 journey — with npm run lint clean. New coverage: the bounding rules, including a sweep across every budget from 40 characters upward asserting that every emitted hunk is byte-identical to an original, so no partial hunk can ever be emitted; deletions kept over additions, and an addition refused in the place of a deletion that would not fit; wholly omitted files named; the fitness negatives (an empty diff with a non-empty changed-file list, an all-hunks-omitted diff, a bounded diff that dropped every deletion, a changed-file set wrong in either direction, a digest of another diff, missing acceptance criteria, gate evidence measured against another tree, and a curated TestOutput that lost its tail — rejected by the contract itself); the delivery negatives (a valid context composed but never rendered, a prompt carrying a different context, and a review phase that ran with no context at all); a journey asserting the compiled reviewer prompt contains the owner's request, the changed-file list, the candidate and base SHAs, real hunk headers and the added line itself; a deletion-only candidate whose removals reach the prompt and are not rendered as additions; the full diff retained at 0600 with no absolute attempt path in the prompt; the widened profile proved to read and still refuse exec, bash, edit and write at resolvePermissionProfile; and the exact bwrap argv prefix through the mask. The 100-pair lifecycle matrix is untouched and still asserts 24 legal / 76 rejected. Typecheck is 740 diagnostics against a baseline of 733, every added one in the same ambient classes the whole repository already emits because the dependency allowlist excludes @types/node; zero new error classes.

Boundary. No marker moved. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 acceptance marker stays unchecked, M9 remains unopened, and the retained pilot attempt is evidence and was not cancelled, retried, attested, landed, edited or deleted. Commits 2 and 3 of the design record — the L25 edge with its correction-counter split and the awsf review command — are not in this commit and each gets its own session, in order. Nothing was pushed and nothing was deleted. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-14 — L25 AWAITING_OWNER → REVIEWING, the correction-counter split, and every REVIEWING failure gets an exit

This amends a normative contract, so it says exactly what moved. The Lifecycle Contract goes from 24 legal / 76 rejected to 25 legal / 75 rejected. It is a single-cell amendment: AWAITING_OWNER → REVIEWING leaves the "everything else" IllegalTransition class, so 27 + 10 + 6 + 32 = 75 and the first three classes' arithmetic — "3 × 9", "all ten X → X pairs" — is untouched. The spawn sites go from five to six. The rejection order, the eleven steps, the actor vocabulary, the tiers and the ceilings are unchanged. This is commit 2 of the owner-approved design record at specs/awsf-candidate-reuse-and-review-evidence.md, authorized by its D1. It is hand-built and spent zero AWSF provider calls. Three things land together, in this order, and only the first two are live today.

1 — The correction allowance was one pair of counters carrying two different allowances. The revision-1 fix, "stop beginPhase() resetting correctionsOwner", was wrong and is withdrawn. call-budget.ts states the contract verbatim — "The correction allowance is per PHASE ({auto: 1, owner: 1}), so a new phase refreshes it" — and suppressing that reset would silently convert every rung-3 owner correction from a per-phase allowance into a once-per-attempt one across every multi-phase recipe. Nobody asked for that. The real defect is that #correctionsOwner was charged by two different things: recordPhaseTransition, for an intra-phase correction that is per phase and refreshed correctly; and authorize(), for an owner re-entry on a task edge, which is per attempt and must not be. So an attempt-scoped counter now carries the second — charged in authorize() when a task edge draws the owner tranche, never by recordPhaseTransition, never reset by beginPhase(), reset by beginAttempt() and by awsf retry. The task machine's steps 6 and 9 read it for the owner tranche; the phase machine keeps the per-phase pair and is handed only that pair, so a phase structurally cannot spend a lifecycle allowance. L13 means all three: a task may not block for an exhausted budget while an automatic correction, an intra-phase owner correction, or an owner re-entry remains, so the guard gained a third check rather than a reinterpretation of an existing one.

The split's blast radius is stated because it is wider than the edge's. The counter reaches the ledger, the snapshot every command persists, awsf new, awsf retry, the attempt projection, the SQLite schema (migration 0003, sessions.owner_reentries, user_version 2 → 3), the session API response, the dashboard's session type and its header meter, and awsf status, which now prints the per-phase pair and the per-attempt re-entries on separate lines because they are separate things. rework.ts's private/owner-rework-${n} is the precedent this preserves rather than breaks: it must be numbered by the re-entry counter, or a second rework in one attempt would collide with the first the moment an intervening phase zeroed the per-phase counter. A status written before the column existed reads as zero re-entries with the allowance coupled to its configured owner number — the honest answer, since the counter that could have charged it did not exist — and nothing on disk is rewritten to make retained attempts readable.

2 — L25 preserves a green instead of invalidating one. The contract had "the owner rejects the review and re-enters" (L16) and lacked "the owner rejects the review and re-buys the review", because it assumed a review's content could be wrong but its evidence could not be missing. Before commit 1 every review was structurally unevidenced, so the only recorded remedy for one was cancel plus awsf retry, which rebuilds a candidate that is already green. L25's guard is eleven checks and the two that carry it are new in kind. gateEvidence makes the edge check the green rather than the boolean summarising it: every configured gate must have a row, every row must have passed, and every row must name this candidate — because gatesPass is one flag an earlier transition wrote, and this is the edge that spends a call to keep it. And gatesInvalidated must be absent, in either direction: L16 and L19 must assert it because they change the tree, and L25 exists precisely because the tree does not, so a request that speaks to invalidation at all is a rework wearing a review's name.

Eligibility is deterministic, and that is what stops verdict shopping. A guard that admitted any non-blank reason would let a fully evidenced review returning concern be superseded by a cold replacement that might return accept. Bounding the frequency is not an answer, because a one-shot verdict lottery is exactly what a mandatory opposite-provider review exists to prevent and once is enough. So reviewEvidenceDefect is a closed two-member enum read off the recorded review phase's gate rows — evidence-gate-absent (no review_evidence_present row at all: every review produced before commit 1, including the retained pilot attempt's) and evidence-gate-failed — and the guard rejects any other value. A review carrying a passing evidence row is not replaceable at all. The owner's reason string is required and is a record, never a key. The consequence is deliberate: once every review carries a passing evidence gate, L25 becomes unreachable for new attempts. Its permanent value is the legacy class and the failure class, not a standing second chance.

Purity held, so the physical check is supplied evidence. candidateUnchanged is a host observation — worktree HEAD is the candidate and clean, canonical HEAD is the base and clean — passed into the guard, because core/src/state/** imports nothing impure and the state-purity fence enforces it. The command that authorizes L25 owes that read three times: before authorizing, immediately before GO, and after the review answers and before L15. Two of those windows do not exist yet, because the command does not exist yet; commit 3 owes them, and the residual window is one process launch wide and named rather than papered over. A clean checkout of a different commit is invisible to both captureChangeSet and assertClean, which is why the check is a SHA comparison and not a cleanliness one.

The tranche coupling, and the cost of it. Per D3, L25 draws the same owner re-entry allowance as L16 and L19: one owner-authorized re-entry of either kind per attempt. After a replacement review, an owner rework is refused as exhausted, and the reverse holds too — a property the suite proves rather than asserts. The accepted regression risk (D5) is stated in full: L25 makes a failed attempt strictly worse. The task leaves AWAITING_OWNER, where it could land, for REVIEWING, where it cannot; the owner tranche is spent by the authorization itself, so L16 is gone too; ReworkTierUnsupported still blocks L19 at T2; and a failed replacement blocks the attempt, which costs the candidate on awsf retry. The owner chose to accept this rather than reopen D3, on the grounds that every deterministic failure now moves before GO, leaving only transport (which has its retry) and a reviewer that cannot emit valid JSON. Commit 3's confirmation prompt must display all of it in plain words, including the sentence "if this review fails, the candidate is lost".

3 — L17 widens, and this repairs a hole that predates L25. Consider today a reviewer whose envelope fails schema validation. L15 needs a valid, consistent verdict — absent. L16 needs a finding of severity ≥ medium carrying a file and a detail — a malformed envelope has no findings at all. L17 needed a transport retry — but a review that answered was never a transport failure. So cancel was the only edge, and a parse error already cost the whole candidate, with the reviewer unable to self-correct because continuity: none refuses a second turn outright. L17's guard now accepts one transport retry or a host-determined review-malformed / review-evidence-invalid failure, and its blocker vocabulary goes from one code to three. Both additions have the property that made transport unavailability qualify in the first place: they need no interpretation — TypeBox either validated or it did not, and a gate row either passed or it did not. A verdict_consistent failure is deliberately excluded and its exits are unchanged, because an inconsistent verdict is content and the host does not decide what a bad review means. That residual is named, not fixed. This change is live the moment it lands and is independently worth landing.

What was deliberately not changed. The completed [x] acceptance checklists of T4 and T5, and T04.md's verbatim build prompt, still read "24 legal / 76 rejected". They are the record of what was verified and instructed then, and rewriting them would make the plan claim T4 asserted a contract that did not exist on that day. The normative tables above are the contract now, and the unchecked criteria moved with them — the Validation section's test:unit row here, and the three Lifecycle-contract rows in awsf-plan-acceptance.md (25 accepts, 75 throws with 32 IllegalTransition, six spawn sites). Those three are reworded rather than marked [f] deliberately: [f] is for a row that turns out to be impossible as written, and these are rows whose subject the owner amended under D1 — so the rewording is disclosed here rather than made quietly, which is what that convention is actually protecting against. ProcessRegistration's edge union was not extended, and revision 1's instruction to do so is withdrawn as unnecessary: SPAWN_SITE_EDGES is derived from LEGAL_EDGES.filter(e => e.spawnSite), so L25 propagated to the broker for free and only the comments and tests that restated "five" needed hands on them. And the host's own inter-state escalation — an L10 with actor host — still draws the per-phase automatic tranche exactly as it always has; widening that too would be a normative change nobody asked for.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,097/1,097 green — 910 unit, 45 contract, 70 simulation, 72 journey, up from 1,081 — with npm run lint clean. 2026-08-15 — awsf review: L25 gains its caller, and one owner-authorized replacement review

L25 had a guard and no caller. This is the caller. Commit 3 of the owner-approved design record at specs/awsf-candidate-reuse-and-review-evidence.md, hand-built at zero AWSF provider calls. awsf review TASK --reason "<why the recorded review is not evidence>" is TTY-only, buys exactly one call, and rebuilds nothing: it is the T2 counterpart of awsf rework and the opposite trade, because it changes no tree and therefore preserves and revalidates the green rather than invalidating it. The name is review and not re-review for a mechanical reason worth recording: the doc-reconciliation meta-test matches documented CLI commands with /(?:^|\s)awsf (db rebuild|[a-z]+)\b/, which cannot match a hyphen, so a documented awsf re-review would be read as awsf re and fail the CLI-table check.

Eligibility is read, never asserted. The command derives reviewEvidenceDefect from the journal — the recorded review phase's own review_evidence_present rows, absent meaning evidence-gate-absent and a failed one meaning evidence-gate-failed — and refuses with ReviewNotReplaceable, at zero cost, when the recorded review carries a passing row. The owner's --reason is required and is a record: it reaches the journal and the transition, and it is deliberately never put in the replacement reviewer's prompt, because telling a cold reviewer that the owner rejected its predecessor briefs it on a conclusion, and a briefed reviewer is an argued-with one. The journal is read rather than SQLite for the same reason the eligibility is host-determined: an awsf db rebuild must not be able to change who is eligible.

The three-point candidate revalidation of §5.3.2, in the command, because a pure guard cannot make it. Before authorizing L25 the full reuse key is re-read from Git: both HEADs, both trees clean, both objects present, ancestry, host author and committer, and git diff --check. Again immediately before GO — inside the launch wrapper, after the permission session is open and the sandbox grant is built, as the last host instruction before the child's first — and a third time after the review answers and before L15. All three are SHA comparisons, never cleanliness ones: a clean checkout of a different commit produces an identical captureChangeSet fingerprint and an empty status --porcelain, so nothing in the permission layer can see it. The two windows behave differently on purpose. A move found before GO bought nothing, so the reservation goes back and no blocker vocabulary is invoked; a move found after the review is host-deterministic evidence that the tree the reviewer read is not the tree the guard approved, and it takes L17 as review-evidence-invalid, which is exactly the case §5.5 names. The residual window is one process launch wide and is named rather than papered over — closing it needs an immutable materialization, which is commit 4's territory and remains deferred.

Generation-qualified persistence, or the replacement silently overwrites the evidence it supersedes. Six identities would have collided on a fresh run id: the envelope file, the DB phase row, the gate rows derived from it, the private runtime directory, the compiled-prompt record and the raw host output — plus the retained full diff, which is keyed by candidate SHA and so would have been rewritten by a replacement of the same candidate. All of them now carry -re<N>, where N is the owner re-entry counter this command is about to charge, following rework.ts's existing owner-rework-${n} pattern. A journey test snapshots the original reviewer-0 set and asserts it is byte-identical afterwards.

Two calls of headroom, or nothing happens at all. §5.4's arithmetic is decisive: with one call left the review spends the last one, and a transport fault then raises CallCeilingExceeded from inside the retry closure — which is not a transport failure, so it is never wrapped as MandatoryReviewUnavailable, so L17 could not have been taken either, and the attempt is stranded. So the command refuses at preflight below two, in those words. In practice it serves build-review (2 spent of 5) freely and refuses a completed simple-sdlc (4 spent of 5). The T2 ceiling was not raised to buy that workflow a replacement review: the ceiling is a T30 experimental control, and raising it is a separate decision nobody has taken.

Every non-transport failure is classified, and two of them are deliberately not L17's. A malformed or schema-invalid replacement blocks on L17 as review-malformed; a failed evidence gate or a post-review tree move blocks as review-evidence-invalid. A permission breach does not: a breach is a policy failure, not a statement about the evidence, and mapping it onto a review vocabulary would let the host declare a policy violation terminal under a word that does not mean that — so it is recorded explicitly, with its code and the owner's remaining exit, instead of inheriting the runner's unclassified rethrow. A verdict_consistent failure keeps the existing behaviour and is the one named residual: an inconsistent verdict is content, the host does not decide what a bad review means, and the attempt stays in REVIEWING with its failed gate on the record. A projection fault after the review answered is held at REVIEWING with sqlite-projection-failed rather than blocked, so a good review is never lost to a bad write — the same handling the runner gives a GATING-sourced fault, extended to this source.

Recovery is command-specific, and it diverges from awsf rework in exactly one way, deliberately. Generic journal replay reconstructs files; it cannot decide whether an in-flight provider ran, and beginAttempt refuses outright while a reservation is held. So the block is modelled on rework.ts's: release outstanding reservations, stamp a recovery timestamp, mark the phase FAILED with defined times, observe and settle the registered process, persist a defined outcome. The divergence: when nothing reached GO, awsf review rewinds the owner re-entry charge, through a new CallBudget.rewindOwnerReentry() that refuses while any reservation is still held. The owner authorized one replacement review; a launch that never happened is not that, and confiscating the single per-attempt allowance for it would leave the attempt with neither a re-entry nor a review. awsf rework deliberately does not do this and was not changed to match — owner-rework.test.ts asserts its re-entry stays charged after a failed launch, and changing what a failed rework costs is a normative change nobody authorized. Re-running awsf review on an attempt with a stale reservation reconciles it first — releasing the call and rewinding the charge when no process ever left REGISTERED — and only then refuses on the reconciled state, which is what unblocks awsf retry.

The human gate at L20 is told that a review was replaced. requiredReviewPresent is one boolean, and a landing screen that showed only the replacement verdict would hide the very thing L25 exists to make visible — so awsf land now displays the superseded verdict, the evidence defect that made it replaceable, and the replacement verdict side by side. Both commands read the journal through one module for that reason: two readers could disagree about which review is on record. And the confirmation prompt is not advisory copy — a test asserts it contains the candidate SHA, the superseded verdict, its evidence defect, callsSpent / ceiling, that the attempt leaves AWAITING_OWNER for REVIEWING, that L16 and L19 are gone once the allowance is spent, and D5's sentence verbatim: "if this review fails, the candidate is lost." journeyApproved is never touched, because the candidate did not change and the attestation still concerns the same tree.

One thing in the design record could not be implemented as specified, and the reason is structural. Commit 3's test list asks for "a failed review_evidence_present on the replacement reaching L17 review-evidence-invalid." In this command that gate cannot fail: its inputs are the host objects the command composed and already proved fit before GO, and its prompt argument is the exact string handed to the provider rather than a separately recorded one, so composition and delivery are the same object. Rather than fabricate a seam to make it fail, both halves of the code path are proved by the other conditions §5.5 gives the same code: a replacement whose declared evidence does not exist on disk, and a candidate that moved between the review and L15. The gate's own negatives remain covered by the unit suite from commit 1, where the two can genuinely differ.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,125/1,125 green — 920 unit, 45 contract, 70 simulation, 90 journey, up from 1,097 — with npm run lint clean. New coverage: a replacement spending exactly one call with no builder launched and the candidate unchanged; the confirmation prompt asserted line by line including D5's sentence; a fully evidenced review refused as not replaceable and a failed evidence row accepted, at zero cost either way; nine zero-cost refusals proved to leave the revision, the spend and the allowance untouched — blank reason, piped owner, exhausted allowance, config-snapshot drift, recorded-model drift, recorded-adapter drift, same-provider substitution, a moved candidate and a dirty worktree; a simple-sdlc at four spent calls refused for insufficient headroom; a candidate moved before GO and again after the answer, refused separately and differently; one transport retry then L17 review-unavailable; a malformed envelope reaching review-malformed; declared evidence that does not exist reaching review-evidence-invalid; an inconsistent verdict not blocking; a permission breach classified rather than rethrown; a crash between L25 and GO releasing the call and rewinding the charge, and the cross-process half reconciling a stale reservation before refusing; the reviewer-re1 artefacts written with the reviewer-0 set byte-identical; the landing screen showing both verdicts; the CLI routing awsf review and requiring --reason; and L25 and L19 proved to share the one allowance from this side. Plus the journal reader's unit cases and the ledger's rewind, which refuses while a reservation is held. Typecheck is 752 diagnostics against a baseline of 740, the twelve added ones all TS2307 for node: builtins in the five new files, in the identical ambient classes the whole repository already emits because the dependency allowlist excludes @types/node; zero new error classes.

Boundary. No status marker moved. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 marker stays unchecked, and M9 remains unopened; the one acceptance row added in awsf-plan-acceptance.md is for work this session completed and the journey suite proves. The retained pilot attempt is evidence and was not cancelled, retried, attested, landed, edited or deleted, and no live task state is encoded anywhere here. awsf review never lands, never deletes, and never rewrites a retained envelope. Commit 4 — the generalized candidate-scoped continuation — remains deferred and unopened, and git worktree add --detach was considered and rejected for commits 1–3 exactly as §5.3.2 records. Nothing was pushed and nothing was deleted. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-15 — The fences for a document tree that does not exist yet, and two invariant scope repairs that stand on their own

Fences first, documents later — the reordering is the point. This is commit 5 of the owner-approved design record at specs/awsf-candidate-reuse-and-review-evidence.md (Part V, §V.9), hand-built at zero AWSF provider calls. Not one line of the driving-document tree is written here. A fence that arrives after the documents it guards has nothing to catch, and the two cheapest items in the sequence turned out to be repairs to AGENTS.md as written rather than preparation for anything: invariant 4 says shell: true appears nowhere while its meta-test walked only core/src and dashboard/src, and invariant 9 says credentials belong "in fixtures or anywhere else" while its sweep walked core/test/fixtures plus four root manifests. Both were narrower than the sentence they enforce, and both are worth landing whether or not a driving document is ever written. Nothing under core/src was opened; this commit is entirely under core/test/unit/meta/.

The tree lives at docs/driving/ and not at .claude/, and the reason is an adapter asymmetry rather than a preference. AWSF does not read skills — the provider CLI does, from its own working directory, and a Claude-route phase's cwd is a managed worktree of this repository. pi-codex suppresses discovery explicitly with --no-skills and four other clean-room flags; claude-code's buildSpec has no verified equivalent. So a committed .claude/ tree would put owner-tier driving instructions — how to prepare a task, read a blocked attempt, decide an owner act — inside the context of the worker phases those instructions are about. Placement is therefore the control, and the guard that holds it is two lines of existsSync that cannot rot. It is written to say exactly what it means: it does not claim isolation, it claims absence. Whether a CLI exposes a tree it discovered is a question about that CLI's gating behaviour, and this repository already refuses to treat an unverified flag as a control. The strictly better guard — a Claude descriptor flag that demonstrably disables project skill and command discovery, pinned in buildSpec in exact argv order the way pi's is — is named in the test's own comment as what would make moving the tree a safe one-commit change if it is ever found and verified.

The other half of that fence was already satisfied, and the honest work was proving it had not been duplicated. pi-codex.test.ts pins --no-skills inside its exact-argv assertions and again in the loop that proves the flag is unconditional across profiles. Adding a third copy would have satisfied a reader looking for the flag while the argv order went unwatched, which is how a pin turns into decoration. The new assertion is arithmetic rather than a magic number: every occurrence of the flag in that file is either inside an ordered clean-room run or in the flag loop, so a legitimately new argv assertion moves both sides at once. The survey that said "two sites" was one short — there are two ordered pins, ephemeral and continuity — which is exactly the kind of remembered fact this check exists to stop trusting.

Doc-reconciliation extends as a narrowing, not as an extension. The existing scanner treats every backticked string in README.md and the Validation section as a claim that a command exists. That is right for a README and wrong for a cookbook, which names commands constantly in explanatory sentences and sometimes names them deliberately as counter-examples; running it over a tree of prose would produce failures that teach authors to stop using backticks, degrading the documents to protect the test. So the driving tree is scanned through fenced bash / sh / console blocks only — a fenced line is a thing a session copies and runs, therefore a claim — with # comment lines skipped and a $ console prompt stripped. The residual gap is named and accepted: prose naming a command that does not exist goes uncaught, and closing it with a cleverer regex costs more in false positives than the gap costs in truth. The unhyphenated-command constraint recorded when awsf review was named still binds, for the same regex reason.

The schema fence extends with three of its six patterns, and the three left out are left out on purpose. A driving document may never restate a schema, so a json fence has no legitimate use in one and the blanket ban transfers whole. The pattern sweep does not: the two field-name patterns match ordinary explanation — a cookbook line reading changedFiles: the host-observed set of paths is a sentence, not a transcription — and "type": "object" matches an ordinary config example. What survives cannot be explanation: a schema id literal, a JSON Schema keyword, a pasted TypeScript declaration. The two pattern sets are therefore different and parameterized rather than widened, and because the driving subset is selected by pattern name, a test asserts the subset is still three — renaming a pattern would otherwise empty the driving set and every fence over it, silently.

A fence that cannot fail is not a fence, and every one of these lands against a directory that is not there. walkFiles returns [] for a missing directory, so all four sweeps are vacuously green on the day they ship — the mirror image of a loud failure, and the single real hazard of this commit. Each therefore carries a companion that feeds its matcher a synthetic offender in memory and asserts it is caught: a credential-shaped token in a fenced example, a shell: true inside a markdown code block, a fenced block naming a command that is not in the CLI table alongside one that is, and a schema id, a JSON Schema keyword, a pasted interface …Output and a json fence. The credential specimen is assembled from two literals rather than written as one, because invariant 9 says credentials belong nowhere and a fence carrying a credential-shaped literal in its own source is the first thing a repository-wide sweep would report. Beyond the companions, all six fences were run once against a real synthetic tree written to docs/driving/ and a real empty .claude/, each observed to fail with the offending path and line, and both removed before commit — the tree is clean and neither is committed.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,136/1,136 green — 931 unit, 45 contract, 70 simulation, 90 journey, up from 1,125 — with npm run lint clean at 0 warnings and 0 errors. Typecheck is 757 diagnostics against a baseline of 752; the five added are all TS2307 for node: builtins in the two new files, the same ambient class the whole repository already emits because the dependency allowlist excludes @types/node. Zero new error classes: the only non-ambient diagnostics in the repository remain the three that predate this commit. One acceptance row is added, for the two invariant scope repairs and nothing else — the fences over the driving tree prove that their matchers bite, which is not the same as proving a tree is clean, and a row claiming the latter would be a marker earned by a directory that does not exist.

Boundary. No status marker moved. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 marker stays unchecked, and M9 remains unopened. No live task state is encoded here and the retained pilot attempt is evidence — it was not cancelled, retried, attested, landed, edited or deleted. core/src was not opened, and claude-code.ts in particular was deliberately not touched: §V.6.0 chose placement over an adapter change precisely so that a markdown convenience never becomes the reason to edit a file the adapter fence and a hand-asserted descriptor test both guard. Commits 6 and 7 — the driving documents themselves — are not in this commit and were not opened; a caution for whoever writes them is recorded where they will see it, in the fence's own header, because the junk-drawer meta-test rejects any tracked basename containing manifest or receipt. Commit 4 remains deferred and unopened. Nothing was pushed and nothing was deleted. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-15 — The driving tree exists: an orientation command, a router, one cookbook, and the two preflight checks that are CLI work instead

Three documents and one test. This is commit 6 of the design record at specs/awsf-candidate-reuse-and-review-evidence.md (Part V, §V.9), hand-built at zero AWSF provider calls: docs/driving/commands/prime-awsf.md (a fixed reading order, a toolchain preflight, then a full stop), docs/driving/skills/awsf/SKILL.md (posture, the four hard rules, and a router), and docs/driving/skills/awsf/cookbooks/preflight_a_task.md. Nothing under core/src was opened. The tree is at docs/driving/ and not at .claude/ for the adapter reason commit 5 recorded, and it is installed or symlinked into the owner's own skill directory from there — this session created no .claude/, added no ignore entry for one, and installed nothing anywhere.

The one item that makes this more than prose is the non-vacuity test. Commit 5's fences all sweep a walked file list, and walkFiles returns [] for a missing directory — so every one of them was green over nothing on the day it landed. Each proves its matcher bites; none can prove the walk arrives. core/test/unit/meta/driving-tree.test.ts is that proof, mirroring the "prompts/ is scanned at all" assertion the schema fence already carries: it fails the moment docs/driving/ is moved, renamed or emptied, which is exactly when four fences would otherwise slip back to catching nothing with no other test noticing. It was verified by renaming the tree and observing the failure, and the walkers were verified to reach all three documents. Beyond that, each of the four sweeps was run once against a real offender written into the live tree — a fenced block naming a command absent from the CLI table, a json fence, a shell: true, and a credential-shaped literal assembled from two pieces rather than written as one — and each was observed to fail with the offending path before the probe was removed. The fifth fence, the .claude/ absence check, is path-based rather than walk-based and needs no such proof; it is green because the directory was not created.

Half of what the design proposed for the cookbook is not in it, and §V.9.1 is why. Hard rule 1 says that if a skill were needed for correctness, that is a defect in the CLI and is fixed there. Applied honestly, the two preflight items that motivated this commit fail it. "Check the configured prompt files exist before starting" is the disclosed pilot-2 false start — a run invoked before its state-root prompt files existed, refused from PREPARED at zero calls — and its fix is awsf new/awsf start validating the configured prompt paths with the escape checks the production runner already performs on them, not a paragraph asking a human to remember. "Check you are in the right repository" is the other disclosed false start — a task created from the AWSF working directory and now permanently retained — and prose cannot prevent a wrong working directory; a displayed resolved canonical repository and HEAD with a TTY confirmation can. Neither was implemented here: this is a markdown commit, and touching core/src would have been a second unauthorized commit wearing this one's name. Both are therefore recorded as named backlog — (i) prompt-path validation at awsf new/awsf start, (ii) resolved-repository-and-HEAD display with TTY confirmation at awsf new — and the cookbook names them out loud in a closing section rather than absorbing them as habits. What survives in it is the judgment layer §V.9.1 leaves standing: the four-line request shape and the rule that the intent is the owner's and the precision is the driver's; how to choose a tier by risk rather than by budget; and reading a diagnosis as evidence rather than as a chore list.

What the documents may not contain decided most of what they say. No schema, edge id, tier ceiling, gate id, blocker code or model id is restated anywhere in the tree; every one of those is pointed at in core/src/contracts/, core/src/state/, awsf.config.yaml or this plan, because a number copied into prose goes stale in silence. The orientation command ends by stopping and says why in two sentences: volunteered state is guessed state, and — the reason specific to this repository — a command that opened with a live status board would be one edit away from a committed file encoding which attempt is running. That is also why every worked example uses the placeholder TASK: the only real task id available to this session is the retained pilot attempt, and a fenced status command naming it in a committed cookbook would be invariant 1 breached by the document meant to teach it. No session ids, continuity locators or machine paths appear. status, watch, dash and journal reads are called read-only; db rebuild is called non-destructive and idempotent and never read-only, because it builds aside and renames into place. private/ is named as never-opened rather than merely omitted. The router lists the one cookbook that exists and says plainly that the rest are unwritten, since a route to a missing document is the same drift these rules exist to prevent.
