2026-08-15 — The rest of the driving tree: five cookbooks, three references, a router that no longer apologizes, and three more defects named rather than written

Eight documents, one test, and two corrections to documents that already existed. This is commit 7 of the design record at specs/awsf-candidate-reuse-and-review-evidence.md (Part V, §V.9), hand-built at zero AWSF provider calls. New: cookbooks/how_to_prompt_for_the_owner.md, cookbooks/choose_the_workflow_and_tier.md, cookbooks/run_and_observe.md, cookbooks/read_a_blocked_attempt.md, cookbooks/owner_acts.md, references/lifecycle.md, references/evidence_map.md, references/gotchas.md. SKILL.md's route table grows from one row to nine and loses the paragraph saying the rest were unwritten, which had become false. Nothing under core/src was opened; the only non-markdown file is one meta-test. No .claude/ was created, no ignore entry added, and nothing was installed anywhere — the owner installs the tree themselves, which is the whole point of §V.6.0's placement decision.

§V.9.1 disqualified part of three of these documents, and all three of those parts are recorded as backlog instead of written. Hard rule 1 says that if a skill were needed for correctness, that is a defect in the CLI and is fixed there. Applied honestly: (iii) read_a_blocked_attempt.md's gathering steps are archaeology, and the fix is an evidence mode on the status command; (iv) owner_acts.md's table of what each act costs and invalidates is precisely what "reference, never duplicate" forbids, and the fix is each command displaying its own cost and invalidation at its confirmation prompt; (v) choose_the_workflow_and_tier.md may not restate the recipes, their ceilings or their phase lists, and the CLI is supposed to supply what exists. None of the three was implemented — this is a document commit, and touching core/src would have been a second unauthorized commit wearing this one's name. Each document names its own gap in a closing section rather than absorbing it, following the pattern commit 6 established for items (i) and (ii).

Two of those three backlog items had to be re-derived against the code, because the design record's premises for them have gone stale. §V.9.1 says awsf status "prints one budget line". It prints nine: state with the blocker code and detail, phase, correction rounds against their maximum, calls spent and reserved against the ceiling, model with its resolution provenance, last activity, the per-phase correction budget, the attempt-scoped owner re-entry allowance, and a next action. So the --evidence gap is narrower than the record believed — the blocker is already displayed, and what is genuinely absent is the failing gate rows, the last envelope and the retained process record. Item (iii) is written against that, not against the record's sentence, because carrying forward a defect that has partly closed is the same failure mode the gotchas.md rule exists to prevent. Similarly, §V.9.1 says the diagnosis command "can print" the recipes: today it prints locks, process liveness, degraded sessions and the size of the state matrix, and nothing prints the workflow catalogue at all. That is item (v), and it also made a sentence in commit 6's preflight_a_task.md false — "reported by the diagnosis command" — which is corrected here rather than left standing beside a new document that says the opposite. Item (iv) was derived the same way: awsf review's prompt is the model and is asserted line by line, while awsf cancel displays nothing but the question, awsf land shows the candidate and the fast-forward meter but no statement of consequence, and awsf rework shows the budget but never says in words that it invalidates the gates and the recorded review — even though its own transition evidence records exactly that.

The split against commit 6's cookbook was decided deliberately, and it went the "absorb and point" way rather than the "move and leave a pointer" way. preflight_a_task.md keeps the four-line request shape and the short tier heuristic, because it is the document open at the moment those are used and stripping the highest-traffic document to a pointer would make it incomplete exactly when it matters. The two new cookbooks go deeper without restating: how_to_prompt_for_the_owner.md is how to get four good lines out of one sentence — what a "Done means" that only looks like a condition reads like, with a three-row before/after table, how to cut, and what may never go into a request — and choose_the_workflow_and_tier.md is the decision procedure, the coupling between the two dials and the fact that it is enforced at compile time rather than mid-run, and the two failure modes worth naming out loud. Each carries an explicit pointer to the preflight section it extends, and the preflight now points forward to both. Two documents saying the same thing differently is the second-source-of-truth failure this design is organized against, and the pointers are what make the split visible rather than accidental.

gotchas.md is the hardest document here, and two of the design record's five candidate traps were dropped rather than shipped. The rule is that every entry is either currently reproducible or sits in a clearly separated Historical section naming the commit that closed it, and the two are never mixed. Dropped: a flag-precedence rule inside one provider CLI, cited in an adapter comment against a pinned version of that CLI's own source — not a file this repository contains, not verifiable from here, and unreachable in any case because the adapter emits one branch or the other and never both; and the fork-session flag that must never be passed, which is verified true but is a rule for whoever edits an adapter rather than a trap with a symptom, and a driving session has no argv of its own to put it in. Both drops are recorded in the document itself so nobody proposes them again without new evidence. What survives is ten live entries, every one read off the source: owner rework refused above the middle tier; owner acts refusing after a configuration snapshot drifts while awsf retry re-snapshots instead; retry carrying spend forward while resetting a different allowance; five commands that use a non-zero exit to report an outcome; a diagnosis command that reports and never repairs; awsf gc deleting nothing; everything resolving from the working directory with nothing displayed before it acts; piped input refusing every owner act; the two provider routes failing in opposite directions on an unknown session, with the host's own assertResumable as the guard and a note that the two implementations are deliberately unequal; and every phase starting failed. Four entries sit in Historical with their closing commits — the unevidenced reviewer, the shared re-entry counter, the trailing-window command evidence, and the continuity locator riding argv into the process row. The second of those carries a correction with it: revision 1's diagnosis of that defect was withdrawn as wrong in the commit that fixed it, and a plausible-but-wrong diagnosis surviving into a trap table is exactly what the live/historical split exists to stop.

A real blind spot in the command fence was found by probing it, and the tree was changed to close it. The scanner reads the word after awsf in a fenced line. In the npm run awsf -- doctor form that word is --, so the CLI half of the fence matched nothing at all — every fenced awsf subcommand in commit 6's documents was unguarded, and the fence's own probe passed for the wrong reason. Every fence in the tree now uses just awsf <command>, which the scanner does read, and both forms invoke the same canonical npm script. The residual gap §V.8 already named and accepted still stands and is worth restating for the next author: the regex reads the command word only, so an invented flag in a fenced block passes. awsf status takes no flags of its own today, and no document here writes one into a runnable block.

One test beyond §V.9's commit-7 row, and the reason it earns its place. The row asks only for cookbooks and references. core/test/unit/meta/driving-routes.test.ts asserts that every relative markdown path the router advertises resolves to a real file inside the tree, and that it advertises at least two — a router naming nothing cannot go stale and cannot be useful either. It is the natural successor to commit 6's non-vacuity proof and it fences the specific drift this commit introduces: a route table that grew from one row to nine is exactly the artefact that outlives the documents it names, because a table is edited when a document is planned and a document is written later or not at all. A route is recognised by carrying a directory component, which is the discriminator rather than an accident of layout — a bare basename in the router names a file elsewhere in the repository, and asserting AGENTS.md resolves inside the tree would assert the opposite of the truth. That distinction was found by the test failing on its first run, which is the test doing its job before it had any documents to protect.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,139/1,139 green — 934 unit, 45 contract, 70 simulation, 90 journey, up from 1,137 — with npm run lint clean at 0 warnings and 0 errors. Typecheck is 764 diagnostics against a baseline of 760; the four added are all TS2307 for the four node: builtins the one new test file imports, the ambient class the whole repository emits because the dependency allowlist excludes @types/node. Zero new error classes: the only non-ambient diagnostics remain the three that predate this commit. Two fences fired for real on these documents before they were fixed, and both were findings about the documents rather than obstacles: the credential sweep caught sk-lifetime inside the ordinary phrase "spend is task-lifetime" in two files, reworded rather than suppressed; and the route test caught the bare-basename case above. Beyond those, each fence was probed once against a synthetic offender written into the live tree — an unknown awsf command, an unknown just target and an unknown npm script in one block, a json fence, and a shell: true inside a fenced example — each observed to fail naming the offending file, and all removed before commit.

What the documents may not contain decided most of what they say, again. lifecycle.md is pointer-only: no state list, no edge, no edge count, no rejection class, no ordering — a table of which file answers which question, plus three properties of those sources that cannot go stale the way a transcribed edge can. evidence_map.md says where every artefact lives and never what is inside one; it lists private/ explicitly and states the never-open rule out loud, because a directory left unmentioned is avoided by habit and one named as forbidden is avoided by rule, and it separately warns that raw/ is readable but unredacted and must never be quoted verbatim into a report, a request or a file. status, watch, dash and journal reads are called read-only; db rebuild is called non-destructive and idempotent and never read-only. Every worked example uses the placeholder TASK: the only real task id available to this session is the retained pilot attempt, and a fenced status command naming it in a committed cookbook would be invariant 1 breached by the documents most tempted to do it — so no session id, continuity locator, machine path or pasted status output appears anywhere in the tree. The status display is described line by line in run_and_observe.md from the formatter's source, with placeholder values and nothing read from a live attempt.

Boundary. No status marker moved. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 marker stays unchecked, and M9 remains unopened. One acceptance row is added, and it claims only what driving-routes.test.ts demonstrates — that the router's routes resolve — which is a different and narrower claim than commit 6's row that the tree is scanned. 2026-08-15 — awsf db rebuild could not replay a journal older than the counter L25 added, which made the documented repair impossible on the databases that needed it

Found by pilot preflight, which is what preflight is for. Driving-session preflight for the T30 pilot ran awsf doctor and got finding: unreadable projection: no such column: owner_reentries. The documented repair for that is awsf db rebuild, and it refused: "projection failed rebuilding … at source_seq 1: Provided value cannot be bound to SQLite parameter 8." It refused correctly — it built the candidate aside, retained it, and left the live database untouched — but the refusal was a deadlock, and the pilot could not proceed through it. Zero AWSF provider calls; this commit is a defect fix and nothing else.

The defect is a backward-compatibility gap in the L25 commit, and it is narrower than it looks. That commit did write the shim — withOwnerReentries reads a pre-owner_reentries status as zero re-entries with the allowance coupled to its configured owner tranche, which is the same answer the ledger would have given because nothing had been charged by a counter that did not exist. What it did not do is apply it everywhere a persisted status is deserialized. It guarded readAttempt, which reads status.json; it did not guard the journal replay, where rebuildCommand takes record.event.next through two as casts on parsed JSON and hands it to the projector. AttemptStatus.budget.ownerReentries is typed number, so a legacy record arriving there is a value lying about its own type; node:sqlite will not bind undefined, and the rebuild dies on the first pre-upgrade attempt it meets — in this state root, a pilot-1 attempt from four days earlier.

Why it stopped an owner act rather than merely printing a finding. A live database that predates the migration cannot take a projection write at all. Every write throws, createDashboardProjection catches it and marks the session degraded, and assertLaunchPermitted then refuses before GO — while a degraded session is separately barred from advancing past GATING, which REVIEWING is. So the chain ran: stale projection → every session degraded on touch → awsf review fails closed before spending a call → and the one command that repairs the projection is the one that cannot run. Each link is a control working as designed; the composition was the trap.

The fix is placement, not logic. The shim is exported and applied wherever untyped JSON becomes an AttemptStatus — readAttempt as before, deriveAttemptStatus, and the rebuild's per-record replay — and deliberately nowhere downstream of those. Folding the coalesce into toAttemptStatusProjection would have covered more callers with less code and was rejected: that function is a pure mapper entitled to trust its parameter type, and a mapper that silently repairs its input hides which caller is feeding it a stale shape. The three boundaries are now the whole set and they are visible in one file. deriveAttemptStatus has no caller today and was fixed anyway — it is exported, it reads journal records, and it is the same hole waiting for its first user.

The test is the one the L25 commit was missing, and it was proved to bite. It creates a real attempt, drives it to a terminal state, then strips ownerReentries from every budget in both the journal and status.json — rewriting a genuine attempt rather than hand-rolling a fixture, because the shape that broke this was a real pre-upgrade journal and a fixture invented from the test's own assumption would prove only that the assumption is self-consistent. It asserts the field's absence as a precondition first, so a green rebuild cannot pass vacuously; then asserts the rebuild succeeds, that the terminal state arrived (so every record replayed, not just the first), that owner_reentries is 0 and never null — null would read as "unknown" to every consumer of that column — and that the reader half of the shim agrees on the same stripped bytes. Reverting the one-line fix makes it fail with the identical production string, at source_seq 1: Provided value cannot be bound to SQLite parameter 8, which is how the reproduction was confirmed rather than assumed.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,140/1,140 green — 935 unit, 45 contract, 70 simulation, 90 journey, up from 1,139 — with npm run lint clean at 0 warnings and 0 errors. Typecheck is 766 against a baseline of 764; the two added are TS7006 and TS2339 in the new test code, the same ambient classes the repository already emits because the dependency allowlist excludes @types/node, and the only non-ambient diagnostics remain the three that predate this work. Then against the live state root, which is the verification that mattered: awsf db rebuild now reports 8 session(s), 6324 record(s), the projection moved from user_version 2 to 3 with owner_reentries present, zero sessions are flagged degraded, and awsf doctor reports healthy: no orphan PID, stale lock, degraded session, or projection finding. The prior database was retained beside the new one as the rebuild protocol requires, and the candidate the earlier refusal left behind is retained too; awsf gc lists both and deletes neither.

Boundary. No status marker moved. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 marker stays unchecked, and M9 remains unopened. One acceptance row is added, for the legacy-journal replay this session's test proves and nothing else. The retained T30.1 attempt was not touched — not cancelled, retried, attested, landed, edited or deleted, no continuity file opened — and it still holds its two spent calls of five and its unspent owner re-entry. Rebuilding the projection rewrites no journal and no attempt: it derives a disposable database from records it only reads, which is the guarantee that made running it safe. No live task state is encoded here. Commit 4 remains deferred and unopened. Nothing was pushed and nothing was deleted. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-15 — A replacement review reserved a retry it would not spend, and forfeited a candidate over one redundant key

Found by a pilot call, which is the expensive way and the only way left. A replacement review ran against a retained T2 candidate on the opposite provider. It read the composed diff and answered concern with five specific, evidenced findings. The envelope was then discarded, because each finding carried a correct severity and a redundant level holding the identical value, and ReviewFindingSchema is additionalProperties: false. Five Unexpected property violations were the only violations — nothing missing, nothing conflicting. The attempt took L17 as review-malformed, the candidate was forfeited, the owner's single re-entry was spent, and the call the headroom rule had reserved for a retry was still unspent. This commit is a defect fix and spent zero AWSF provider calls.

A first diagnosis was wrong and is recorded rather than quietly replaced. The session's initial reading was that awsf review had failed to wire up correction rounds the production runner has, since build-review gives its reviewer phase maxCorrections: 1. Checking the runner refuted it: authorizeCorrection opens with if (!route.continuity) return null, and the reviewer is configured continuity: "none" deliberately, because a resumable reviewer is an argued-with one. That maxCorrections: 1 is therefore dead for the configured reviewer route, and awsf run would have failed on the same envelope too — on L8 rather than L17. The trap was in both commands, and a fix built on the first diagnosis would have wired a correction transport to a route that structurally cannot have one.

The real defect is a retry reserved for one failure class and reachable by another. awsf review refuses below two calls of headroom in exactly those words — one for the review, one for its single permitted retry — but runMandatoryReview reaches that retry only through isTransportFailure. A cold reviewer holds no state, so the one recovery its route actually supports is being asked again; the command reserved that remedy and then declined to apply it to the only failure class the remedy fits. An optional isContractFailure predicate now admits a schema-invalid envelope to the same two-attempt loop. It is optional and absent by default because it is sound only where the route is cold: the production runner does not pass it, and a phase's own correction allowance keeps ownership of that decision there. Exhausting it rethrows the malformed failure as itself rather than as MandatoryReviewUnavailable — the review was available and answered twice, and calling that unavailable would misname the failure the owner reads. ReplacementReviewInconsistent, ReplacementReviewEvidenceInvalid, ReviewCandidateMoved and PermissionBreach are deliberately not retryable: those are content, evidence and policy, and a second roll of the dice on any of them is a verdict appeal.

A latent identity collision had to be fixed with it, or the retry would have destroyed the evidence for the retry. The turn's envelope was written at a hardcoded correctionRound: 0, its gate rows at a hardcoded round 0, and its projection identities derived from both. No transport retry ever reached that code — a transport fault dies before parsing — so the collision was unreachable and unseen. A contract retry has written an envelope, so the second turn would have hit immutable envelope already exists on disk, and in the projection the gate rows are INSERT OR REPLACE and the envelope INSERT OR IGNORE: the retry would have overwritten the record of why it happened, and then had its own record dropped. Both are now round-scoped, which is the same generation-qualification the -re<N> identities already apply one level up. The phase's own round/maximumRounds display is deliberately left at 0/0: no correction tranche was charged, and a retry paid for by the held call is not a correction-allowance round.

The correction the retry is handed is about form, and the test enforces that it stays that way. The second turn's prompt appends the failing schema paths and an instruction to return the same review unchanged in substance — same verdict, same findings, same limitations. It carries neither the owner's --reason nor the superseded verdict, for the reason the first prompt does not: a reviewer told what to find is not a reviewer. A unit test asserts the appended block matches none of accept, concern, owner, superseded or reject, and that assertion caught the first draft, which opened "your previous response was rejected" — accurate about the envelope, and one word away from reading as a verdict. Appending is safe against review_evidence_present because that gate is a containment check on the serialized context rather than a digest of the whole prompt; the original prompt remains a prefix, and the test asserts that too. The privacy preflight was made a function of the prompt so each turn validates its own descriptor — reusing the first turn's grant for a different prompt would have tripped the launch wrapper's own equality check, which is the guarantee that a sandbox grant cannot change between validation and GO.

Verification, at zero provider cost. Full suite 1,148/1,148 green — 942 unit, 45 contract, 70 simulation, 91 journey, up from 1,140 — with npm run lint clean at 0 warnings and 0 errors. New coverage: a contract failure spending the held retry once and no more; a retry that succeeds returning the retry's answer with both turns retained under distinct rounds and the rejected one still naming the redundant key; the evidence gate still passing on the appended prompt; a transport fault still exhausting into MandatoryReviewUnavailable and never consulting the contract predicate; and the production runner's behaviour proved unchanged when the predicate is absent. The existing malformed-replacement journey test was updated rather than deleted: it now asserts two launches and four spent calls, because the old assertion of three encoded the defect. Typecheck is 768 against a baseline of 766, the two added both TS2307 for node: builtins in the one new test file, the same ambient class the allowlist's exclusion of @types/node produces everywhere; zero new error classes, and the non-ambient diagnostics remain the three that predate this work. One narrowing regression surfaced and was fixed rather than suppressed: the recovery block's processRecord guard relied on flow analysis that cannot see an assignment made inside the broker's register callback.

Boundary. No status marker moved. T30 stays todo, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 marker stays unchecked, and M9 remains unopened. One acceptance row is added, for the retry behaviour this session's tests prove and nothing else — no pilot completion is claimed, no candidate landed, and no pilot record was written. The blocked attempt was left exactly as the failure left it: not cancelled, not retried, not attested, not edited, no continuity file opened, and its superseded review artefacts still byte-identical. The five findings that review produced are retained as unredacted host capture and are quoted nowhere. No live task state is encoded here. Commit 4 remains deferred and unopened. Nothing was pushed and nothing was deleted. built on: claude:opus-5.

2026-08-15 — Pilot 2's subject leaves the pipeline: the Herdr work is applied by hand, and what that costs T30, M8 and T31

The decision, and the arithmetic behind it. The blocked T2 attempt holds a good audit of a candidate it can no longer land. Its remaining route was cancel plus awsf retry, which carries the three spent calls forward and leaves two — exactly build-review's cost of one builder and one review, with zero margin for a transport retry on either. Spending that on rebuilding a candidate whose defects are already named, when the named defects can simply be applied, is the worse trade. So the Herdr-visible work is applied to the target repository by hand, outside AWSF, on a subscription route, and the retained attempt is left untouched as evidence. Zero AWSF provider calls; this commit amends the plan and moves no marker.

Two corrections to the replacement review, made by reading the code it said it could not see. That review was diff-scoped and honest about its limits, and two of its five findings resolve differently against the unchanged sources. Its process-tree finding rested, in its own words, on whether the close-wait short-circuits for an already-closed child — and it does, on its first line, so the "leader exits while a descendant survives" branch is correct and the finding reduces to a genuine coverage gap: the only cancellation fixture makes both levels ignore SIGTERM, so the group-inspection disjunct never decides the outcome and the branch the change exists for is never exercised. Its survivor-reporting finding, by contrast, is confirmed on both halves: the structural cancellation outcome is flattened into an error message and re-detected downstream by regex, and that detection is additionally gated on the abort signal, so the identical tree-cancellation failure arriving from the timeout path reports as an ordinary failure with no survivor warning at all. A reviewer that names its own blind spot precisely is what makes this kind of correction possible, and it is the argument for the diff-scoped reviewer rather than against it.

A requirement the candidate does not meet, found while preparing the journey rather than by any gate. The whole Herdr-visible mode is selected by a runtime object installed at a well-known symbol, and the design says so deliberately — no environment heuristic selects this mode. Nothing in the candidate installs it: the symbol is referenced only inside its own defining module, and the new tests supply a fake runtime as an explicit argument. So the production path always resolves to the explicitly-labelled embedded mode, and requirement 1 — a tab per provider-spawning command when running inside the terminal multiplexer — is not met by this candidate alone. The builder disclosed exactly this in its own envelope. The consequence is that the eight-step end-user journey authored before the build is unattestable as written, because seven of its eight steps have nothing to observe; only the embedded-mode step is exercisable. That gap is the first item of the hand-off, ahead of the review's findings.

What this costs the plan, stated rather than absorbed. T30's proof obligation is that AWSF lands a real T2 task within its ceiling through a human at a terminal. Landing this change by hand proves the feature and proves nothing whatever about the factory, so it cannot satisfy T30 and is not offered as satisfying it. T30's first half is already earned and remains so — the live tier-2 binding, inversion-routed review, verdict_consistent, and evidence-bearing landing guards are all checked. What remains unchecked is the one live landing. Because the hand-off consumes this particular task, pilot 2 needs a different real T2 subject, and a new task id carries a fresh task-scoped ceiling rather than inheriting this one's spend — spend is scoped to the task, which is what makes that honest rather than a loophole. T31 is not reachable yet: its stated entry is M8 is [x], M8 is [wip], and M8's remaining boxes are pilot 2 landing, a green suite after both pilots, and a current portability matrix. Amending T30 to accept a hand-landing was considered and rejected: it would delete the only obligation the milestone exists to discharge, and a milestone that can be satisfied by not using the tool is not a milestone.

Boundary. No status marker moved and none may be inferred from this entry. T30 stays todo, its ticket state stays todo with it, M8 stays [wip], every T30 and Pilot-2 marker stays unchecked, M9 stays unopened and T31 stays gated. No acceptance row is added — this session completed no acceptance-bearing work in the plan's sense. No pilot record was written and none is due. The retained attempt was not cancelled, retried, attested, landed, edited or deleted, and no continuity file was opened. The review's findings are retained as unredacted host capture and are quoted nowhere here; everything asserted above was re-derived from the candidate's own committed diff. No live task state is encoded in this file. Nothing was pushed and nothing was deleted. built on: claude:opus-5.
